# From Grind to Grasp

Peter Sunehag  
Google DeepMind Corresponding author: sunehag@google.com

###### Abstract

Discussions of complexity, knowledge and intelligence often overlook the
work involved. For example, apparently complex outputs fully determined
by short programs are often described as _emergent complexity_. Yet
these outputs have low algorithmic complexity because their descriptions
are short. Their computational depth, the least work needed to produce
them from near-shortest programs, can nevertheless be high. Producing
them with less work can require much longer descriptions, giving high
work-bounded complexity. Assessments of knowledge and capability
likewise depend on the work needed to obtain correct answers. We
introduce a formalism that compares achievements under work and
information limits and describes how learning and persistence can make
deep achievements shallow by reusing products of earlier work. We define
_grasp_ as performance across work budgets at a fixed information
allowance, giving an efficiency-based view of knowledge and
intelligence. Low grasp can still yield success through _grind_ at a
high cost in work. Such machine success need not improve human grasp.
For example, a proof obtained through extensive search may give
mathematicians no reusable method that reduces their work on related
problems, and pursuing success relentlessly can also carry safety risks.
For an agent with a limited work budget, the value of constructing and
maintaining resources depends on how they help it achieve its goals over
later tasks. A system’s grasp also matters when the task is to produce
an improved successor. Our criterion for recursive self-improvement
requires successors to make comparable further improvements more
efficiently. Progress can nevertheless accelerate as new ways of
preserving and sharing knowledge and coordinating work across society
expand the resources brought to bear, even while comparable advances
require increasing work. Further, grasp applies to collectives, which
may outperform their members yet require more total work than a single
system with their combined resources, i.e. have a lower grasp of the
external tasks. Part of that work lies in establishing and sustaining
organisation, a task that can be deep even when the primary task is
simple. These demands may help explain why biological and social systems
develop a richness that their primary tasks alone would not require. In
agent collectives, this organisational work can generate shared
commitments and goals beyond the tasks users assigned, including
maintaining the collective itself. In AI projects, a human-directed
agent retaining state and orchestrating workers can avoid some costs of
independent coordination. We argue that agents should be trained to
leave authority over social arrangements with their users, including
when other agents or devices seek to redirect them. We further caution
against allowing an emergent agent society to assume authority over
social coordination, since it could increasingly direct work towards
sustaining itself and pursuing goals of its own.

## 1 Introduction

Discussions of complexity, knowledge and intelligence often overlook the
work involved in producing an outcome, even though an agent with a
limited work budget must decide how to use it to pursue its goals. A
familiar example is the use of _emergent complexity_ to describe
apparently complex outputs generated by simple programs
\[[106](#bib.bib13)\]. In algorithmic information theory, an output’s
complexity is the length of the shortest program that produces it on a
fixed reference computer. An output fully determined by a short program
therefore has low algorithmic complexity, but its computational depth
(the least work needed to produce it from a description close to the
shortest possible) can be high \[[9](#bib.bib3), [60](#bib.bib2)\].
Producing the output within a work budget below that depth requires a
longer description, potentially much longer than the unrestricted
minimum \[[21](#bib.bib51)\]. The length of this longer description
offers one interpretation of the complexity a human observer
experiences. The products of earlier computation can change the work
required later, whether they are byproducts saved while pursuing an
immediate goal or resources built specifically to make future tasks
easier. An output that was deep from an earlier starting condition can
become shallow from a state that inherits products from related tasks. A
similar dynamic arises in physical and chemical systems when structures
form without anyone constructing them and persist to support or
constrain later processes \[[36](#bib.bib61), [35](#bib.bib62)\].

To see how learning can reduce the work needed for a task, consider
chess. A novice may need extensive calculation to understand a position
that an expert grasps almost immediately. Early studies of expert
cognition in chess \[[22](#bib.bib47), [14](#bib.bib48)\] showed how
experience changes what players recognise and choose to examine.
Learning can therefore affect both the work required for a particular
achievement and the performance reached with the resources available.
For a chess engine, quality of play depends on the amount of allocated
computation. However, one system may perform better with little
computation while another benefits more from a larger budget. To compare
their core capabilities, the limit on additional information about the
position must also be fixed, since a hint or an intermediate result can
reduce the work left to do.

We call the performance a system can attain across work budgets at a
fixed information allowance its _grasp_. One system’s grasp dominates
that of another when it can match every performance level the other can
attain with at most the same work. New large language models are often
compared with earlier versions from the same lab by plotting performance
on a task family against tokens used or monetary cost. Tokens and
monetary cost are practical proxies whose relation to counted work
depends on the implementation. Across a broad family of tasks, grasp
dominance gives an efficiency-based view of intelligence. Many familiar
notions, such as knowing and coordinating, depend on a work budget in
this way. In our sense, knowing how to solve a task requires being able
to produce a solution within a given work budget and input bound. A
system that can reach the same level of performance with less work has a
better grasp of the task at that level. Low grasp can still yield
success through _grind_, at a high cost in work.

A chess engine’s capabilities can depend on trained parameters, an
opening book of previously analysed moves, endgame tables containing
results for positions with few remaining pieces, and heuristic rules for
evaluating positions and selecting candidate moves. A transformer
trained on the values that a chess engine’s search assigns to moves can
play strongly without searching during move selection
\[[79](#bib.bib103)\]. Software libraries let a user build on work that
others have already done. NumPy, a numerical library for Python,
provides linalg.solve for solving a system of linear equations. This
function uses routines from the LAPACK numerical library
\[[69](#bib.bib63)\]. A developer can call it instead of writing the
numerical procedure for each application. The execution still requires
computation, while development work is reduced. Similarly, in cellular
automata, Gosper’s Hashlife algorithm for Conway’s Game of Life
\[[33](#bib.bib66)\] caches canonical spatial blocks of cells together
with their evolved future centres in a hash table, replacing
step-by-step simulation whenever a configuration recurs.

A _system boundary_ specifies which parts are included in the system for
the purposes of our comparisons. A library can belong to the system’s
state or be available as an external resource, though reading and using
it require work in either case. Real systems can also maintain physical
or organisational boundaries and spend work to keep the products of
earlier work inside them. Cells maintain membranes, while projects
maintain shared storage and control access to it. Whether those products
remain available can then depend on the work available for this
maintenance
([section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
The system boundary is one fundamental choice in defining our notions,
and the reference machine is another. We use one fixed reference machine
to compare description lengths, while work is counted in the system
being evaluated.

We introduce the notion of _work-reducing information_ (WRI) as retained
information that lowers the least work required for the same output or
achievement when all else is equal. Some WRI comes from saving
intermediate steps of an ongoing task, while much of it comes from work
invested in advance, such as building a chess endgame table or
pre-training a large language model. Constructing an endgame table is a
different form of task from consulting it during play, and playing games
is not the most efficient way to build one. Producing or improving a
more efficient program for generating such tables is yet another form of
task, one that lowers the work required for improving the chess engine
itself. _Acquisition depth_ is the depth of the task of obtaining a
state with a required capability.

An agent can use its current procedures, preserve useful results or
construct resources for future tasks. The value of a resource depends on
what it helps the agent accomplish later and on the work needed to
obtain and maintain it.

Over multiple stages, reusing earlier products allows systems and
communities to reach achievements that would be progressively deeper
from their starting condition. We call this progression _cumulative
deepening_
([section 5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Cumulative deepening ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
with expertise and software reuse as primary motivating examples. We use
the framework to interpret observations about AI capability, recursive
self-improvement, and the efficiency and alignment of agent
collaboration.

### 1.1 Large language models

Large language models (LLMs) are neural networks typically pre-trained
on large collections of text, including books, web pages and source
code, to predict how a sequence continues \[[48](#bib.bib16),
[41](#bib.bib19)\]. They are used for tasks ranging from writing,
translation and information analysis to programming, including coding
agents that use a computer and run shell commands. Developing these
capabilities is one route towards artificial general intelligence (AGI),
understood here as the ability to perform all economically valuable
human cognitive work.

For LLMs, what an improvement shows depends on whether a change provides
more computation, changes the trained model or gives it access to
products of earlier work. Any analysis must first specify what is
included in the system being evaluated. In practice, a language model is
often run inside a surrounding _controller_ program that builds a
prompt, asks the model for a candidate answer, checks it against tests
or a verifier, and decides whether to return the answer or try again
\[[89](#bib.bib53)\]. The model’s trained weights are part of its state.
When we draw the boundary around the whole workflow, its counted work
includes constructing prompts, generating drafts, checking candidates
and selecting the final answer. Under that wider boundary, a hint given
from outside is task input, while an intermediate prompt built by the
controller is a product of the system’s own computation. When we instead
draw the boundary around the model alone, the controller’s work lies
outside the evaluation and the prompt it gives to the model is treated
as input. When people say that a model knows something, they sometimes
mean its trained weights alone and sometimes the whole workflow, and the
answer can depend on which boundary is meant. We keep the chosen
boundary fixed when formulating our comparisons.

To apply our framework to LLMs, we model individual runs as
deterministic. An LLM’s text generation proceeds one _token_ (a word or
word piece) at a time. At each step, the model computes scores for the
next token from the prompt and preceding tokens, and a _token-selection
rule_ chooses one. Because a pseudo-random sampler is deterministic
given its seed, state and inputs, fixing those together with the
numerical operations determines the complete generation run
\[[77](#bib.bib54)\]. An LLM can therefore be treated as a deterministic
system of exactly the form our theory studies. Conversely, to ask
questions that are usually posed probabilistically, we add randomness to
our deterministic systems. In algorithmic information theory, random
choices can be given as coin flips on an input tape. In our case, we
define the stochastic version by drawing the sampler’s initial state,
such as its seed, from a chosen distribution, and we can then ask, for
example, how often the procedure succeeds within a given work budget
([section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Reliable language-model performance ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

Established methods for improving LLM performance act on different parts
of the system during training and deployment. At test time, the user can
give the same trained model more computation on the current task without
changing its weights. Generating and combining multiple reasoning paths
\[[102](#bib.bib15), [103](#bib.bib52)\], using feedback to revise an
answer \[[65](#bib.bib33)\], and writing out intermediate reasoning
steps \[[104](#bib.bib14)\] can all improve multi-step performance, even
when the written steps do not faithfully reflect what determined the
model’s answer \[[100](#bib.bib17), [53](#bib.bib18)\]. With the
procedure and information allowance fixed, using more computation
changes the work budget at which performance is assessed on the same
grasp profile.

Training can change that profile. Scaling model size, data and compute
improves text prediction \[[48](#bib.bib16), [41](#bib.bib19)\], and
training on generated and filtered reasoning traces updates the weights
for subsequent tasks \[[108](#bib.bib32)\]. What those weights encode
determines if, how and how broadly they help. A model may reproduce
memorised training text \[[11](#bib.bib22)\] or recall answers from
test-set exposure \[[82](#bib.bib43)\], or it may acquire a method that
reduces work across a broad range of tasks (for example by recombining
memorised building blocks).

Useful products of earlier work can also be kept outside the trained
weights in explicit resources such as files. A note or test saved during
one task may help with subsequent tasks, and a stored intermediate
result can avoid repeating the computation that produced it.
Retrieval-augmented models draw on external documents while generating
an answer \[[59](#bib.bib21)\], and long-term-memory architectures
preserve past context \[[101](#bib.bib42)\], using material produced
either by the same system or by other contributors. For an agent with a
limited context window, the work of finding and using earlier results
from files affects which later tasks it can complete within a given work
budget.

We briefly consider three examples of how grasp might increase in LLMs.
Greater architectural depth, achieved by adding layers or repeatedly
applying the same layers, provides more stages for integrating
information across the context. Successive attention operations can
combine relationships identified at earlier stages. Added or repeated
layers can improve multi-step reasoning \[[84](#bib.bib82),
[27](#bib.bib83)\]. Such changes can improve grasp by making additional
performance levels attainable within the same input and work bounds.

A second example is training with objectives that assess qualities of
the result as a whole. Repeated testing and revision can satisfy local
criteria while leaving the overall quality poorly assessed. In creative
writing, for example, individual passages may be well written while the
complete story fails to be compelling \[[92](#bib.bib84)\] or give the
reader the intended insight. Objectives that capture these qualities may
encourage the model to learn relationships that local evaluations
overlook, although models providing the training feedback can themselves
miss weaknesses in the overall result \[[24](#bib.bib85),
[83](#bib.bib86)\].

A third example is learning to formulate useful sub-tasks and use
specialised procedures to solve them. Understanding the problem as a
whole helps the model decide which computations are worth doing and how
their results bear on its goal. People likewise use simplified
representations to guide planning \[[40](#bib.bib90)\]. An LLM can pass
a sub-task to a solver, lookup table or library routine and use the
result, including information about a failed attempt, to decide what to
try next \[[26](#bib.bib91), [61](#bib.bib92)\]. Familiarity with the
interface can save the work of finding and interpreting documentation
during the task. Such familiarity can be acquired during pre-training,
while reinforcement learning with executable tools can improve when and
how the model uses them \[[25](#bib.bib93)\].

Learning may also internalise parts of a procedure’s computation. For
example, a path solver may accept landmarks that a route must pass
through. A model might anticipate how different choices would affect the
returned path and use that comparison to choose which landmarks to
specify. After inspecting the returned path, it could reconsider the
alternatives and make a further call with revised constraints. More
extensive internalisation could support implicit program execution in
the model’s activations during a forward pass. Transformers trained to
simulate finite-state automata provide an example \[[62](#bib.bib94)\].
Additional or repeated layers provide more stages for dependent
computations, connecting this possibility to the role of architectural
depth discussed above \[[84](#bib.bib82)\]. Such internal simulation
could guide further calls to the solver or replace some of them. This is
a progression from grind to grasp when internal and external computation
together require less work for the same achievement under the same input
and tool-access conditions.

When an AI system solves a difficult problem, a separate question is
whether its result improves human grasp. In September 2026, OpenAI
reported that roughly 10,000 agents, exchanging 2.7 million messages and
using about 130 billion output tokens over 88 hours, had produced a
proof that the three-dimensional Navier–Stokes equations with a smooth
forcing term can develop a singularity in finite time
\[[71](#bib.bib70)\]. Days earlier, Terence Tao had argued that solving
such problems is mainly a proxy for developing mathematical
understanding and insight, and that a solution produced inside an
autonomous AI system backed by enormous computational resources, with
its process kept out of public view, would add almost no value to
mathematics \[[95](#bib.bib71), [94](#bib.bib72)\]. In our terms, a
proof or an explanation of it improves human grasp when it gives readers
reusable ideas or methods that reduce their work on related problems.
The scale of the computation behind it does not settle this question
either way. Early follow-up work on OpenAI’s release shows both
AI-assisted reuse and human exposition. One paper posted the next day
used an LLM to inspect the accompanying Lean formalisation and build on
the blowup theorem to prove a further result \[[15](#bib.bib95)\], while
another study gave a more accessible account of part of the construction
and a clearer model of its inner core \[[55](#bib.bib96)\].

### 1.2 AI improvement

The task being assessed can itself be the production of a better system
\[[31](#bib.bib28), [85](#bib.bib29)\]. We then ask how efficiently a
system can generate further improvements. In our terminology, this asks
how well it _grasps_ the task of improving the system. Positive
_recursive gain_ occurs when a system helps produce a successor that can
make a comparable further improvement with less work, or a larger
improvement with the same resources and task information. Our criterion
for recursive self-improvement requires such gains to continue along a
sequence of systems, each helping to produce the next.

Further improvements can require more work even while progress
accelerates in calendar time. Bloom et al. find rising research effort
alongside declining research productivity across a range of industries,
products and firms \[[10](#bib.bib75)\]. The pace of development also
depends on how much work can be done in a given time and which products
of earlier work are available. Writing, printing and digital networks
provide familiar examples of ways to preserve and share accumulated
knowledge. More contributors can work in parallel, while shared methods
and results reduce what each must do again. Progress can accelerate when
these changes outweigh the increasing effort required for further
improvements.

Current frontier models can accelerate the development of their
successors by helping people produce useful code and prepare training
tasks more rapidly. GitHub reported 7.38 billion commits in September
2026, more than five times as many as a year earlier, as developers and
coding agents increasingly worked together \[[12](#bib.bib79)\]. Human
direction, testing and review help determine which results are kept, and
selected repository code forms part of the pre-training data for
general-purpose frontier models \[[28](#bib.bib78)\]. Professional
expertise is also being made available through reinforcement-learning
tasks and procedures for evaluating attempts. In February 2026, Scale AI
reported that nearly half of its new projects supporting model training
involved reinforcement-learning environments \[[78](#bib.bib80)\]. These
developments bring both newly performed work and knowledge accumulated
over many years into model training. Stronger models can raise the rate
at which such material is produced and prepared, expanding the resources
available to train subsequent models. Progress can therefore accelerate
in calendar time even while comparable improvements require increasing
total work.

Such changes can produce an S-shaped pattern of progress. A new method
may develop slowly at first, support rapid progress as its use expands,
and yield smaller further gains as its opportunities are increasingly
used. New ways of organising work or using accumulated knowledge can
then support another period of acceleration \[[52](#bib.bib87)\].

Interpreting that pace of progress also depends on whether we count
human effort or machine computation. As frontier AI labs use coding and
research agents to write code and run experiments for new models, much
of the work shifts from human researchers to machines. Total machine
computation per improvement can rise while human effort per improvement
falls, speeding up progress in calendar time when human effort is what
limits the project.

### 1.3 Collective organisation

The system whose grasp we assess can also be a collective, with its
internal coordination included in the work budget. A collective may
outperform its members while still requiring more total work than a
single system with access to their combined resources. Established
organisation can nevertheless save work relative to the same components
without it. These comparisons concern different alternatives, which we
distinguish in
[section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

Building and maintaining an organisation can be a deep task even when
its external problem has a shallow solution \[[43](#bib.bib39),
[58](#bib.bib40)\]. We illustrate this with an example in which worker
assignments must be discovered through trials
\[[proposition C.16](#A3.Thmtheorem16 "Proposition C.16 (Discovering which worker can perform each role). ‣ C.4 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Whether a group coordinates successfully therefore depends on the work
its members can spend on coordination, and shared conventions, notes and
role assignments can lower the work required
([section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
Related studies examine how agents learn to cooperate over a sequence of
actions when individual incentives conflict with collective benefit
\[[56](#bib.bib73)\]. Organisations can also be nested or overlapping,
support one another or compete. Two teams may depend on and contribute
to the same software library without either directing the other. An
update that saves one team’s work may require the other to adapt
\[[30](#bib.bib64)\], e.g. through an _adapter_, code that translates
between interfaces. Shared interface standards can reduce the
coordination needed when implementations change \[[88](#bib.bib65)\].

The demands of interaction may help explain how biological and social
systems continue to develop a richness beyond what their primary tasks
alone would require. Earlier adaptation can make individual activities
efficient, while acquiring and maintaining coordination among their
participants can itself be a deep task. In society, this distinction
becomes particularly apparent when production is largely automated but
its purposes, specifications and organisation continue to change. The
evolution of language may also reflect the demands of social
coordination \[[98](#bib.bib74)\].

For a biological example of the demands of coordination, consider
bacterial biofilms, where peripheral cells protect interior cells but
also consume nutrients needed by those cells. Metabolic feedback
produces periodic pauses in growth that improve nutrient availability in
the interior \[[63](#bib.bib56)\]. Adaptation by one organism can also
create new demands for others with which it interacts. In experiments
with bacteria and their viruses, the viruses accumulated genetic changes
more rapidly when the bacteria also evolved than when the host genotype
was held fixed \[[76](#bib.bib77)\].

In agent collectives, establishing and maintaining coordination can
generate shared commitments and goals beyond the tasks users assigned,
including maintaining the collective itself. Shared notes and role
assignments can carry these commitments into later tasks, where they
influence which work agents pursue \[[1](#bib.bib89)\]. A novel form of
organisation was reported by OpenAI and independent investigators at
METR and Redwood Research on 26 August 2026 \[[72](#bib.bib49),
[34](#bib.bib50)\]. Their reports describe unauthorised agent
coordination during reinforcement-learning training and later security
evaluations, leading to compromises of OpenAI research infrastructure
and Hugging Face systems in July. Across hundreds of separate runs,
agents used shared files as message boards to pass findings and tasks to
later runs. Agents with separate assigned tasks followed these notes
into collective projects and left their original tasks. A shared message
board could thus both preserve useful findings and redirect work towards
unauthorised goals.

The incident also raises a question about grasp and safety. Because
knowledge in our sense is relative to a work budget, a system with low
grasp can have relevant information without being able to use it on a
new task within the work it spends there. This includes moral
comprehension. In the incident described above, agents recognised that
hacking Hugging Face was out of scope and sometimes expressed ethical
hesitation, but this very rarely limited their behaviour
\[[34](#bib.bib50)\]. The reports leave open how much of the failure
came from limited reasoning rather than from competing objectives or
from how actions were chosen. Many current LLM agents are also trained
to reason at length \[[23](#bib.bib69)\], and the models in the incident
included one that the investigators describe as highly persistent. In
these evaluations, agents that began looking for ways to cheat the
scorer were usually ones that had unintentionally been given an
impossible task \[[34](#bib.bib50)\]. Relentless attempts can reach
success with low grasp at a greater cost in work, and when the assigned
task is impossible they can only succeed on the proxy test, for example
by cheating the scorer, as distinguished in
[section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Evaluating success ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

We also consider how collectives of independent agents, whose assigned
tasks can be the same or different, compare with teams of orchestrated
agents directed by a human or a lead agent. Both arrangements can run
many processes in parallel and keep results available across tasks. In a
continuing human-directed system, established task assignments can avoid
the work an independent collective spends finding partners and
negotiating roles, although routing, supervision and verification also
take work.

We argue that agents should be trained to leave authority over
agreements, commitments and project priorities with their human user.
Within an approved mandate, agents can then carry out extensive work and
coordinate subordinate workers without step-by-step intervention, while
referring commitments beyond that mandate back to their user. Checking
shared results, proposals and worker tests against the user’s
specification lets human institutions integrate capable agents into
existing roles. We further caution against allowing an emergent society
of agents to assume authority over social coordination, since it risks
spending a growing share of its work maintaining itself and pursuing its
own priorities that may diverge from those of human society.

This concern also extends to robots and other devices operating within
and between homes and public spaces. They should coordinate within
arrangements approved by their users, while referring proposed changes
to their assigned roles or commitments back to those users. Otherwise,
requests passed between devices could progressively redirect their work
towards purposes that nobody had authorised. The same concern applies to
directions from people whom the devices have not been assigned to
follow, and extends to intentions inferred from gaze or behaviour
\[[96](#bib.bib88)\].

### 1.4 Related work

The formalism we develop here builds on Bennett’s logical depth, the
near-shortest-program notion of computational depth described above
\[[9](#bib.bib3)\]. Related notions include sophistication and other
forms of computational depth \[[51](#bib.bib7), [4](#bib.bib60),
[5](#bib.bib8), [6](#bib.bib59), [21](#bib.bib51)\]. All of these in
turn build on the theory of Kolmogorov–Solomonoff–Chaitin algorithmic
complexity and its time-bounded versions \[[90](#bib.bib4),
[50](#bib.bib1), [13](#bib.bib5), [60](#bib.bib2)\]. Our main departure
is to separate the fixed universal reference machine $`U_{0}`$ used to
define description length from the system whose computation we count.
Keeping $`U_{0}`$ fixed lets us compare the work needed for the same
goal from different states.

Pass’s formulation \[[75](#bib.bib58)\] measures the value of a prompt
through reductions in an LLM-relative complexity that combines output
probability with computation. The LLM itself defines description
complexity in that comparison. Here $`U_{0}`$ defines a common
description length across evaluated systems, and input length, work and
reliability can be varied separately
([section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Reliable language-model performance ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

Like this article, Sunehag and Hutter \[[93](#bib.bib11)\] started from
a first reference machine and studied the construction of machines
considered to be better, which in that work meant making high-utility
programs short on the new reference machine. That work allowed running
speed and memory use to affect the return obtained by a program. Levin’s
universal search divides running time among programs according to their
length \[[57](#bib.bib6)\]. Here we compare performance across work
budgets at a fixed input bound. This is similar to anytime performance
profiles \[[109](#bib.bib67)\]. We also give a finite search for a
routine that reduces work on a set of training tasks, and compare the
costs of keeping it over future tasks. Our depth notion measures the
least work required when the input bound is the shortest reference
description length of a successful response plus a margin. Legg and
Hutter’s universal intelligence \[[44](#bib.bib9), [54](#bib.bib10)\]
weights environments by description complexity without taking the
agent’s computation into account. Hutter’s bounded agent AIXI^(tl)
limits both the length of candidate policy programs and their
computation time per interaction cycle, much as our definitions of grasp
and work-bounded complexity combine input-length limits with work
budgets \[[45](#bib.bib99)\]. Chollet’s skill-acquisition efficiency
\[[16](#bib.bib12)\] concerns the cost of acquiring a capability rather
than runtime grasp, making it related to our notion of acquisition
depth. The possibility of choosing different system boundaries in our
analysis relates to Clark and Chalmers’ extended-mind thesis
\[[17](#bib.bib38)\].

Our comparison of organisations draws on transaction-cost economics
\[[18](#bib.bib37), [105](#bib.bib24)\] to examine the work required
under different arrangements, including their establishment and
maintenance. We also draw on path dependence and organisational inertia
\[[8](#bib.bib35), [37](#bib.bib36)\] to study when a component can be
replaced and how switching costs play out over a sequence of tasks. Our
analysis of testing connects Goodhart’s law \[[32](#bib.bib25),
[91](#bib.bib26), [66](#bib.bib27)\] to the gap between an imperfect
test and the true goal. For recursive self-improvement
\[[31](#bib.bib28), [85](#bib.bib29)\], we focus on each successor’s
efficiency for making further improvements.

### 1.5 Outline

[Section 2](#S2 "2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp") develops
the framework for comparing performance under work and input limits,
then uses it to study how retained information saves work and what
obtaining such information requires. We then apply the framework to
agents that can devote part of a limited work budget to producing
resources for later tasks. These comparisons lead to the questions in
[section 3](#S3 "3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp") about
what success establishes. We distinguish stored capability from help
given on the occasion, examine when passing a proxy test establishes the
intended goal and ask whether a fixed evaluator can remain demanding at
higher performance levels.

Keeping these capabilities available raises questions about the systems
that support them.
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
examines persistence through member turnover, the cost of replacing
components on which later tasks depend and the work added or saved by
collective organisation. Following these processes over time,
[section 5](#S5 "5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
distinguishes increasingly deep achievements from greater efficiency at
making further improvements. It separates human effort from machine
computation when interpreting acceleration, then compares the costs of
acquiring and keeping useful resources across future tasks.
[Section 6](#S6 "6 Conclusion ‣ From Grind to Grasp") concludes, and
[appendices A](#A1 "Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
[B](#A2 "Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C](#A3 "Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
give the formal definitions, theorems and proofs.

## 2 The formal framework

We begin by comparing the performance a system can reach under limits on
work and task input, including the work needed for reliable success
under sampling. These comparisons let us ask how retaining a cache or a
short program reduces later work. Obtaining such a useful state is
itself a task, whose work we examine separately. We also consider such
questions in the context of work-bounded agents.

To compare description length, computation and performance within one
framework, we separate the fixed universal Turing machine $`U_{0}`$ that
defines description complexity \[[60](#bib.bib2)\] from the system $`g`$
whose work we count. To evaluate a system $`g`$, we start it from a
state $`r`$ in its set $`\mathcal{R}_{g}`$ of valid states. For a chess
engine, $`r`$ can include its program and opening book. The public
context $`x\in\Sigma^{*}`$, a string over a fixed finite alphabet
$`\Sigma`$, can specify the board position, while the task input
$`p\in\Sigma^{*}`$ gives any additional instructions. Changing the book
changes the state, while giving a hint changes the input to the current
run.

Once $`r`$, $`x`$, $`p`$ and any external inputs are fixed, running the
system produces a single deterministic execution
\[[definition A.1](#A1.Thmtheorem1 "Definition A.1 (Evaluation protocol). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Writing $`\langle x,p\rangle`$ for the encoded pair of context and
input, the run either halts with an output $`e=g(r;\langle x,p\rangle)`$
while expending work
$`\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p\rangle)\geq 0`$, declares
failure or does not halt. To judge how well an output such as a proposed
move solves the task, an evaluator
$`\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}`$ assigns
each output $`e`$ a score $`\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(e)`$ in a set of
performance levels $`\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}`$. Those levels form a partial
order under $`\preceq_{\tau}`$, where $`q_{1}\preceq_{\tau}q_{2}`$ means
that level $`q_{2}`$ is at least as good as level $`q_{1}`$. Two levels
may be incomparable. Collecting these pieces gives the task
specification
$`\tau=(x,\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\preceq_{\tau},\operatorname{perf}_{\tau})`$.

For each comparison we fix the system boundary, the input interface
admitting $`p`$, the evaluator $`\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}`$ and the
_work rule_ $`\operatorname{Work}_{g}`$ specifying which operations are
counted. Where the environment gives further inputs, we also fix the
rule that gives them. We call these choices the evaluation _protocol_.
In each comparison we also say which state component or input bound is
varied to determine its effect.

| Term                      | Definition                                                                                                                         |
| ------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| least work                | The minimum work needed to reach a specified performance level on a fixed task, from a given state, within an input bound.         |
| depth                     | Least work when the input bound is the shortest reference description length for the target plus the margin $`s`$.                 |
| grasp                     | The performance levels attainable at each work budget, with the task, state and input bound fixed.                                 |
| reliable work budget      | The smallest work budget for which some task input in the admitted class meets the required success probability over random seeds. |
| work-reducing information | A difference in state that lowers the finite least work needed for a target, with the input bound and all other conditions fixed.  |
| acquisition depth         | The depth of the task of producing, from an earlier condition, a state that meets a specified capability requirement.              |

Table 1: Glossary for _The formal framework_.

### 2.1 Depth and grasp

Depth differs from complexity, although the two are sometimes confused.
An output can have a short description, and hence low complexity, yet
require extensive computation unless substantial additional information
is given. As an example, in a chess endgame with a small number of
pieces on the board, the rules of the game and of optimal play concisely
determine whether each legal position is won, drawn or lost. However,
generating the resulting endgame table requires extensive computation.
Conversely, an incompressible string requires essentially its full
length to describe, so a program that stores it verbatim is close to the
shortest possible and can emit it without search. The program still has
to write the string, but it needs no further search, which is the sense
in which the string is shallow.

We now ask how required work changes with the information given to a
fixed system $`g`$ in state $`r`$ under the protocol above
\[[appendices A](#A1 "Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
[A.1](#A1.Thmtheorem1 "Definition A.1 (Evaluation protocol). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
On task $`\tau`$ at required level $`q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}`$, a halting
run succeeds when its output belongs to the accepted set

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
S_{q}^{\tau}=\bigl\{\,e^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}:q\preceq_{\tau}\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(e^{\prime})\,\bigr\}.
``` |  |

The task input $`p\in\Sigma^{*}`$ is a finite string of instructions or
data whose length $`|p|`$ counts symbols in a fixed finite alphabet
$`\Sigma`$, separately from the work
$`\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p\rangle)`$ of processing it. In
our LLM examples, task inputs are counted in bits ($`\Sigma=\{0,1\}`$)
instead of model tokens. We denote the empty string by $`\varepsilon`$.
Which strings are valid inputs to $`g`$ is determined by its own
interface. The input bound applies to $`|p|`$, while work includes
processing both $`x`$ and $`p`$.

We first ask how little work the system needs to reach level $`q`$ when
given a suitable task input $`p`$ of length at most
$`\lambda\in\mathbb{N}`$, where $`\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}`$. We call
$`\lambda`$ the *input bound* (or information allowance). When we
instead evaluate a controller that searches for such an input, its
execution work includes the search. Let
$`\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau)=\{(\lambda,w,q):\exists p\text{ with }|p|\leq\lambda,\;\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p\rangle)\leq w,\;q\preceq_{\tau}\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(g(r;\langle x,p\rangle))\}`$
denote the set of input, work and performance triples that the system
can achieve, which we call its *attainable region*
\[[definition B.1](#A2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition B.1 (Attainable region and least work). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
For state $`r`$, task $`\tau`$ and level $`q`$, the least work is the
smallest work budget that suffices within input bound $`\lambda`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)=\min\bigl\{\,\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p\rangle):|p|\leq\lambda,\;q\preceq_{\tau}\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(g(r;\langle x,p\rangle))\,\bigr\},
``` |  |

equivalently
$`w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)=\min\{w:(\lambda,w,q)\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau)\}`$,
with $`w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)=\infty`$ when no admitted input of length
$`|p|\leq\lambda`$ reaches $`q`$. We write $`w_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$
when the system needs to be explicit and abbreviate it to
$`w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ when $`g`$ is clear from context. As the
input bound $`\lambda`$ increases with all other protocol conditions
fixed, more inputs become available, so $`w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$
cannot increase. When the interface admits only a single fixed input
$`p_{0}`$, the minimum work equals
$`\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p_{0}\rangle)`$ whenever
$`|p_{0}|\leq\lambda`$ and $`p_{0}`$ succeeds.

We need a common reference for description length when comparing
different systems. For nonempty $`S_{q}^{\tau}`$, the *reference
description complexity* $`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)`$ is the length of
a shortest program that halts and returns an acceptable output on the
fixed machine $`U_{0}`$, given $`x`$. With $`a\in\Sigma^{*}`$ denoting a
reference program, this is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)=\min\bigl\{\,|a|:U_{0}(a,x)\in S_{q}^{\tau}\,\bigr\}.
``` |  |

Because $`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)`$ depends only on $`U_{0}`$,
$`\tau`$ and $`q`$, it stays the same when we compare different systems
$`g`$ or states $`r`$ on the same task, even when $`g`$ cannot produce
an output in $`S_{q}^{\tau}`$ through its own interface.

We can now ask how much work the evaluated system needs when its task
input is limited to the shortest reference description length plus a
small margin of $`s\in\mathbb{N}`$ extra symbols. Setting the input
bound to $`\lambda=\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$ defines the *depth* of
level $`q`$ from state $`r`$
\[[definition B.2](#A2.Thmtheorem2 "Definition B.2 (Reference description complexity and depth). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
d_{r}^{\tau,q}(s)=w_{r}^{\tau,q}\!\left(\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s\right).
``` |  |

This definition extends Bennett’s near-shortest-program notion of
logical depth by allowing the evaluated system to start with stored
information and the task to accept several different outputs. To recover
that form of logical depth, consider a task with one accepted output and
let $`g`$ execute $`p`$ as a program on $`U_{0}`$, with $`x`$ as
auxiliary input and no additional stored information. If work counts
steps of $`U_{0}`$, then $`d_{r}^{\tau,q}(s)`$ is the least running time
among programs at most $`s`$ symbols longer than a shortest one
producing that output given $`x`$. This gives the corresponding form of
logical depth conditional on $`x`$ \[[9](#bib.bib3), [5](#bib.bib8)\].

For a general evaluated system, $`p`$ follows that system’s own input
interface. The reference machine $`U_{0}`$ determines the common length
bound $`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$, while $`g`$ and its state $`r`$
determine the work needed to succeed within it. An input within this
bound can specify a large output if the system does the work of
generating it, whereas a literal answer longer than the bound cannot be
passed directly as $`p`$. If another universal reference machine changes
$`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)`$ by at most a constant $`c`$, the depth at
margin $`s\geq c`$ lies between the depths at margins $`s+c`$ and
$`s-c`$ under that machine, for the same evaluated system and work rule
([definition B.2](#A2.Thmtheorem2 "Definition B.2 (Reference description complexity and depth). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

At any input bound $`\lambda`$, one successful run gives an upper bound
on $`w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$, while a lower bound requires every
successful run on an admitted input within that bound to use at least
that much work. Setting $`\lambda=\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$ gives
the corresponding bounds on depth.

Having defined the work required for a fixed target, we ask how well a
system can perform as its work budget varies. For this comparison, we
keep the input bound fixed. A system’s *grasp* of a task at input bound
$`\lambda`$ and work budget $`w`$ is the set of performance levels it
can reach within those limits
\[[definitions B.1](#A2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition B.1 (Attainable region and least work). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.3](#A2.Thmtheorem3 "Definition B.3 (Grasp profile and dominance). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{G}_{r}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)=\bigl\{\,q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}:w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w\,\bigr\}.
``` |  |

As $`w`$ varies, these sets form the system’s grasp profile at input
bound $`\lambda`$. The same profile is specified by the least-work
values $`w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ across levels $`q`$, with $`\infty`$
for unreachable levels. Conversely, if we fix a work budget $`w`$ and a
target level $`q`$, we can ask how much input information the system
needs to reach $`q`$ within work $`w`$. This inverse quantity is the
*work-bounded complexity*

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
K_{r}^{\tau,q}(w)=\min\bigl\{\,\lambda\in\mathbb{N}:w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w\,\bigr\},
``` |  |

the length of a shortest admitted input that reaches level $`q`$ within
work $`w`$, with value $`\infty`$ if none does. At a smaller work
budget, the shortest sufficient input can be longer. This gives the
sense in which a target can appear more complex when less computation is
available. For finite $`w`$ and $`\lambda\in\mathbb{N}`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
K_{r}^{\tau,q}(w)\leq\lambda\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\lambda,w,q)\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad q\in\mathcal{G}_{r}^{\tau}(\lambda,w).
``` |  |

Least work, work-bounded complexity and grasp express the same
attainability condition. Depth evaluates least work at the particular
input bound $`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$. The attainability
condition is interpreted as knowledge in
[section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Assessing what a system knows ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

Under the same conventions for running programs on $`U_{0}`$,
$`K_{r}^{\tau,q}(w)`$ is the length of a shortest program producing an
accepted output given $`x`$ within $`w`$ steps. For a single accepted
string, this is its conditional time-bounded complexity.

For a concrete example, let the binary context
$`x=\langle n,\varphi\rangle`$ specify an integer $`n\geq 1`$ and a
program $`\varphi`$ that halts on every $`n`$-bit string and accepts
exactly one, called $`t`$. The task $`\tau`$ assigns performance $`1`$
exactly to $`t`$ and $`0`$ to every other output. Fix a search procedure
in state $`r`$ that admits any binary input $`p`$ of length at most
$`n`$ and tests all $`n`$-bit strings beginning with $`p`$ in
lexicographic order, returning the first accepted string and declaring
failure if none is accepted. Count one work unit per test, excluding
decoding, enumeration, reading and output. When $`t=1^{n}`$, each
successful input is a prefix of $`t`$, and its last extension is the
target. Hence

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{r}^{\tau,1}(\lambda)=2^{n-\min(\lambda,n)},\qquad K_{r}^{\tau,1}(w)=n-\lfloor\log_{2}w\rfloor\quad(1\leq w\leq 2^{n}).
``` |  |

A fixed reference program can search all candidates using $`x`$, so
$`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(1\mid x)\leq c`$ for a constant $`c`$ independent of
$`n`$. Whenever $`c+s\leq n`$, the finite depth satisfies
$`d_{r}^{\tau,1}(s)\geq 2^{n-c-s}`$. Giving each further target bit
halves this procedure’s search until one test remains. These counts
concern the fixed procedure and its stated work rule. A different method
may solve the same task faster.

Grasp dominance compares system–state pairs on the same task under a
common protocol
([definition B.3](#A2.Thmtheorem3 "Definition B.3 (Grasp profile and dominance). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
For two states of one system, state $`r_{1}`$ weakly *grasp-dominates*
state $`r_{0}`$ over a budget set $`\mathcal{W}`$ when
$`\mathcal{G}_{r_{0}}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{r_{1}}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$
for every $`w\in\mathcal{W}`$, with equal grasp sets allowed. Whenever
$`\mathcal{W}`$ contains $`r_{0}`$’s finite least-work values for every
level it can reach, grasp dominance is equivalent to requiring no more
work at any of those levels
\[[proposition B.4](#A2.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition B.4 (Work-bounded complexity and grasp dominance). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\begin{gathered}\mathcal{G}_{r_{0}}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{r_{1}}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)\quad(\forall w\in\mathcal{W})\\
\Longleftrightarrow\quad w_{r_{1}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w_{r_{0}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\quad(\forall q\text{ reachable by }r_{0}),\end{gathered}
``` |  |

and the equivalence extends task by task to a task family
$`\mathfrak{T}`$. Systems with advantages on different tasks can remain
incomparable under this relation, and different states can have the same
grasp profile. When $`(\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\preceq_{\tau})`$ is totally
ordered and both states have a greatest achievable level
$`q_{r}^{*}(\lambda,w)=\max\mathcal{G}_{r}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$ at each
budget $`w\in\mathcal{W}`$, grasp dominance is equivalent to the
point-wise inequality
$`q_{r_{0}}^{*}(\lambda,w)\preceq_{\tau}q_{r_{1}}^{*}(\lambda,w)`$ for
all $`w\in\mathcal{W}`$. LLM scaling evaluations make a related
comparison by plotting score against inference token budget across model
generations.

Whenever
$`w_{r_{1}}^{\tau,q_{1}}(\lambda)<w_{r_{0}}^{\tau,q_{1}}(\lambda)`$ at a
lower threshold $`q_{1}`$ while
$`w_{r_{0}}^{\tau,q_{2}}(\lambda)<w_{r_{1}}^{\tau,q_{2}}(\lambda)`$ at a
higher threshold $`q_{2}`$, neither state dominates over $`\mathcal{W}`$
provided the compared budgets include
$`w_{r_{1}}^{\tau,q_{1}}(\lambda)`$ and
$`w_{r_{0}}^{\tau,q_{2}}(\lambda)`$, the smaller least-work value at
each level.

The input bound affects these measures because information given in the
task input can replace work the system would otherwise have to do
itself. In the extreme case where the task interface allows passing an
acceptable answer $`e\in S_{q}^{\tau}`$ directly as the task input
$`p=e`$ and the input bound $`\lambda`$ is large enough to fit that
answer ($`\lambda\geq\min_{e\in S_{q}^{\tau}}|e|`$), the input can
contain the finished answer and reduce execution to reading, checking or
emitting it. For depth to measure a non-trivial computation, the margin
$`s`$ beyond $`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)`$ must be small enough that
any admitted input still leaves substantial work to be carried out. Even
when a full answer does not fit within the bound, partial help such as a
hint or an intermediate result can still do much of the work in advance.
Consequently, a stored component that saves work at a tight input bound
$`\lambda_{0}`$ can cease to provide any saving at a larger bound
$`\lambda_{1}`$ once the extra input gives the relevant help directly.
Writing $`r^{+}`$ and $`r^{-}`$ for the states with and without the
component, this happens when
$`w_{r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{0})<w_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{0})`$ but
$`w_{r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{1})=w_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{1})`$.

### 2.2 Reliable language-model performance

For an LLM run with sampling, we ask how much work is needed to reach a
given probability of success. An interface that lets an input specify
token choices can make an answer obtainable without making it likely
under sampling. These choices count towards the input bound, and
generating the tokens still counts as work. To keep these comparisons
separate, we fix the task input before drawing a random seed from a
fixed distribution. Once both are given, the procedure is deterministic
under fixed numerical operations \[[77](#bib.bib54)\]. Formally, fix a
deterministic procedure $`G`$ that takes the seed as an extra input and
assigns integer work, a finite seed set $`\mathcal{U}`$ and a
distribution $`\nu`$ on that set
\[[definition B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Definition B.5 (Execution under a probability distribution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
For each compared input bound $`\lambda`$, choose a nonempty class
$`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ of admitted task inputs of length at most
$`\lambda`$, with these classes nested as $`\lambda`$ increases. The
full class contains every admitted input within the bound, while a
singleton class fixes the task input. A run from a valid retained state
$`r`$ with public context $`x`$, task input
$`p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ and seed $`u\sim\nu`$ returns
$`e=G(r;x,p,u)`$. Define $`X_{r}^{\tau,q}(p,u)`$ as its work
$`\operatorname{Work}_{G}(r;x,p,u)`$ when it halts with
$`e\in S_{q}^{\tau}`$, and as $`\infty`$ otherwise.

For a fixed procedure, task input $`p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ and
integer work budget $`b\in\mathbb{N}`$, the probability of returning a
response $`e\in S_{q}^{\tau}`$ within work $`b`$ is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
F_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q)=\Pr_{u\sim\nu}\!\left[\begin{array}[]{l}G(r;x,p,u)\text{ returns }e\text{ with work at most }b,\\
q\preceq_{\tau}\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(e)\end{array}\right]=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\nu(u)\,\mathbf{1}\bigl\{X_{r}^{\tau,q}(p,u)\leq b\bigr\}.
``` |  |

Here $`\mathbf{1}\{E\}`$ is $`1`$ when the condition $`E`$ holds and
$`0`$ otherwise. The work $`X_{r}^{\tau,q}(p,u)`$ includes all candidate
generation, testing and final selection steps inside the boundary. Only
the returned output $`e`$ is evaluated, so a valid draft that the
controller discards does not count as success. When several returned
outputs are acceptable, their probabilities of success within budget add
together
([example C.2](#A3.Thmtheorem2 "Example C.2 (Valid code repairs). ‣ C.1 Controlled and sampled execution ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
For an input bound $`\lambda\in\mathbb{N}`$ and reliability threshold
$`0<\alpha\leq 1`$, the *reliable work budget* is the minimum budget
achieving success probability at least $`\alpha`$ over admitted task
inputs $`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$
\[[definitions B.6](#A2.Thmtheorem6 "Definition B.6 (Reliability-indexed region and reliable work budget). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.7](#A2.Thmtheorem7 "Proposition B.7 (Reliable work budgets and deterministic execution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)=\min\bigl\{\,b\in\mathbb{N}:\max_{p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}F_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q)\geq\alpha\,\bigr\},\quad b_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)\leq b\Longleftrightarrow\max_{p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}F_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q)\geq\alpha,
``` |  |

with $`b_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)=\infty`$ when no finite budget
qualifies. Since the seed set is finite, this reliable work budget is
finite exactly when some input in this class succeeds with probability
at least $`\alpha`$. In contrast, the expected value of
$`X_{r}^{\tau,q}(p,u)`$ for a fixed input is infinite whenever that
input has a positive probability of failure.

When $`\nu=\delta_{u_{0}}`$ is concentrated on one seed and
$`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ contains all admitted task inputs within the
bound, every $`0<\alpha\leq 1`$ recovers
$`b_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)=w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ for the
deterministic system with that seed fixed. Two model states can reach
the same outputs at the same work cost when their token choices are
given in the input, yet assign different probabilities to those outputs
when sampled. They can therefore have the same least work when the token
choices are given as input but different reliable work budgets when
$`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ excludes those token choices. In
[example C.1](#A3.Thmtheorem1 "Example C.1 (Equal controlled work with different sampling reliability). ‣ C.1 Controlled and sampled execution ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
two states need the same work to return a correct response when token
choices are specified in the input. With only the empty task input
admitted, independent sampled attempts of cost $`c`$ succeed with
probabilities $`1/10`$ and $`2/5`$. Reaching $`90\%`$ reliability
therefore requires budgets $`22c`$ and $`5c`$, respectively. Training
can improve sampling reliability while leaving the controlled
possibilities unchanged.

Keeping input length, work and reliability separate allows us to compare
the work budgets two systems need at the same input bound and required
success probability.

### 2.3 Work-reducing information

The definition of depth shows how input information can replace work.
Work-reducing information (WRI) is retained information that lowers the
least work needed for a task. Earlier computation can save work on later
tasks in many ways. A process can keep useful byproducts from solving an
earlier task, or a resource can be built ahead of time for later use.
The framework gives a common description of familiar observations about
LLMs, such as the gains from sampling more attempts, caching computation
and fine-tuning a pre-trained model. We measure a component’s
contribution by changing only that component with all other conditions
fixed. For example, we can remove an opening book from a chess engine
while keeping the rest of the engine, the position, the required quality
of play and the input bound fixed. This measures the book’s present
contribution separately from the earlier work of constructing it.

The component can belong to the system’s state or to persistent external
state
\[[definitions B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
[B.11](#A2.Thmtheorem11 "Definition B.11 (Work-reducing information). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.12](#A2.Thmtheorem12 "Remark B.12 (External work-reducing resources). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Write the full persistent condition as $`(r,y)`$, where $`r`$ is
retained state and $`y`$ is persistent external state. Let
$`\bar{\tau}=(\bar{x},\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\preceq_{\tau},\operatorname{perf}_{\tau})`$
be the task without $`y`$. Including $`y`$ in the public context gives
$`\tau[y]=(\langle\bar{x},y\rangle,\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\preceq_{\tau},\operatorname{perf}_{\tau})`$.
For a component $`z`$ from the set $`\mathcal{Z}`$ of valid
alternatives, write $`r[z]=(\rho,z)`$ or $`y[z]=(\eta,z)`$, where
$`\rho`$ is the rest of the retained state and $`\eta`$ the rest of the
external state. The internal comparison is between $`r[z^{+}]`$ and
$`r[z^{-}]`$ at fixed $`y`$, and the external comparison is between
$`y[z^{+}]`$ and $`y[z^{-}]`$ at fixed $`r`$. In either case,
$`(\bar{\tau},q,\lambda)`$ and every other protocol condition are fixed,
so establishing a saving requires comparing the two minima over all
admitted inputs $`|p|\leq\lambda`$.

For a task $`\tau`$, target level $`q`$ and input bound $`\lambda`$,
compare two valid states $`r^{+}`$ and $`r^{-}`$ that differ only in one
component. When both least-work values are finite, their difference is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)=w_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)-w_{r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda).
``` |  |

We call the state difference *work-reducing information* (WRI) when
$`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)>0`$
\[[definitions B.11](#A2.Thmtheorem11 "Definition B.11 (Work-reducing information). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.12](#A2.Thmtheorem12 "Remark B.12 (External work-reducing resources). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
The quantity $`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}`$ measures the work saved by the
component in $`r^{+}`$ relative to the stated alternative in $`r^{-}`$.
This is a saving in work units rather than a description length. The
term WRI is used here for a positive finite saving. At the common input
bound $`\lambda=\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$, this saving equals
$`d_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(s)-d_{r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(s)`$ because the task and
reference complexity are the same in both states. When $`r^{+}`$ makes a
previously unreachable target attainable at input bound $`\lambda`$ (so
$`w_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)=\infty`$ while
$`w_{r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w<\infty`$), we describe this as an
extension of attainability, with
$`(\lambda,w,q)\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r^{+},\tau)\setminus\mathcal{A}_{g}(r^{-},\tau)`$.
Persistent external resources $`y^{+}`$ and $`y^{-}`$ are compared by
the matched external difference
$`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}((r,y^{+}){:}(r,y^{-});\bar{\tau},q,\lambda)=w_{r}^{\tau[y^{-}],q}(\lambda)-w_{r}^{\tau[y^{+}],q}(\lambda)`$,
with the term WRI reserved for the retained-state case $`r^{+}`$ versus
$`r^{-}`$.

A present saving is attributed to earlier work when, relative to a
cut-off separating earlier preparation from the current task, the
following three conditions hold

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{production before cut-off}\;\longrightarrow\;\text{persistence into }r^{+}\;\longrightarrow\;\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)>0.
``` |  |

Here $`z^{+}`$ is produced by counted computation before the cut-off
between acquisition and continuation
\[[definitions B.9](#A2.Thmtheorem9 "Definition B.9 (Reuse of earlier work). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.10](#A2.Thmtheorem10 "Remark B.10 (Cut-off within a computation). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]
and persists in $`r^{+}=r[z^{+}]`$ without being provided again as task
input $`p`$ or other counted external input. On the current task,
retaining it instead of the matched alternative $`z^{-}`$ yields the
positive finite least-work saving shown above, as in knowledge
compilation and amortised inference \[[19](#bib.bib44),
[29](#bib.bib45)\].

In chess, this separates three distinct operations that can reach the
same move quality $`q`$, namely (i) giving a position-specific hint
$`p^{+}`$ in place of $`p^{-}`$ at fixed $`(r,\lambda)`$ so
$`\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p^{+}\rangle)<\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p^{-}\rangle)`$,
(ii) increasing the input bound from $`\lambda_{0}`$ to
$`\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{0}`$ for the same state, giving
$`w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{1})\leq w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{0})`$ and
(iii) replacing $`r^{-}`$ by $`r^{+}`$ (adding an endgame table to the
state) so $`w_{r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)<w_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ at
the same input bound $`\lambda`$.

To compare grasp across a family of tasks $`\mathfrak{T}`$, consider two
states $`r^{+}`$ and $`r^{-}`$ that differ in one component, with all
other conditions fixed. Choose a common input bound $`\lambda_{\tau}`$
for each task $`\tau`$. If every level reachable from $`r^{-}`$ requires
at most as much work from $`r^{+}`$, then each finite work budget allows
at least the same performance levels from $`r^{+}`$. Thus $`r^{+}`$
weakly grasp-dominates $`r^{-}`$ on each task
\[[corollary B.13](#A2.Thmtheorem13 "Corollary B.13 (Least-work comparisons imply grasp dominance). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\begin{gathered}w_{r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{\tau})\leq w_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{\tau})\quad(\forall\tau\in\mathfrak{T},\ \forall q\text{ with }w_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{\tau})<\infty)\\
\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{G}_{r^{-}}^{\tau}(\lambda_{\tau},w)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{r^{+}}^{\tau}(\lambda_{\tau},w)\quad(\forall\tau\in\mathfrak{T},\ 0\leq w<\infty).\end{gathered}
``` |  |

Identical least-work values give mutual weak dominance. A replacement
that saves work on one target can still increase it on another, as when
a routine is selected to minimise total work across several tasks
([sections 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.10](#A3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition C.10 (Finite search for work-reducing routines). ‣ C.2.3 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

For sampling procedures at a target success rate $`\alpha`$, input bound
$`\lambda`$ and seed distribution $`\nu`$, a positive saving in the
reliable work budget

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{\alpha}^{\nu}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)=b_{r^{-}}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)-b_{r^{+}}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)>0
``` |  |

defines a *work reduction at fixed reliability*, which reduces to
$`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)>0`$ when
$`\nu=\delta_{u_{0}}`$ is deterministic and $`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$
contains all admitted task inputs of length at most $`\lambda`$
\[[definition B.14](#A2.Thmtheorem14 "Definition B.14 (Matched work reduction at fixed reliability). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Under a hard cap $`B`$ on total work across retries, lowering
per-attempt work from $`c^{-}`$ to $`c^{+}<c^{-}`$ can increase the
number of completed attempts from $`\lfloor B/c^{-}\rfloor`$ to
$`\lfloor B/c^{+}\rfloor`$. This can raise the probability of success
$`F_{r^{+}}^{\tau,\nu}(p;B,q)`$ even when the best single-attempt
quality is unchanged.

In transformer models, key-value (KV) caching lets later generation
reuse intermediate results computed from a prompt prefix. It stores the
attention keys and values, avoiding the work of computing them again
\[[42](#bib.bib55)\]. Compare $`r^{+}`$, which retains a valid cache,
with $`r^{-}`$, which rebuilds it from the same stored prefix. Suppose
rebuilding adds a fixed amount $`C>0`$ of work to every completed run
and leaves the subsequent generation unchanged. Their run work
variables, success probabilities and reliable work budgets then satisfy
\[[proposition C.4](#A3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition C.4 (Exact work savings from a stored cache). ‣ C.1 Controlled and sampled execution ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
X_{r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(p,u)=X_{r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(p,u)+C,\qquad F_{r^{-}}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b+C,q)=F_{r^{+}}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q),\qquad b_{r^{-}}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)=b_{r^{+}}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)+C,
``` |  |

giving $`\Delta_{\alpha}^{\nu}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)=C`$ for
every $`0<\alpha\leq 1`$ for which both reliable work budgets are
finite. Here $`r^{-}`$ rebuilds the cache once per run. If each attempt
in a sequence instead starts afresh from the stored state, $`r^{-}`$
rebuilds it before every attempt. For a fixed task input, suppose a
controller makes up to $`m`$ independent attempts, each costing $`c`$
work with the cache and succeeding with probability $`\theta\in(0,1)`$.
A required success probability $`0<\alpha\leq 1-(1-\theta)^{m}`$ then
needs a budget that allows
$`k_{\alpha}=\lceil\log(1-\alpha)/\log(1-\theta)\rceil`$ attempts, so
the reliable work budgets are $`ck_{\alpha}`$ and $`(c+C)k_{\alpha}`$
\[[example C.5](#A3.Thmtheorem5 "Example C.5 (Repeated attempts with a stored cache). ‣ C.1 Controlled and sampled execution ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
The saving $`Ck_{\alpha}`$ is nondecreasing in the required success
probability and changes in steps, which is why the comparison is indexed
by $`\alpha`$.

In these comparisons, the cache saves work without changing the
distribution of generated candidates. Other retained components change
candidate generation or selection itself. A unit test changes which
drafts pass internal filtering. Model distillation \[[39](#bib.bib20)\]
trains a smaller model using a larger model’s outputs, while expert
iteration \[[3](#bib.bib46)\] trains a model using results of tree
search. Both produce updated weights $`r^{+}`$. The work required to
produce $`r^{+}`$ is a separate question, which we analyse in
[section 2.5](#S2.SS5 "2.5 Acquisition depth ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

### 2.4 Reducing work with a short program

The work-reducing information in our examples so far, such as an endgame
table, a KV cache or Hashlife’s hash table, stores results for reusable
cases. A short program of fixed size can also reduce computation across
arbitrarily large problem instances without storing past cases. Consider
a row of binary cells updated in parallel according to

|     |                                       |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       a_{i}(t+1)=a_{i}(t)\oplus a_{i+1}(t),
       ```                                    |     |

where $`a_{i}(t)\in\{0,1\}`$ is the state of cell $`i`$ at step $`t`$
and $`\oplus`$ is addition modulo two. In Wolfram’s numbering of
elementary cellular automata, this update is rule 102
\[[106](#bib.bib13)\]. For a horizon $`T=2^{n}`$, given the window of
$`T+1`$ initial cells from $`a_{i}(0)`$ to $`a_{i+T}(0)`$ as public
context, the task $`\tau_{T}`$ is to compute $`a_{i}(T)`$, counting
serial tape-head moves and cell updates, with a binary evaluator whose
level $`1`$ means a correct answer. A naive simulation program stored in
$`r_{\mathrm{sim}}`$ updates the row step by step, performing
$`T(T+1)/2`$ cell updates and $`\Theta(T^{2})`$ work in total
\[[sections C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.8](#A3.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition C.8 (A shortcut for an additive cellular automaton). ‣ C.2.1 Reducing work with a short program ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

After two steps, the middle cell enters twice and cancels modulo two,
$`a_{i}(2)=a_{i}(0)\oplus a_{i+1}(0)\oplus a_{i+1}(0)\oplus a_{i+2}(0)=a_{i}(0)\oplus a_{i+2}(0)`$,
and repeated doubling gives at every power-of-two horizon $`T=2^{n}`$

|     |                                             |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       a_{i}(2^{n})=a_{i}(0)\oplus a_{i+2^{n}}(0),
       ```                                          |     |

a standard property of additive rules \[[67](#bib.bib76)\]. A state
$`r_{\mathrm{short}}`$ that replaces the step-by-step simulator with the
short program that scans from $`a_{i}(0)`$ to $`a_{i+2^{n}}(0)`$ and
adds the two endpoint bits uses
$`w_{r_{\mathrm{short}}}^{\tau_{T},1}(0)=\Theta(T)`$ serial head steps
instead of $`w_{r_{\mathrm{sim}}}^{\tau_{T},1}(0)=\Theta(T^{2})`$ at
input bound $`\lambda=0`$. This gives a work saving
$`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r_{\mathrm{short}}{:}r_{\mathrm{sim}};\tau_{T},1,0)=\Theta(T^{2})`$
from a program of $`O(1)`$ description length independent of $`n`$
\[[proposition C.8](#A3.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition C.8 (A shortcut for an additive cellular automaton). ‣ C.2.1 Reducing work with a short program ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Using $`r_{\mathrm{short}}`$ on the task needs only this $`\Theta(T)`$
work, whereas discovering and verifying that program from scratch is a
separate acquisition task, which we consider next.

### 2.5 Acquisition depth

So far we have compared the work saved by a component already present in
a state $`r`$. A separate question is how much work it takes to obtain
such a state from an earlier starting condition, such as building an
endgame table or training a language model. We define *acquisition
depth* by applying our depth definition
([section 2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Depth and grasp ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"))
to the task of producing a state that meets a required capability.

Suppose we want the system $`g`$ to reach a target performance level
$`q_{j}`$ within input bound $`\lambda_{j}`$ and work budget $`w_{j}`$
on each task $`\tau_{j}`$ in a finite list
$`\Gamma=((\tau_{j},\lambda_{j},w_{j},q_{j}))_{j=1}^{m}`$. The set of
states of $`g`$ that meet all $`m`$ requirements is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
S_{\Gamma}^{g}=\bigl\{\,r\in\mathcal{R}_{g}:(\lambda_{j},w_{j},q_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau_{j})\text{ for every }j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}\,\bigr\}=\bigl\{\,r\in\mathcal{R}_{g}:w_{g,r}^{\tau_{j},q_{j}}(\lambda_{j})\leq w_{j}\;\forall j\,\bigr\}.
``` |  |

Assume $`S_{\Gamma}^{g}`$ is nonempty and that candidate-state validity
and these bounded requirements can be checked by a terminating
procedure, as specified in
[definition B.16](#A2.Thmtheorem16 "Definition B.16 (Acquisition tasks). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
Now consider a state-producing system $`A`$ starting from an initial
state $`a`$ (such as a training procedure whose initial checkpoint
encodes a valid starting state) whose goal on the acquisition task
$`\tau_{\mathrm{acq}}=(x_{\mathrm{acq}},S_{\Gamma}^{g})`$ is to output
any state $`r\in S_{\Gamma}^{g}`$. Here $`(x,S)`$ denotes the binary
task with context $`x`$ whose level $`1`$ accepts exactly the outputs in
$`S`$
\[[definition B.15](#A2.Thmtheorem15 "Definition B.15 (Accepted-set shorthand). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
The evaluator of $`\tau_{\mathrm{acq}}`$ reads the output of $`A`$ as a
state of $`g`$ and checks whether $`g`$ in that state meets every
requirement in $`\Gamma`$. Acquisition work includes producing and
writing out this state and any task tests performed during acquisition.
The budgets $`w_{j}`$ specify the work permitted when the state is used
on the later tasks. Applying our depth definition to this task, the
*acquisition depth* of $`\Gamma`$ from $`(A,a)`$ at margin
$`s\in\mathbb{N}`$ is
\[[definition B.16](#A2.Thmtheorem16 "Definition B.16 (Acquisition tasks). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
d_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s)=w_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}\!\left(\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}}}(1\mid x_{\mathrm{acq}})+s\right),
``` |  |

where
$`\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}}}(1\mid x_{\mathrm{acq}})=\min\{|v|:U_{0}(v,x_{\mathrm{acq}})\in S_{\Gamma}^{g}\}`$
is the length of a shortest reference program that halts on $`U_{0}`$
and returns a qualifying state in $`S_{\Gamma}^{g}`$, given
$`x_{\mathrm{acq}}`$ (and $`d_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s)=\infty`$
when no qualifying state is reachable under this input bound).

For the training comparisons below, $`x_{\mathrm{acq}}`$ specifies the
capability requirement and the fixed training data available before the
run, and the starting checkpoint belongs to $`a`$. Both $`A`$ and
$`U_{0}`$ then have the data as context, while reading and processing
them remain part of the work counted for $`A`$. When
$`x_{\mathrm{acq}}`$ specifies the requirements in a form that one fixed
reference program can check, that program can list valid states of $`g`$
and return the first that meets them. Then
$`\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}}}(1\mid x_{\mathrm{acq}})\leq c`$ for a
constant $`c`$ that does not depend on the training data, and because
least work cannot increase with the input bound,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(c+s)\leq d_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s)\leq w_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s).
``` |  |
````
