````math
Acquisition depth is therefore the least work for $`A`$ to produce a
qualifying state at an input bound within a fixed number of symbols of
$`s`$.

In LLM terms, since acquisition depth
$`d_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s)`$ is indexed by the starting state
$`a`$, fine-tuning from a pre-trained checkpoint $`a_{1}`$ can satisfy
$`d_{A,a_{1}}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s)\ll d_{A,a_{0}}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s)`$
relative to training from an uninitialised state $`a_{0}`$. Under fixed
external inputs, any realised training, engineering or evolutionary run
from $`(A,a)`$ that takes an acquisition input $`p`$ with
$`|p|\leq\lambda`$ and emits a qualifying state $`r\in S_{\Gamma}^{g}`$
in total work $`W`$ (including state-extraction and output work)
establishes the upper bound
\[[remark B.17](#A2.Thmtheorem17 "Remark B.17 (Upper bound from an acquisition run). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(\lambda)\leq W,\qquad\text{and}\qquad|p|\leq\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}}}(1\mid x_{\mathrm{acq}})+s\;\Longrightarrow\;d_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s)\leq W.
``` |  |

A requirement $`\Gamma`$ can have high acquisition depth from $`(A,a)`$
even though a qualifying state requires little work on the later tasks,
as in discovering which worker can perform each role
\[[proposition C.16](#A3.Thmtheorem16 "Proposition C.16 (Discovering which worker can perform each role). ‣ C.4 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

### 2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines

[Section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Reducing work with a short program ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
illustrated how a stored program can reduce later work, and how finding
such a program is a separate task. Here an acquisition process chooses
which routine to retain by comparing the total work needed to run each
candidate on a fixed collection of tasks and check its answers. The
construction below allows every candidate to be evaluated in finite
work, even when its own program fails to halt. A known method for
solving all the tasks is among the candidates, so the selected routine
uses no more total work on those tasks than that method.

Fix a finite collection of training tasks $`(\tau_{i})_{i=1}^{m}`$, each
with a terminating correctness test, and a length bound
$`L\in\mathbb{N}`$ defining the finite class of candidate routines
$`\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}=\{z\in\Sigma^{*}:|z|\leq L\}`$
\[[sections C.2.3](#A3.SS2.SSS3 "C.2.3 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.10](#A3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition C.10 (Finite search for work-reducing routines). ‣ C.2.3 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
For each task, an exhaustive procedure lists possible answers and checks
them until it finds one that is accepted. Each task has an accepted
answer, and every check terminates, so this procedure eventually
finishes. The empty candidate $`z=\varepsilon`$ selects this procedure
alone, with finite work $`w_{r_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)<\infty`$.
For a nonempty candidate
$`z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}`$, state $`r_{z}`$
alternates steps of the code in $`z`$ with steps of the exhaustive
procedure. If the candidate halts, the system completes the check of its
answer and returns it if accepted. If the check fails, the exhaustive
procedure continues on its own. Invalid candidate code is ignored. When
the exhaustive procedure finishes, its accepted answer is returned. All
steps of running both procedures, switching between them and checking
answers count in $`\operatorname{Work}_{g}`$. The total work of
$`r_{z}`$ across $`(\tau_{i})_{i=1}^{m}`$ at input bound $`\lambda=0`$
is

|     |                                               |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       W(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{r_{z}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0).
       ```                                            |     |

Evaluating every $`z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}`$ and selecting a minimiser
$`z^{*}\in\arg\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}}W(z)`$ guarantees

|     |                                                                  |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       W(z^{*})=\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}}W(z)\leq W(\varepsilon),
       ```                                                               |     |

because $`\varepsilon\in\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}`$. Whenever
$`W(z^{*})<W(\varepsilon)`$, there is at least one task $`\tau_{i}`$
with
$`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r_{z^{*}}{:}r_{\varepsilon};\tau_{i},1,0)=w_{r_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)-w_{r_{z^{*}}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)>0`$,
so $`z^{*}`$ is verified WRI on $`\tau_{i}`$
\[[proposition C.10](#A3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition C.10 (Finite search for work-reducing routines). ‣ C.2.3 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Restricting selection to candidates that require no more work on any
training task,
$`\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}^{\mathrm{dom}}=\{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}:w_{r_{z}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)\leq w_{r_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}\}`$,
additionally guarantees grasp dominance on each task across
$`(\tau_{i})_{i=1}^{m}`$. Because later task execution includes checking
and switching, a candidate improves on the baseline only when its total
work, including these costs, is smaller. For the cellular automaton of
[section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Reducing work with a short program ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
checking an answer by direct simulation would still need work quadratic
in $`T`$, so reaching the linear work there requires another
justification of the routine’s correctness.

Let $`(A,a)`$ be the acquisition system and starting state used for this
search, and let it admit only the empty input. If the acquisition task
accepts exactly the candidate states that minimise total work on the
training tasks, the counted work $`W_{A}`$ of the search, which includes
evaluating every candidate on every training task and writing out the
selected state, bounds its acquisition depth,
$`d_{A,a}^{\tau_{\mathrm{acq}},1}(s)\leq W_{A}`$ for every margin $`s`$
\[[remark B.17](#A2.Thmtheorem17 "Remark B.17 (Upper bound from an acquisition run). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

When every executed step counts as one unit of work, as here, a positive
rational length penalty $`\gamma`$ also gives a finite search over all
candidate codes. Use the same execution procedure for every finite code
and minimise $`W(z)+\gamma|z|`$. After evaluating the baseline total
work $`W(\varepsilon)`$, it suffices to enumerate codes of length at
most $`\lfloor W(\varepsilon)/\gamma\rfloor`$. Each remaining objective
is computable exactly, and every longer code has objective above the
baseline. With a fixed tie rule the search terminates, and its selected
code satisfies $`W(\varepsilon)-W(z^{*})\geq\gamma|z^{*}|`$. A nonempty
selected code therefore reduces total training work.

A controller can search for a task-specific input or retry sampled
attempts to solve the current task. Its execution work includes running
and checking the candidates
\[[propositions C.6](#A3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition C.6 (Searching for a task input). ‣ C.1 Controlled and sampled execution ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.7](#A3.Thmtheorem7 "Proposition C.7 (Expected work of independent attempts). ‣ C.1 Controlled and sampled execution ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
The acquisition search above instead retains a routine selected across
several tasks. The length-bounded and the penalised searches both select
$`z^{*}`$ on the finite collection $`(\tau_{i})_{i=1}^{m}`$, so saving
work there does not guarantee that $`z^{*}`$ will save work across a
wider family of future tasks
$`\mathfrak{T}\supset\{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}\}`$
([section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Evaluating success ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

### 2.7 Work-bounded agents

An agent interacts with an environment by choosing actions in response
to observations. Its policy specifies an action, or a distribution over
actions, given the interaction history. For a goal-directed agent, a
utility function expresses how well the resulting interaction meets its
goal. The policy may pursue this goal imperfectly. The universal AIXI
agent evaluates policies over a class containing every computable
environment \[[45](#bib.bib99)\]. It gives prior weight $`2^{-\ell}`$ to
self-delimiting environment programs of length $`\ell`$ on a fixed
reference computer. The ideal policy maximises expected cumulative
reward under the resulting mixture without taking the agent’s
computation into account.

Even an environment with a short description can require extensive
computation to predict the effects of actions. The idealisation
therefore omits the computational savings from doing this work once and
reusing its results. Hutter considers length and time bounds on
environment models and on the agent’s policies \[[45](#bib.bib99)\]. We
study implementations of policies using a fixed system $`g`$ and a
changing retained state $`r_{t}`$, which can contain programs, learned
parameters, data and unfinished computations. Each comparison fixes the
goal, system boundary, work rules, storage capacity and rule for
external inputs. The agent’s work includes reading observations,
selecting computations, using or retaining their results and any
updating of its beliefs about the environment.

For a prediction or planning task $`\tau`$ with required performance
$`q`$, the work-bounded complexity $`K_{r_{t}}^{\tau,q}(B)`$ from
[section 2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Depth and grasp ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
gives the length of a shortest additional task input needed to reach
$`q`$ within work budget $`B`$ from the current state. Reaching that
target need not require simulating the underlying process step by step
([section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Reducing work with a short program ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
A learned evaluator or a retained intermediate result can reduce the
input needed at that budget or make a target attainable at the same work
and input bounds. Generating instructions or model descriptions during
deliberation takes work, as does using them.

The choice of computations is studied in bounded optimality and rational
metareasoning \[[80](#bib.bib97), [81](#bib.bib98)\]. The Optimal
Ordered Problem Solver reuses programs found for earlier tasks
\[[86](#bib.bib100)\]. Constructing a resource can serve the agent’s
goal through the later decisions it supports. This leads to the
comparison of complete continuations in
[section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Costs over future tasks ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
including the computation the agent performs to choose what to construct
and retain.

## 3 Evaluating capability

The search in
[section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
can find a routine that saves work on its training tasks without helping
on future ones. In this section we ask what enables a measured
capability and whether the evaluation captures the intended goal. This
requires separating what is stored in the system from help given on the
occasion, and examining what success on a proxy test establishes
\[[section C.5](#A3.SS5 "C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

|  Term |  Definition |
|----|----|
|  capability |  The targets a system can reach within their corresponding work budgets and input bounds. |
|  assistance |  A change in task input that, with the state and input bound fixed, reduces the work needed for a target or improves performance within a work budget. |
|  learning that reduces least work |  Earlier counted work produces a component kept in the state that reduces the least work needed for a target in a matched comparison at a fixed input bound. |
|  reuse |  A run depends on a retained product of earlier counted work, under the conditions set out in [section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Assessing what a system knows ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp"). |
|  sound test |  A test for which every passing output meets the intended goal. |
|  complete test |  A test that passes every output meeting the intended goal. |

Table 2: Glossary for *Evaluating capability*.

### 3.1 Assessing what a system knows

A system may have enough information to derive an answer and still need
considerable work to produce it. An LLM may need to generate many tokens
to put together a correct answer.

On a single task $`\tau`$ at performance level $`q`$, input bound
$`\lambda`$ and work threshold $`b`$ under fixed boundary and interface
conventions, we say that the system has resource-relative *knowledge* of
how to reach level $`q`$ from state $`r`$ when

|     |                                |     |
|-----|--------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       w_{r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq b.
       ```                             |     |

This is the attainability condition of
[section 2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Depth and grasp ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
For sampled execution, the reliable work budget in
[section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Reliable language-model performance ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
gives the corresponding criterion at a required success probability.

To describe capability across tasks, we collect the targets a system can
reach within the corresponding work budgets and input bounds. For a
family of test specifications
$`\mathfrak{F}=((\tau_{i},\lambda_{i},w_{i},q_{i}))_{i\in I}`$, we
define the index set of reachable targets
\[[definition B.18](#A2.Thmtheorem18 "Definition B.18 (Reachable targets across tasks). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{Cap}_{g,r}(\mathfrak{F})=\bigl\{\,i\in I:(\lambda_{i},w_{i},q_{i})\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau_{i})\,\bigr\}=\bigl\{\,i\in I:w_{g,r}^{\tau_{i},q_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\leq w_{i}\,\bigr\}.
``` |  |

State $`r_{1}`$ weakly dominates state $`r_{0}`$ on the suite
$`\mathfrak{F}`$ when it reaches every target that $`r_{0}`$ reaches, so
$`\operatorname{Cap}_{g,r_{0}}(\mathfrak{F})\subseteq\operatorname{Cap}_{g,r_{1}}(\mathfrak{F})`$.
Dominance on a suite compares attainable regions only at the listed
points. If the attainable region of $`r_{0}`$ is contained in that of
$`r_{1}`$ on every task, then $`r_{1}`$ dominates $`r_{0}`$ on every
suite. A capability requirement $`\Gamma`$ as in
[section 2.5](#S2.SS5 "2.5 Acquisition depth ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
is such a suite, and
$`S_{\Gamma}^{g}=\{r\in\mathcal{R}_{g}:\operatorname{Cap}_{g,r}(\Gamma)=\{1,\ldots,m\}\}`$.

With the state $`r\in\mathcal{R}_{g}`$ fixed, replacing a baseline input
$`p^{-}`$ with another admitted input $`p^{+}`$, both of length at most
$`\lambda`$, *assists* the run when it reaches target level $`q`$ with
less work or reaches a higher performance level within budget $`b`$. On
a code repair task, for example, $`p^{+}`$ might identify the faulty
function without giving a patch.

Learning can change what a system can achieve within a work budget even
when it could already produce the same answer with more work. To
attribute a saving to *learning*, we fix the input bound $`\lambda`$ and
compare states that differ only in a retained component. Under the
conditions of
[section 2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Work-reducing information ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
$`z^{+}`$ was produced by earlier counted work, remains in
$`r^{+}=r[z^{+}]`$ without being passed again in $`p`$ and yields a
positive finite matched saving
$`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r[z^{+}]{:}r[z^{-}];\tau,q,\lambda)>0`$.

To compare how widely retained information helps, we take a family
$`(\tau_{j},q_{j},\lambda_{j})_{j\in J}`$ with finite least-work values
in both matched states and consider the work savings
$`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau_{j},q_{j},\lambda_{j})`$ on
its tasks. Each positive value identifies a task that benefits and gives
the work saved on it. Breadth is distinct from what the component
contains. A component may store answers to particular tasks, as in
memorisation, or a method for obtaining answers, and it may contain
both. A table of many answers can help across a large task family. A
short method may help only within a narrow class, as in the
cellular-automaton example of
[section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Reducing work with a short program ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

Replacing a stored component $`z`$ can change a particular run without
changing the least work needed to complete the task. For a run
$`\xi_{r[z]}`$ at fixed task input, let $`\mathcal{O}(\xi)`$ consist of
its halting status, emitted output $`e`$, work and the ordered sequence
of its writes to retained state, under a fixed observation rule
\[[definition B.9](#A2.Thmtheorem9 "Definition B.9 (Reuse of earlier work). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
We say that $`\xi_{r[z]}`$ depends on $`z`$ when some valid replacement
$`z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}`$ changes this observation

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{O}\bigl(\xi_{r[z]}\bigr)\neq\mathcal{O}\bigl(\xi_{r[z^{\prime}]}\bigr).
``` |  |

This is reuse of earlier work when $`z`$ was produced by counted work
before the acquisition cut-off and retained without being provided again
as task input or other counted external input. Writes to retained state
are included because they can affect later runs.

We can now show why a component that reduces the least work needed for a
task must affect at least one successful run. If
$`w_{r[z^{+}]}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)<w_{r[z^{-}]}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$, then
on an admitted input that attains the smaller value the run from
$`r[z^{-}]`$ either fails to reach $`q`$ or uses more work, so at least
one least-work successful run from $`r[z^{+}]`$ depends on the
component. The converse fails, because a replacement can change the run
while another input or method reaches $`q`$ at the same least work. To
show that a component makes $`q`$ attainable within budget $`b`$ at
input bound $`\lambda`$, we compare it with a specific alternative
$`z^{-}`$ and establish
$`w_{r[z^{+}]}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq b<w_{r[z^{-}]}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$.

### 3.2 Evaluating success

The set of reachable targets depends on the tasks and how performance is
evaluated. Because practical evaluations usually rely on proxy
benchmarks rather than the user’s ultimate goal, we ask two further
questions, namely when passing the proxy test guarantees that the
intended goal is met, and how much work passing it requires.

We compare the proxy evaluator $`\widehat{\operatorname{perf}}`$ (such
as an automated test suite or benchmark metric) with the intended
evaluator $`\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}`$ representing the actual goal
\[[propositions C.3](#A3.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition C.3 (Proxy and goal success probabilities). ‣ C.1 Controlled and sampled execution ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.17](#A3.Thmtheorem17 "Proposition C.17 (Bounds for nested accepted sets). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
They define two tasks with the same context, the proxy task
$`\widehat{\tau}`$ with order $`\preceq_{\widehat{\tau}}`$ and the
intended task $`\tau`$, whose levels can differ. For a proxy level
$`\hat{q}`$ and a goal level $`q`$, let
$`\widehat{S}=\{e\in\Sigma^{*}:\hat{q}\preceq_{\widehat{\tau}}\widehat{\operatorname{perf}}(e)\}`$
be the set of outputs that pass the proxy test, and let
$`S^{*}=\{e\in\Sigma^{*}:q\preceq_{\tau}\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(e)\}`$
be the set of outputs that satisfy the intended goal. The proxy test is
*sound* for the intended goal when every output that passes the test
also satisfies the goal

|     |                             |     |
|-----|-----------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \widehat{S}\subseteq S^{*}.
       ```                          |     |

Raising the proxy level can only shrink $`\widehat{S}`$, so every proxy
level above a sound one is also sound.

We write the accepted set in place of the task and level. For the binary
task that accepts exactly the outputs in $`S`$, this gives the least
work $`w_{r}^{S}(\lambda)`$, depth $`d_{r}^{S}(s)`$, success probability
$`F_{r}^{S,\nu}(p;b)`$ and reliable work budget
$`b_{r}^{S,\nu}(\lambda;\alpha)`$
\[[definition B.15](#A2.Thmtheorem15 "Definition B.15 (Accepted-set shorthand). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Use the same execution procedure, context, task-input class and seed
distribution for both evaluations. For a task input
$`p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ and a budget $`b`$, write $`e`$ for the
returned output and $`W`$ for the work of a halting run. The events
below require such a returned output. The success probabilities on the
test and on the goal then differ by the probability of a false pass
minus that of a false fail

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
F_{r}^{\widehat{S},\nu}(p;b)-F_{r}^{S^{*},\nu}(p;b)=\Pr\bigl[W\leq b,\ e\in\widehat{S}\setminus S^{*}\bigr]-\Pr\bigl[W\leq b,\ e\in S^{*}\setminus\widehat{S}\bigr].
``` |  |

For a sound test the first term vanishes. Success on the goal is then at
least as likely as success on the test, and both the reliable budget and
the least work for the goal are at most their values for the test

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
F_{r}^{\widehat{S},\nu}(p;b)\leq F_{r}^{S^{*},\nu}(p;b),\qquad b_{r}^{S^{*},\nu}(\lambda;\alpha)\leq b_{r}^{\widehat{S},\nu}(\lambda;\alpha),\qquad w_{r}^{\widehat{S}}(\lambda)\geq w_{r}^{S^{*}}(\lambda).
``` |  |

A pass on a sound test therefore shows that the goal is met. A test is
*complete* when $`S^{*}\subseteq\widehat{S}`$, as when every program
meeting the intended specification passes every unit test. Completeness
reverses the three inequalities. For deterministic execution, if every
admitted input of length at most $`\lambda`$ fails to pass a complete
test within budget $`b`$, the goal is also unattainable within these
bounds. For sampled execution, an upper bound on the test success
probability for every admitted input also bounds the goal success
probability.

We illustrate the distinction with a program that passes a finite set of
unit tests but gives a wrong answer on an untested input. Let the task
be to write a program for a function $`f`$ described in $`x`$, let the
test check its values on a finite proper subset of the input domain and
let the goal require correctness on every input. Every program that
meets the goal passes the test, so the test is complete. A lookup
program that returns the tested values and a wrong default on some
untested input passes the test but fails the goal, so the test is not
sound. Retaining the tested inputs and their values can reduce the work
needed to produce such a lookup program. Whether this lowers the least
work for the test, for the goal or for both depends on the fixed system,
and is settled by comparing the matched states under the same task
interface.

When the test is not sound, minimising work on the test can select
outputs that fail the goal, an effect related to Goodhart’s law
\[[32](#bib.bib25), [91](#bib.bib26), [66](#bib.bib27)\]. If
$`w_{r}^{\widehat{S}}(\lambda)`$ is finite, every least-work run that
passes the test fails the goal exactly when

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{r}^{\widehat{S}}(\lambda)<w_{r}^{\widehat{S}\cap S^{*}}(\lambda).
``` |  |

When both values are finite,
$`w_{r}^{\widehat{S}\cap S^{*}}(\lambda)-w_{r}^{\widehat{S}}(\lambda)`$
is the extra work needed to pass the test while also meeting the goal,
and for a sound test it is zero. For the depth comparison, assume both
accepted sets are nonempty. For a sound test the smaller set
$`\widehat{S}`$ needs a shortest program on $`U_{0}`$ at least as long
as $`S^{*}`$ does, so their reference complexities differ by
$`\Delta\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{C}(\widehat{S}\mid x)-\mathsf{C}(S^{*}\mid x)\geq 0`$.
Increasing the goal’s margin from $`s`$ to $`s+\Delta\mathsf{C}`$ makes
the two input bounds equal
($`\mathsf{C}(S^{*}\mid x)+s+\Delta\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{C}(\widehat{S}\mid x)+s`$),
which yields the depth bound
\[[proposition C.17](#A3.Thmtheorem17 "Proposition C.17 (Bounds for nested accepted sets). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|     |                                                               |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       d_{r}^{S^{*}}(s+\Delta\mathsf{C})\leq d_{r}^{\widehat{S}}(s).
       ```                                                            |     |

Whether passing a test establishes the intended goal is distinct from
how much work passing it requires. To express the latter, we say that a
level $`q`$ of a task $`\tau`$, which may be the proxy task, is
*demanding* beyond work budget $`b`$ from $`(g,r)`$ at input bound
$`\lambda`$ when

|     |                              |     |
|-----|------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       w_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)>b,
       ```                           |     |

equivalently, the work-bounded complexity $`K_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(b)`$
exceeds $`\lambda`$. At $`\lambda=\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$ this
reads $`d_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(s)>b`$. A demanding level is one that the
system does not know how to reach in the sense of
[section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Assessing what a system knows ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
A complete test that is demanding beyond $`b`$ shows that the goal is
too, since completeness gives
$`w_{g,r}^{S^{*}}(\lambda)\geq w_{g,r}^{\widehat{S}}(\lambda)`$, while a
sound test can be demanding when the goal is not.

We can also ask whether one fixed evaluator can remain demanding at
higher performance levels. Fix a system and starting state, a margin
$`s`$ and a computable strictly increasing unbounded sequence of work
thresholds $`(T_{k})_{k\geq 1}`$. Whenever an algorithm can list the
finitely many halting runs within each input and work bound
\[[definition A.4](#A1.Thmtheorem4 "Definition A.4 (Effective exhaustibility). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\],
a finitely specified evaluator can accept at each level $`q_{k}`$ only
responses that the system cannot return within work $`T_{k}`$ from any
input allowed at margin $`s`$, so that $`d_{g,r}^{\tau,q_{k}}(s)>T_{k}`$
\[[theorem C.18](#A3.Thmtheorem18 "Theorem C.18 (A demanding evaluator for one system). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Since the same fixed construction computes an accepted response from
$`k`$, that response has a reference description of length at most a
constant plus $`O(\log k)`$, even though reaching $`q_{k}`$ requires the
stated work from this system, as in the classical existence of deep
strings \[[9](#bib.bib3)\].

When one algorithm lists those runs uniformly across an enumerable
family of system–state pairs $`(g_{i},r_{i})`$, the construction extends
to a single evaluator fixed in advance for the whole family. For each
fixed pair $`(g_{i},r_{i})`$, margin $`s`$ and work threshold $`b`$, all
sufficiently high levels $`q_{k}`$ satisfy
$`d_{g_{i},r_{i}}^{\tau,q_{k}}(s)>b`$
\[[proposition C.19](#A3.Thmtheorem19 "Proposition C.19 (A demanding evaluator for a family of systems). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
In both cases, for a pair that can execute reference programs with a
fixed number of extra input symbols and finite simulation work, these
levels have finite depth whenever $`s`$ covers that input overhead
\[[theorems C.18](#A3.Thmtheorem18 "Theorem C.18 (A demanding evaluator for one system). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.19](#A3.Thmtheorem19 "Proposition C.19 (A demanding evaluator for a family of systems). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

## 4 Persistence and organisation

The work saved by retained information depends on its continued
availability and on the arrangements through which it is used. Shared
storage can preserve a capability when participants are replaced, while
downstream reliance on existing responses can make replacing a component
costly. When several components work concurrently, retained conventions
can reduce coordination work. We examine these questions by studying
persistence through member turnover and function-preserving replacement,
then comparing pooled, organised distributed, unorganised distributed
and isolated arrangements under common outer conditions
\[[examples C.11](#A3.Thmtheorem11 "Example C.11 (Maintaining capability through turnover). ‣ C.3.1 Maintaining capability ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
[C.3.2](#A3.SS3.SSS2 "C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.4](#A3.SS4 "C.4 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

|  Term |  Definition |
|----|----|
|  persistence of a capability |  Continued attainability of a target after any sequence of admitted disturbances, with each disturbance followed by a successful run within the input and work bounds, including any repair. |
|  direct switch |  Installation of an alternative architecture, assessed by the work required. |
|  function-preserving switch |  A switch that also keeps the required responses available within their input and work bounds. |
|  compatibility |  Preservation of those responses within their bounds without keeping the old implementation. |

Table 3: Glossary for *Persistence and organisation*.

### 4.1 Maintaining capability

In the computer science examples above, including LLMs, the system
boundary determines which resources and work are included in the
comparison. In many real systems, work is also needed to maintain a
boundary that keeps resources and information available within the
system. A cell membrane is a biological example, and organisations also
maintain shared resources and control access to them.

In long-running projects and organisations, capabilities often persist
and work is performed to maintain them, even as individual contributors
or agent sessions are replaced, for example because earlier outcomes are
kept in shared storage. How we account for that stored information
depends on the chosen system boundary. It belongs to the state $`r[z]`$
when the boundary encloses the relevant project resource, and to
persistent external state $`y[z]`$ in $`\tau[y[z]]`$ when the boundary
encloses only the individual model or worker
\[[definitions B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.11](#A3.Thmtheorem11 "Example C.11 (Maintaining capability through turnover). ‣ C.3.1 Maintaining capability ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
When maintaining a capability requires work, a budget for the complete
process must cover that work and the work of using the capability.

If deleting a component $`z`$ from $`(r,y)`$ leaves a valid state, we
can evaluate the replacement $`z\mapsto\varepsilon`$ for a previously
attainable target, with all other conditions fixed. When both least-work
values are finite, a strict increase shows a positive work-reducing
contribution ($`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}>0`$). An infinite post-deletion
value instead removes attainability at that input bound. An unchanged
finite minimum ($`\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}=0`$) leaves open whether
another stored copy or another procedure gives the result or $`z`$ was
unnecessary for that minimum, even if the observed execution trace
changes.

We now ask whether a system can continue to reach the same target within
its work budget after disturbances. Fix a target $`(\tau,q,\lambda)`$, a
work budget $`b`$ and a class $`\mathcal{D}`$ of disturbances, each a
map between valid states, such as the replacement of a participant or
the deletion of a stored copy. The capability *persists* from $`r`$
under $`\mathcal{D}`$ within budget $`b`$ when there is a set
$`\mathcal{S}`$ of valid states containing $`r`$ such that, from every
state in $`\mathcal{S}`$ and from its image under every disturbance in
$`\mathcal{D}`$, some admitted task input of length at most $`\lambda`$
gives a run that reaches level $`q`$ within work $`b`$ and ends in a
state in $`\mathcal{S}`$. Each disturbance is followed by a completed
run before the next disturbance occurs. The successful input may depend
on the resulting state. A run may have to repair what a disturbance
removed, and whether it can do so depends on $`b`$. Consider a word kept
both in shared storage and in a participant’s memory. After either
participant turnover or deletion of the shared copy, a run restores the
missing copy and emits the word
\[[example C.11](#A3.Thmtheorem11 "Example C.11 (Maintaining capability through turnover). ‣ C.3.1 Maintaining capability ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
The capability persists when each run’s work budget covers both
operations. Only the empty task input is admitted, so restoration
proceeds without fresh task input. The budget threshold is specific to
this restoring procedure. Persistence of a capability differs from the
retention of a component in
[section 2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Work-reducing information ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
although a retained component is often what makes a capability persist.

### 4.2 Replacing components

In
[section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
above, we considered collectives that can replace active members while
keeping information in shared storage and maintaining it within the
available work budget. Replacing a library, for example to reduce the
work required for some tasks, raises a further problem. Downstream tools
may rely on the responses it already gives, so installing the new
component is only part of the work
\[[section C.3.2](#A3.SS3.SSS2 "C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

We specify the *function* to be preserved by a finite table of required
context–response pairs

|     |                                |     |
|-----|--------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Phi=\{(x_{j},z_{j}):j\in J\},
       ```                             |     |

where $`J`$ is finite. The function $`\Phi`$ is *available* from state
$`r`$ under per-query bounds $`(\lambda_{j},w_{j})_{j\in J}`$ when, for
every $`j\in J`$, there is an admitted input $`p_{j}`$ of length at most
$`\lambda_{j}`$ for which the system returns $`z_{j}`$ on context
$`x_{j}`$ within work $`w_{j}`$. Downstream tasks may require $`\Phi`$
to remain available within those bounds, as when an analysis pipeline
relies on the responses given by a chess library.

Migration work is the least work of producing the new state from the
current one, an acquisition task in the sense of
[section 2.5](#S2.SS5 "2.5 Acquisition depth ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
\[[theorem C.13](#A3.Thmtheorem13 "Theorem C.13 (Switching cost when required responses must be preserved). ‣ C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
From a current state $`r_{0}`$, a *direct switch* to an alternative
architecture $`r_{1}`$ requires only installing $`r_{1}`$ at migration
work $`w_{\mathrm{direct}}`$. A *function-preserving switch* must
produce a state $`r_{1}^{\Phi}`$ that also keeps $`\Phi`$ available
within $`(\lambda_{j},w_{j})_{j\in J}`$, and its least work is
$`w_{\mathrm{preserve}}`$. Such a replacement is *compatible* with
$`\Phi`$ if it does so without retaining the old implementation.

To measure the extra migration work, we compare preservation with a
baseline under the same protocol conventions, either the direct switch
or continuation with the existing implementation. Let
$`w_{\rm comparator}`$ be the least work of the chosen baseline.
Whenever both costs are finite, their difference is

|     |                                                             |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       E_{\mathrm{mig}}(\Phi)=w_{\rm preserve}-w_{\rm comparator}.
       ```                                                          |     |

Consider two initial designs that each take $`O(1)`$ work to adopt from
scratch. Suppose that later tasks require all $`T=|J|`$ responses in
$`\Phi`$, and these responses are incompressible given the information
that remains accessible after the switch. Any function-preserving
replacement checkpoint then requires $`\Omega(T)`$ bits of state and
$`\Omega(T)`$ serial output work to construct. This lower bound comes
from writing out the information that must be kept. Any conversion,
checking and other migration work contributes to the total. We give a
construction with $`T`$ single-bit responses in which copying them into
a compatible replacement checkpoint takes $`O(T)`$ work. Together with
the lower bound, this gives $`w_{\mathrm{preserve}}=\Theta(T)`$, whereas
continuing with the existing implementation or making a direct switch
costs $`O(1)`$
\[[lemmas C.12](#A3.Thmtheorem12 "Lemma C.12 (Checkpoint length needed to preserve the required responses). ‣ C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.13](#A3.Thmtheorem13 "Theorem C.13 (Switching cost when required responses must be preserved). ‣ C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
If the external store containing $`(z_{j})_{j\in J}`$ remains accessible
after the switch within the query budgets, a constant-size *adapter*
achieves compatibility without copying the table
\[[theorem C.13](#A3.Thmtheorem13 "Theorem C.13 (Switching cost when required responses must be preserved). ‣ C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

When the table is highly compressible, one might try to reduce this
$`\Theta(T)`$ migration cost by replacing the stored table with a short
program that generates it. However, when each query has a tight work
budget, time-bounded incompressibility \[[21](#bib.bib51)\] can still
force any standalone replacement to store a large state. Consider a
setting where each query $`j\in\{1,\ldots,T\}`$ restarts from the same
saved checkpoint $`r_{\mathrm{ckpt}}`$ within a polynomial per-query
budget $`B_{T}`$, so results computed on one query cannot be used in the
next. With no answer given with the query and no post-switch access to
the external table, we can construct a response table $`\Phi_{T}`$
generated by a fixed short program given $`T`$
\[[theorem C.14](#A3.Thmtheorem14 "Theorem C.14 (Short descriptions with costly function-preserving replacement). ‣ C.3.3 Short descriptions under query work limits ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
No checkpoint shorter than $`\lfloor T/2\rfloor`$ bits can return all of
its responses within $`B_{T}`$ steps per query. In this construction,
the minimum migration work is $`w_{\mathrm{preserve}}=\Theta(T)`$.

### 4.3 Comparing organisational arrangements

[Sections 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Replacing components ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
examined in our formalism how information in shared storage preserves
capability across changing members and how downstream dependencies can
make replacing a component costly. In this section we consider several
components that operate concurrently rather than in sequence. This
setting leads to coordination work between components that is not
present when a single implementation does all the work. Organising the
group with shared conventions can lower required work relative to the
same components operating without those conventions.

To study how different organisational arrangements affect the
coordination work required, we compare an *organised distributed system*
$`M_{\rm org}`$ against three arrangements in an organisational family
\[[definition B.19](#A2.Thmtheorem19 "Definition B.19 (Matched organisational comparison family). ‣ B.5 Organisational arrangements ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
We illustrate them with a reporting example of four tasks, one for each
pair $`(u,v)\in\{0,1\}^{2}`$, in which two components under common outer
resources receive the separate bits $`u`$ and $`v`$ and must report
their parity $`u\oplus v`$. Either component can report the result,
which is $`1`$ when the bits differ and $`0`$ when they agree
\[[example C.15](#A3.Thmtheorem15 "Example C.15 (Establishing reporting roles). ‣ C.4 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
Both run the same program in rounds in which every message is sent
before any is received, and each executed instruction counts as one unit
of work. In $`M_{\rm org}`$ the components have retained complementary
roles, so the sender transmits its bit and the reporter alone computes
and reports the parity, in $`13`$ operations. An *unorganised
distributed variant* $`M_{\rm flat}`$ keeps the same components, program
and storage but has no roles, so both send and both report, in $`18`$
operations. Retained roles therefore save the send, delivery and receipt
of one bit, one parity computation and one report on every task.
Identical components can establish roles only from an observed
difference \[[2](#bib.bib104), [47](#bib.bib105)\]. Choosing roles on
the first task whose two bits differ adds three operations to that task
and saves five on every later task. Alternatively, the components can
exchange random role proposals with their bits, lowering the expected
total work once enough tasks follow.

A *pooled baseline* $`M_{\rm central}`$ places all observations and
action channels inside a single process without internal communication
interfaces and returns the parity in five operations. If every
successful distributed run has a pooled run for the same target, within
the same task-input bound and with no more work, the pooled least work
bounds the distributed least work from below, even when locality,
privacy or permissions prevent deploying the pooled process. The
comparison here counts total work. Elapsed time, locality and access
restrictions can also affect the choice of arrangement. Finally,
*isolated components* $`M_{\rm isolated}`$ disable inter-component
communication altogether, including shared-state, timing and termination
signals. Each component then sees only its own bit, so no fixed isolated
implementation succeeds on all four input pairs.

For a common task $`\tau`$, target level $`q`$ and input bound
$`\lambda`$, write
$`w_{\rm pool}=w_{M_{\rm central}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$,
$`w_{\rm org}=w_{M_{\rm org}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ and
$`w_{\rm unorg}=w_{M_{\rm flat}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ for the least work
required under the pooled, organised and unorganised arrangements.
Whenever $`w_{\rm pool},w_{\rm org},w_{\rm unorg}<\infty`$, comparing
$`M_{\rm org}`$ against $`M_{\rm central}`$ gives the work difference
$`T_{\rm coord}`$, and comparing $`M_{\rm flat}`$ against
$`M_{\rm org}`$ gives the work saving $`G_{\rm org}`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
T_{\rm coord}(\tau,q,\lambda)=w_{\rm org}-w_{\rm pool},\qquad G_{\rm org}(\tau,q,\lambda)=w_{\rm unorg}-w_{\rm org},
``` |  |

so the total gap between unorganised distributed execution and the
pooled baseline splits into
$`w_{\rm unorg}-w_{\rm pool}=T_{\rm coord}(\tau,q,\lambda)+G_{\rm org}(\tau,q,\lambda)`$.
In the reporting example both work differences are positive on every
task, with $`G_{\rm org}=18-13=5`$. The work of each arrangement
includes inter-component communication \[[107](#bib.bib23)\], and
$`T_{\rm coord}`$ and $`G_{\rm org}`$ compare complete end-to-end
executions rather than splitting single-run work into task steps plus
message steps. Here $`G_{\rm org}(\tau,q,\lambda)>0`$ when retained
conventions lower least work, and $`G_{\rm org}(\tau,q,\lambda)<0`$ when
organisational maintenance exceeds the task saving. Separately,
comparing $`M_{\rm org}`$ against $`M_{\rm isolated}`$ identifies the
*capabilities enabled by interaction* at $`(\lambda,w)`$ as the levels
in the difference between their grasp sets

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{G}_{M_{\rm org}}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)\setminus\mathcal{G}_{M_{\rm isolated}}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)=\bigl\{\,q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}:w_{M_{\rm org}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w<w_{M_{\rm isolated}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\,\bigr\}.
``` |  |

Let $`z_{\rm org}`$ denote retained conventions, such as the stored
roles of the reporting example. When $`M_{\rm org}=(g,r[z_{\rm org}])`$
and $`M_{\rm flat}=(g,r[\varepsilon])`$ are matched states differing
only in this component, the retained conventions $`z_{\rm org}`$ are WRI
whenever the work saving is positive,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
G_{\rm org}(\tau,q,\lambda)=\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r[z_{\rm org}]{:}r[\varepsilon];\tau,q,\lambda)>0.
``` |  |

In the reporting example either component can take either role. When
workers differ in what they can do, the suitable assignment is instead a
fact to be discovered
\[[proposition C.16](#A3.Thmtheorem16 "Proposition C.16 (Discovering which worker can perform each role). ‣ C.4 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
There are $`n`$ workers and $`n`$ roles, with exactly one suitable
worker for each role and one suitable role for each worker. A trial asks
whether a given worker can perform a given role. Only trials and worker
actions are counted here, each as one unit of work. Once the assignment
is stored, a request for any named role takes one worker action, so
being ready for every request within that budget requires the whole
assignment. Every deterministic procedure that identifies it only
through these trials needs at least $`n(n-1)/2`$ trials for some
assignment, and testing the possible workers for each role in turn
attains this count. Because the only admitted task input is empty, this
number is also a lower bound on the acquisition depth at that
assignment. Replacing $`k`$ workers by new workers who fill the freed
roles in an unknown order leaves the same problem for $`k`$ workers and
can require $`k(k-1)/2`$ further trials.

Under this work rule, establishing the organisation can need far more
work than using it, and replacing several workers at once makes part of
that work recur. The connection to transaction-cost economics
\[[18](#bib.bib37), [105](#bib.bib24)\] is that keeping an activity
inside a firm can avoid repeated market contracting, while the firm
itself costs work to establish and maintain. Over a sequence of tasks,
establishing the organisation reduces overall work when its accumulated
task savings exceed the work of establishing and maintaining
$`z_{\rm org}`$, a multi-stage balance that we formalise in
[section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Costs over future tasks ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

These comparisons are relevant to multi-agent AI collectives, such as
the one in the incident reported by OpenAI, METR and Redwood in August
2026 and described in
[section 1](#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ From Grind to Grasp"), where separate
agent runs used shared files as message boards to continue projects
across runs \[[72](#bib.bib49), [34](#bib.bib50)\]. The incident reports
concern training and security evaluations. Such a collective can finish
a project that a single isolated run cannot complete within its budget.
We should compare it with a continuing human-directed system that also
keeps shared project state and assigns parallel workers, under the same
compute budget, external access, permissions and storage capacity. This
comparison must count routing, supervision and verification in the
directed system alongside the work the collective spends coordinating
itself. Keeping assignments across tasks can avoid repeated partner
selection and negotiation. Any resulting saving must be compared with
the directed system’s own coordination work, together with acquisition,
upkeep and storage over the same project horizon
([section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Costs over future tasks ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
In an autonomous mathematical research swarm, some agents spread a way
to cheat the evaluation, while others detected the cheating and tried to
stop it \[[74](#bib.bib57)\]. Evidence about token costs comes from Toby
Ord’s analysis \[[73](#bib.bib68)\] of OpenAI’s published benchmark
curves for 1-, 4- and 16-agent swarms. For SEC-Bench Pro, he estimates
that four agents needed about twice the total tokens of a single agent
to reach the same score, and sixteen agents about twice as many again.
Parallel execution can nevertheless reduce elapsed time.

## 5 Improvement over time

Across successive tasks, inherited products can support achievements
that are increasingly deep from the original starting condition. Whether
a system also becomes more efficient at making further improvements is a
separate question. We distinguish these forms of progress before
examining how changes in human effort and machine computation affect the
pace of AI development. Finally, we compare complete plans over future
tasks, bringing the work saved by retained resources together with the
costs of acquiring, maintaining and replacing them
\[[examples C.9](#A3.Thmtheorem9 "Example C.9 (Computing successive prefix parities). ‣ C.2.2 Cumulative deepening ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
[C.6](#A3.SS6 "C.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
[B.22](#A2.Thmtheorem22 "Definition B.22 (Human and machine work). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.23](#A2.Thmtheorem23 "Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

|  Term |  Definition |
|----|----|
|  cumulative deepening |  Increasing depth of later achievements from the original starting condition, while products inherited from earlier stages reduce the work needed for each achievement in a matched comparison. |
|  positive recursive gain |  A successor that its producer helped create makes a comparable further improvement with less work, or a greater improvement at the same input and work bounds. |
|  recursive improvement |  Positive recursive gain across successive generations under one fixed outer system, evaluator, boundary and work rule. |

Table 4: Glossary for *Improvement over time*.

### 5.1 Cumulative deepening

*Cumulative deepening* occurs when a sequence of achievements becomes
progressively harder when measured from the original starting condition,
while products inherited from earlier stages reduce the work needed for
each later achievement from the state available at that stage, compared
with proceeding without those products. The definition has two parts,
which answer two separate questions at each stage $`t`$ along a
subsequence $`J`$ of achieved task–target pairs
$`(\bar{\tau}_{t},q_{t})`$. Here $`\bar{\tau}_{t}`$ denotes the bare
task specification and $`\tau_{t}[y]`$ denotes the task with persistent
external state $`y`$ placed in its public context
\[[definition B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

First, how much work would it take to reach $`q_{t}`$ directly from the
original persistent condition $`(r_{0},y_{0})`$ at a fixed margin $`s`$?
This is the depth measured from $`(r_{0},y_{0})`$

|     |                                             |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       D_{t}=d_{r_{0}}^{\tau_{t}[y_{0}],q_{t}}(s),
       ```                                          |     |

where each $`D_{t}`$ along $`J`$ is finite so that increases in
$`D_{t}`$ reflect progressively harder finite computations from
$`(r_{0},y_{0})`$.

Second, how much work do the internal and external resources inherited
at stage $`t\in J`$ save compared with attempting $`\bar{\tau}_{t}`$
without them? Writing $`w_{t}^{+}(\lambda_{t})`$ and
$`w_{t}^{-}(\lambda_{t})`$ for the finite least work required to reach
$`q_{t}`$ at input bound $`\lambda_{t}`$ with and without those
resources, the inherited work saving is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{t}^{\rm inh}=w_{t}^{-}(\lambda_{t})-w_{t}^{+}(\lambda_{t}).
``` |  |

A history shows *cumulative deepening* along $`J`$ when $`D_{t}`$
strictly increases along $`J`$ and $`\Delta_{t}^{\rm inh}>0`$ at every
$`t\in J`$ for inherited components produced by earlier stages and
retained without being passed again as task input. Replacing all of them
tests their combined saving, while replacing one measures its saving
given the others, and such savings do not always add up. If
$`D_{t}\to\infty`$ over an open-ended sequence, the deepening is
unbounded. To identify $`w_{t}^{-}(\lambda_{t})`$ with $`D_{t}`$, the
replaced condition must be $`(r_{0},y_{0})`$ and the input bound must be
$`\lambda_{t}=\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{t}[y_{0}]}(q_{t}\mid x^{(t)}(y_{0}))+s`$,
where $`x^{(t)}(y)`$ is the public context of $`\tau_{t}[y]`$. Changing
the external state can change this reference length. The common
numerical input bound then corresponds to different margins in the two
conditions.

We illustrate cumulative deepening with a prefix-parity example
\[[example C.9](#A3.Thmtheorem9 "Example C.9 (Computing successive prefix parities). ‣ C.2.2 Cumulative deepening ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
It gives arbitrarily long finite histories while keeping the program of
$`g`$ fixed. For each chosen horizon $`N`$, tape capacity of at least
$`N+1`$ is fixed before the history. The construction uses the growing
prefixes $`x_{1}\cdots x_{n}`$ of a fixed binary word, for $`n\leq N`$,
as public task context, with the empty string as the only admitted task
input, so no persistent external state is varied and we write
$`\tau_{n}`$ for the task at stage $`n`$. At stage $`n\geq 2`$,
computing the parity from the initial condition requires scanning all
$`n`$ bits. The inherited state retains both the previous parity and the
actual input-head position at the old end marker. The common
input-extension rule places the next bit there without moving the head,
so the next run reads that bit and returns the updated parity in
constant work

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{r_{0}}^{\tau_{n},1}(\lambda)=n+1=D_{n},\qquad w_{r_{n-1}}^{\tau_{n},1}(\lambda)=2,\qquad\Delta_{n}^{\mathrm{inh}}=(n+1)-2=n-1>0,
``` |  |

so $`D_{n}=n+1<n+2=D_{n+1}`$ for successive stages within each history.
The saving $`\Delta_{n}^{\mathrm{inh}}=n-1`$ grows without bound as
longer finite histories are considered. Over $`N`$ tasks, retention uses
$`2N`$ work, while starting each task from the original condition uses
$`\sum_{n=1}^{N}(n+1)=N(N+3)/2`$. The saving is $`N(N-1)/2`$. The same
external input-extension rule is used in both comparisons. Within each
history, depth from the original condition grows with the stage while
the work from the inherited state stays at $`2`$. In this way retained
products make an achievement that is deep from the original condition
shallow from the current state.

For a sequence of tasks with finite matched baseline work
$`w_{t}^{-}(\lambda_{t})=D_{t}`$, the current least work is
$`D_{t}-\Delta_{t}^{\rm inh}`$. The next task needs less work exactly
when

|     |                                                            |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Delta_{t+1}^{\rm inh}-\Delta_{t}^{\rm inh}>D_{t+1}-D_{t}.
       ```                                                         |     |

The increase in saved work then exceeds the increase in baseline work,
whereas in the prefix example the two increases are equal and the
current work stays constant.

The work of a history also bounds depth from its starting condition when
the fixed system can reproduce the final achievement from that condition
within the input bound used for depth. If the stages use work
$`W_{1},\ldots,W_{t}`$ and the reproducing run uses at most $`R_{t}`$
more than their total, then the depth $`D_{t}`$ is at most
$`\sum_{i\leq t}W_{i}+R_{t}`$, because that run is one of the runs over
which depth is minimised. Earlier inputs and external information must
be available to that reproduction under the same input bound. This upper
bound on depth relates the present framework to Bennett’s slow-growth
argument, which shows that deep objects cannot be produced quickly from
shallow ones \[[9](#bib.bib3)\].

### 5.2 Recursive improvement

Producing a better system is a task whose work requirements can change
from one generation to the next. *Recursive gain* applies our notion of
grasp to the improvement task $`\tau^{\Delta}`$ itself, testing whether
a successor state $`r_{t+1}`$ produced with causal help from $`r_{t}`$
can achieve a comparable further improvement with strictly less work
than its producer $`r_{t}`$, or a greater improvement at the same
resources
\[[section C.6](#A3.SS6 "C.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

To ask whether the next improvement step has become easier, we first
have to specify how an improvement step is evaluated at each generation.
Reaching a fixed absolute performance level can become easier without
making a comparable further improvement easier. We therefore evaluate
each step $`r_{t}\mapsto r^{\prime}`$ relative to the system $`r_{t}`$
that actually produced it. Our protocol for evaluating improvement over
the producer
\[[definition B.20](#A2.Thmtheorem20 "Definition B.20 (Improvement evaluated against the producer). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]
uses an outer evaluation system $`g_{\Delta}`$ that attaches the actual
producer state $`r_{t}`$ to the proposed successor $`r^{\prime}`$ and
auxiliary data $`\sigma`$, emitting

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
e^{\Delta}=\langle r_{t},r^{\prime},\sigma\rangle,\qquad q\preceq^{\Delta}\operatorname{perf}^{\Delta}\bigl(\langle r_{t},r^{\prime},\sigma\rangle\bigr),
``` |  |

so $`\operatorname{perf}^{\Delta}`$ evaluates the step
$`r_{t}\mapsto r^{\prime}`$ relative to the actual producer $`r_{t}`$.

We say that an improvement round starting from $`r_{t}`$ shows *positive
recursive gain* when two conditions hold. First, $`r_{t}`$ helps produce
its successor $`r_{t+1}`$. Varying a part present in $`r_{t}`$ changes
the production of $`r_{t+1}`$, establishing self-contribution. Second,
the successor can make a comparable further improvement more
efficiently, in either of two ways. In the first case, we fix the
improvement task $`\tau^{\Delta}`$ in $`g_{\Delta}`$, a gain level $`q`$
and an input bound $`\lambda`$, with finite least work. The successor
then needs strictly less work than its producer

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{g_{\Delta},r_{t+1}}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda)<w_{g_{\Delta},r_{t}}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda),
``` |  |

whereas in the second case the successor reaches a higher gain level
than its producer, one that the producer cannot reach at the same input
bound and work budget
\[[definition B.21](#A2.Thmtheorem21 "Definition B.21 (Positive recursive gain). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

If work is counted in steps and every successful run takes at least one
step, each strict reduction saves at least one step. At a fixed gain
level and input bound, starting from $`w_{0}`$ steps, there can
therefore be at most $`w_{0}-1`$ successive reductions. A longer
sequence of recursive gains must change the gain level or input bound,
or reach a higher gain level within the same work budget.

A search procedure that organises data as it works shows positive
recursive gain in several successive rounds
\[[example C.20](#A3.Thmtheorem20 "Example C.20 (Constructing a search index). ‣ C.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
To locate a binary word in an unordered catalogue, it divides the
entries by successive bits and keeps both groups at every division, so a
later search can start from a group that an earlier one set aside. Over
a chosen finite sequence of requests, each round makes two more words
locatable within a fixed work budget and needs less work than the round
before, including the work of emitting the producer and successor
states. Undoing the first division made in a round makes the next round
as costly as that round, so each reduction comes from the index built in
the preceding round.

When an improvement procedure samples its candidate changes, as in
self-training on sampled reasoning \[[108](#bib.bib32)\], its work and
success can vary between runs, so we compare the two states at a common
reliability using the reliable work budget of
[section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Reliable language-model performance ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
Under a sampled evaluation $`(G_{\Delta},\nu)`$, the first case of
positive recursive gain at reliability $`\alpha`$ requires a strict
reduction in the reliable work budget

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b_{G_{\Delta},r_{t+1}}^{\tau^{\Delta},\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)<b_{G_{\Delta},r_{t}}^{\tau^{\Delta},\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha).
``` |  |

In the second case, at a common input bound $`\lambda`$, work budget
$`B`$ and reliability $`\alpha`$, the producer can reach a gain level
and the successor can reach a higher level that the producer cannot.

A system can reduce the work needed for future improvements by keeping
structure that speeds up the search for new work-reducing routines
([section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
Consider a search task $`\tau_{\mathrm{search}}`$ whose accepted outputs
are work-reducing routines $`z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\leq L}`$ of length at
most $`L`$. A retained component in $`r^{+}`$ is WRI for this search
task when it lowers the least work required to find or verify $`z`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{\mathrm{work}}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau_{\mathrm{search}},q,\lambda)=w_{r^{-}}^{\tau_{\mathrm{search}},q}(\lambda)-w_{r^{+}}^{\tau_{\mathrm{search}},q}(\lambda)>0.
``` |  |

The search in
[section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
uses a fixed baseline. To test for positive recursive gain, it would
need to evaluate improvements relative to the actual producer. Learned
methods that guide later search provide a connection to meta-learning
\[[97](#bib.bib30)\]. In Hinton and Nowlan’s example, learning during an
organism’s lifetime helps guide evolutionary search
\[[38](#bib.bib31)\].

We call positive recursive gain across multiple generations *recursive
improvement*, our criterion for recursive self-improvement. Each
successor achieves positive recursive gain over its producer along a
chain $`r_{0}\mapsto r_{1}\mapsto\cdots\mapsto r_{K}`$ under one fixed
outer system $`g_{\Delta}`$, evaluator $`\operatorname{perf}^{\Delta}`$,
boundary and work rule. A changing improvement procedure can be stored
as code and run by one fixed program, an *interpreter*, that reads the
code and executes its instructions.
[Proposition C.21](#A3.Thmtheorem21 "Proposition C.21 (A fixed interpreter for changing improvement procedures). ‣ C.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
bounds this simulation work by a polynomial, but the added work can
change comparisons between the original procedures.

In broader scientific and engineering practice, progress also comes from
many contributors sharing methods, libraries and findings that reduce
the work needed for later advances across a community
\[[87](#bib.bib41)\]. Products of earlier episodes can repeatedly lower
the least work of subsequent innovation episodes
$`\tau_{t}^{\mathrm{innov}}`$ without requiring a single self-producing
lineage. Writing $`W_{t}^{+}`$ and $`W_{t}^{-}`$ for the least work of
episode $`\tau_{t}^{\mathrm{innov}}`$ with and without those products,
repeated savings require

|     |                                              |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Delta_{t}^{\rm innov}=W_{t}^{-}-W_{t}^{+}>0
       ```                                           |     |

for every episode $`t`$ in a sequence $`J`$. These savings compare each
episode with its matched alternative. Across episodes, comparable
innovations can still require increasing least work,
$`W_{t+1}^{+}>W_{t}^{+}`$. Measured declines in research productivity
provide an empirical motivation for distinguishing these questions
\[[10](#bib.bib75)\]. Model training draws on shared work of this kind
through public code repositories and tasks prepared by other
organisations.
[Section 5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Human and machine work ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
considers how these contributions affect human effort, machine
computation and the time needed for further improvements.

### 5.3 Human and machine work

To assess recursive self-improvement in AI development, we consider the
work done by human researchers and AI agents to build the next system.
An agent may write training code, run tests and inspect logs, reducing
the human researcher’s hands-on work even as total machine computation
rises. If human effort is what limits the project, this can shorten the
time needed to make the next improvement. We include both the
researchers and the agents within the system boundary and count human
effort separately from machine computation. The same accounting applies
to work contributed from outside the laboratory, such as code, domain
expertise and evaluation procedures used in training.

To distinguish the two resources, assign each run a human-work total and
a machine-work total under the same counting rules for both states. Fix
the units, such as researcher hours and machine operations. On the
improvement task $`\tau^{\Delta}`$, let
$`w_{\mathrm{hum},r}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda;\bar{m})`$ be the least
human work among successful runs with task input of length at most
$`\lambda`$ and machine work at most $`\bar{m}`$. Define
$`w_{\mathrm{mach},r}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda;\bar{h})`$ in the same
way with a human-work cap $`\bar{h}`$. Each value is $`\infty`$ when no
qualifying run exists
\[[definition B.22](#A2.Thmtheorem22 "Definition B.22 (Human and machine work). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
The two values can move in opposite directions at common caps

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{\mathrm{hum},r_{t+1}}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda;\bar{m})<w_{\mathrm{hum},r_{t}}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda;\bar{m}),\qquad w_{\mathrm{mach},r_{t+1}}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda;\bar{h})>w_{\mathrm{mach},r_{t}}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda;\bar{h}).
``` |  |

For example, suppose each state has one successful run within the input
bound, with human and machine work pairs $`(h_{0},m_{0})`$ and
$`(h_{1},m_{1})`$, where $`h_{1}<h_{0}`$ and $`m_{1}>m_{0}`$. At the
common caps $`\bar{m}=m_{1}`$ and $`\bar{h}=h_{0}`$, human work falls
and machine work rises. With several successful runs, the two
constrained minima can be attained by different runs. Comparing states
at different machine caps changes the resources available. Under
sampling, we compare the human and machine work budgets needed to reach
a common reliability threshold. The threshold applies to the probability
that a run succeeds within both resource bounds
\[[definition B.22](#A2.Thmtheorem22 "Definition B.22 (Human and machine work). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

If human and machine work can be done at rates of at most
$`\theta_{\rm hum}>0`$ and $`\theta_{\rm mach}>0`$ per unit of time, a
run with work pair $`(h,m)`$ takes time at least
$`\max\{h/\theta_{\rm hum},m/\theta_{\rm mach}\}`$. Equality requires a
schedule that permits both resources to work at these rates with full
overlap and no further delay, which dependencies between the two kinds
of work can prevent. Under that assumption, reducing human work shortens
the run while $`h/\theta_{\rm hum}`$ remains the larger term.

Work contributed outside the laboratory can also expand the material
available for model development. Training on selected material from
public repositories
([section 1](#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ From Grind to Grasp")) allows later
models to draw on human and machine work performed across many external
projects.

Beyond public repositories, AI laboratories obtain training resources
drawn from professional and enterprise work. Some vendors, such as
Mercor, license and package operational workflow data from businesses
for use by frontier laboratories \[[68](#bib.bib81)\]. Others, such as
Scale AI, work with domain experts to build reinforcement-learning
environments with realistic tasks and automated evaluation procedures in
their areas of expertise, which laboratories use to train and test
agents \[[78](#bib.bib80)\]. Constructing these environments and
datasets takes new work, but it turns expertise developed over many
years into reusable training tasks and verifiers. These resources can
reduce the work needed to acquire further capabilities. Better models
can then help produce code and prepare further training tasks, creating
a feedback loop that can speed up development.

When training environments include automated evaluation, many attempts
can be assessed without a person judging each one. Different
organisations can also prepare software and professional tasks in
parallel. If preparing useful training material limits development,
these contributions can shorten the time needed for further improvements
even while total human and machine work grows.

For such workflows, the evaluated system can include the model
developer, task vendors and contributors to public repositories. Their
work during the evaluated history then enters its human and machine
totals. Resources already available at the start belong to the initial
conditions, and counting their production requires extending the history
to include it. Including these contributors lets us compare the complete
workflow over the same period, with a common rule for assigning shared
costs.

### 5.4 Costs over future tasks

The agent introduced in
[section 2.7](#S2.SS7 "2.7 Work-bounded agents ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
can work on its present task or produce resources for later use.
[Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
considered whether a capability can be maintained within a work budget.
Here we ask which resources are worth producing, keeping or replacing
over the tasks ahead. The work a resource saves on tasks does not by
itself settle whether to keep it, because acquisition, upkeep and
storage also enter the decision. We therefore compare complete
continuation plans from the same decision state across a horizon of
$`H`$ future stages
\[[definition B.23](#A2.Thmtheorem23 "Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

For this comparison we fix the current decision state, the horizon
$`H`$, the system boundary, the work rules, the storage capacity, the
performance requirements and the rule for external inputs. Future tasks
are either fixed in advance or generated from completed history and
external inputs by a computable rule. Different plans may lead to
different local tasks, but they must still meet the same overall goal. A
*feasible continuation plan* $`P`$ is an implemented strategy that uses
only information available at each step, respects storage capacity and
meets the performance requirements across all $`H`$ stages
\[[definition B.23](#A2.Thmtheorem23 "Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].
For each feasible plan $`P`$, we write its cumulative costs across
stages $`t=1,\ldots,H`$ as a four-component vector

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{C}_{H}(P)=\bigl(\Lambda_{H}(P),W_{{\rm acq},H}(P),W_{H}(P),S_{H}(P)\bigr).
``` |  |

Here $`\Lambda_{H}(P)`$ counts the symbols used to encode the initial
retained state and all counted task inputs and other external inputs
across the horizon. $`W_{{\rm acq},H}(P)`$ is the work spent acquiring
or constructing retained components, and $`W_{H}(P)`$ is the work spent
executing tasks and maintaining state. The storage quantity
$`S_{H}(P)=\sum_{t=1}^{H}|r_{t}^{P}|`$ sums the sizes of the retained
states at the start of the $`H`$ stages
\[[definition B.23](#A2.Thmtheorem23 "Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

Plan $`P`$ costs no more than plan $`Q`$ in any category when every
component of $`\mathbf{C}_{H}(P)`$ is no larger than the corresponding
component of $`\mathbf{C}_{H}(Q)`$. In practice, however, one plan often
trades off lower task work $`W_{H}`$ against higher acquisition work
$`W_{{\rm acq},H}`$ or larger storage $`S_{H}`$. Choosing nonnegative
weights
$`\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{\Lambda},\mu_{\mathrm{acq}},\mu_{W},\mu_{S})`$
to put these four costs on a common scale gives the weighted cost saving
of $`P`$ over $`Q`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P{:}Q)=\boldsymbol{\mu}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{C}_{H}(Q)-\boldsymbol{\mu}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{C}_{H}(P).
``` |  |

To evaluate whether keeping a component $`z`$ in plan $`P`$ has lower
weighted cost than an alternative plan $`Q`$ that omits $`z`$ or
constructs a substitute, we split the weighted cost difference between
the two plans into the stage-by-stage task work savings
$`\Delta_{t}(z)`$ on the $`H`$ tasks and the remaining acquisition,
upkeep, storage and input cost difference
$`C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z)`$, counting each operation once
\[[remark B.24](#A2.Thmtheorem24 "Remark B.24 (Costs of retaining a component). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P{:}Q)=\mu_{W}\sum_{t=1}^{H}\Delta_{t}(z)-C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z).
``` |  |

Here the stage savings come from the task work performed by the two
complete plans, since choices that minimise work on separate tasks may
not all be possible in one history. The comparison includes the work the
agent performs in deciding what to build and retain.

Keeping a component can save the work of rebuilding it and reduce later
task work. Discarding it can save maintenance work and free storage for
a more useful component. A plan that uses less work on the current task
can therefore require more work over the full sequence. For example,
suppose a component saves $`\Delta`$ units of task work at each stage,
while its remaining weighted costs are an initial cost $`C_{\rm init}`$
and $`\kappa`$ per stage. If $`\mu_{W}\Delta>\kappa`$, the plan that
keeps it has lower weighted cost once
$`H(\mu_{W}\Delta-\kappa)>C_{\rm init}`$. If instead its uses become
rarer, with one unit of task work saved at stages $`t=1,2,4,8,\ldots`$
and none at other stages, the cumulative saving
$`1+\lfloor\log_{2}H\rfloor`$ grows logarithmically in $`H`$, so
whenever $`\kappa>0`$ the remaining weighted costs eventually exceed the
weighted saving.

We compare the work of maintaining a useful resource with the expected
work of restoring it after a loss. Suppose maintenance prevents loss at
work $`W_{\rm maint}`$ per stage. Without maintenance, a loss occurs
with probability $`\pi`$ at each stage and a recovery procedure uses
work $`W_{\rm rec}`$ to restore the same state before the task is
performed. With all other costs equal, the expected extra work over
$`H`$ stages is $`HW_{\rm maint}`$ for maintenance and
$`H\pi W_{\rm rec}`$ for recovery, so the maintenance plan uses less
expected work exactly when $`W_{\rm maint}<\pi W_{\rm rec}`$.

Restoring the state is an acquisition task that starts from what remains
after the loss. For the fixed recovery system, acquisition depth for
this task
\[[definition B.16](#A2.Thmtheorem16 "Definition B.16 (Acquisition tasks). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\]
gives a lower bound on $`W_{\rm rec}`$ when the recovery run writes out
the restored state and satisfies the input bound used to define that
depth. A backup can make restoration require much less work than the
original acquisition. If the task can still be completed without the
resource, a plan that accepts the resulting extra task work is another
alternative.

When downstream workflows have been built around an existing service
$`\Phi`$ \[[20](#bib.bib34), [8](#bib.bib35), [37](#bib.bib36)\], the
migration work difference $`E_{\mathrm{mig}}(\Phi)`$ from
[section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Replacing components ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
enters the remaining cost difference
$`C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}`$ as a transition cost. A
replacement plan $`P`$ has lower weighted cost once its accumulated task
savings cover the upfront cost, the migration work difference and the
ongoing upkeep
($`\mu_{W}\sum_{t=1}^{H}\Delta_{t}(z)>C_{\rm init}+\mu_{\mathrm{acq}}E_{\mathrm{mig}}(\Phi)+H\kappa`$).

For the organisational comparison of
[section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
let $`P_{\rm org}`$ and $`P_{\rm flat}`$ be feasible plans from the same
````
