````math
decision state, with and without the retained coordination conventions
$`z_{\rm org}`$. Using their stage work differences
$`\Delta_{t}(z_{\rm org})`$, keeping the conventions is worthwhile when

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P_{\rm org}{:}P_{\rm flat})=\mu_{W}\sum_{t=1}^{H}\Delta_{t}(z_{\rm org})-C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z_{\rm org})>0.
``` |  |

Assume that the stage task, performance target and input bound are the
same for both plans, each complete plan attains its corresponding
single-task minima and those costs include exactly the work assigned to
that stage. The stage differences then equal the work saving from
organisation $`G_{\rm org}(\tau_{t},q_{t},\lambda_{t})`$. Any
maintenance already counted there must be excluded from
$`C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z_{\rm org})`$. In the worker
assignment of
[section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
another plan identifies the worker for a role when that role is first
requested and keeps the result. This can require fewer trials than
preparing the whole assignment, provided each request allows the work
needed for its trials and the worker action
\[[proposition C.16](#A3.Thmtheorem16 "Proposition C.16 (Discovering which worker can perform each role). ‣ C.4 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

## 6 Conclusion

We have developed a formal framework for studying how computation,
learning and organisation reduce the work required to achieve complex
outcomes. In this framework, a system’s *grasp* across a broad range of
tasks provides an efficiency-based view of knowledge and intelligence,
while learning, building reusable resources and sharing infrastructure
are what move a system or community from grind to grasp. Low grasp has
costs beyond efficiency. A system may then fail to reach a sound moral
judgement within the work it spends on the question, and results reached
through grind may add little to human understanding.

We have focused on how an achievement that initially requires extensive
work from a short description can later require much less work when
useful products of earlier work are preserved. This *work-reducing
information* can consist of byproducts saved while solving an immediate
task or resources built deliberately for later use, such as a chess
endgame table, a software library or a large language model. Treating
acquisition as a separate task distinguishes the work of obtaining a
capability from the work of using it. A benchmark score alone does not
show how much computation was used or how much information was given, so
it has become common to compare systems across work budgets. As
evaluations approach their scoring limits, comparing the work needed to
reach a given score can still distinguish systems.

Progress across sequences of tasks and system generations takes several
distinct forms. In *cumulative deepening*, successive achievements have
increasing depth from a fixed starting condition, while inherited
products reduce the work needed at each stage. When those products are
shared, different contributors can use earlier results and methods
without repeating the work that produced them. *Positive recursive gain*
requires a specific producer–successor comparison, in which the
successor needs less work than its producer for a comparable next
improvement, or achieves more with the same resources. In contemporary
AI research, automating coding and experimentation can also reduce human
researcher effort per advance even when total machine computation rises,
so human and machine work should be tracked separately. Progress can
then also be faster in elapsed time, provided that human effort is the
bottleneck.

Finally, for multi-agent organisation, the appropriate comparison for a
collective of short-lived independent runs is a continuing
human-directed system with shared project state and orchestrated
parallel worker agents. The two arrangements should be compared under
the same task requirements and resource limits, with the same access to
external information and tools. The directed arrangement can save work
when the partner selection and negotiation it avoids outweigh its
additional routing, supervision and verification costs. A full project
comparison also includes acquisition, upkeep and storage over the same
horizon
([section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Costs over future tasks ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
The risk of a collective pursuing its own goals supports leaving
agreements, commitments and project goals to human users, while agents
execute approved work and direct subordinate workers. Acquiring
resources can help agents pursue many different goals
\[[70](#bib.bib101), [99](#bib.bib102)\], so an agent’s efficiency in
building and keeping them does not establish that it serves its users’
goals. Human institutions can then integrate increasingly capable AI by
checking its results, proposals and tests against the requirements of
assigned roles, while keeping direction over how their arrangements and
priorities develop. Otherwise, an emergent agent society risks directing
its work towards structures that sustain and enable it, rather than
towards meeting human society’s needs.

## Appendix A The model

These appendices collect the model, definitions and proofs used in the
main text.
[Appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp") fixes the
computational setting,
[appendix B](#A2 "Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
defines the quantities the main text uses, and
[appendix C](#A3 "Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
states and proves the results the text relies on.

All represented objects are finite strings over a fixed alphabet
$`\Sigma`$ with $`|\Sigma|\geq 2`$, and $`\Sigma^{*}`$ denotes the set
of all finite strings over $`\Sigma`$, including the empty string
$`\varepsilon`$. We use $`\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}`$. Binary
constructions use $`\Sigma=\{0,1\}`$. We fix standard efficient
injective encodings throughout. For a fixed number of fields, the tuple
code $`\langle\cdot\rangle`$ has length equal to the sum of the field
lengths up to logarithmic delimiter overhead, and its fields can be
encoded and recovered in polynomial time in the total encoded length.
When a self-delimiting representation is needed, $`\overline{z}`$
denotes the fixed code of $`z`$, whose endpoint can be determined from
the code itself. For $`n\in\mathbb{N}`$, the code length
$`\ell(n)=|\overline{n}|`$ satisfies $`\ell(n)=O(\log(n+2))`$. Finite
transition tables and tape configurations use explicit encodings with
polynomial-time parsing and updates.

For the later bounds on replacement-state length and migration work,
tables and other stored bit strings use explicit serial representations.
A stored string of $`T`$ bits, together with fixed routing or lookup
code and $`O(\log(T+2))`$ symbols specifying its length and format,
occupies $`O(T+1)`$ symbols. Copying and emitting this representation
takes $`O(T+1)`$ work. These conventions govern representation overhead.
A short program can still require extensive work to execute.

A system $`g`$ is a deterministic finite-description process with a
declared class $`\mathcal{R}_{g}`$ of valid finite states. A run starts
from $`r\in\mathcal{R}_{g}`$. The chosen boundary fixes which persistent
objects belong to the system or its retained state. The task protocol
specifies the task-input interface and the rule for providing other
external (*exogenous*) inputs.

For a finite realised run, external events and random choices can be
represented as exogenous inputs. Conditional on them, execution is
deterministic. This deterministic description assigns no probabilities,
while
[definition B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Definition B.5 (Execution under a probability distribution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
adds a probability distribution on a finite source set and specifies how
observations are delivered and execution outcomes are observed for
reliability comparisons.

###### Definition A.1 (Evaluation protocol).

A task is a tuple

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\tau=(x,\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\preceq_{\tau},\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}),
``` |  |

where $`x\in\Sigma^{*}`$ is public context,
$`(\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\preceq_{\tau})`$ is a nonempty partially ordered
performance set, and the task evaluator
$`\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}`$ grades
completed responses. An evaluation protocol fixes the system boundary,
the work-counting rule, the task evaluator, the task-input interface and
any rule for providing further external inputs or environment responses.
Fix a system $`g`$, a valid retained state $`r`$, context $`x`$, an
admitted task input $`p`$ and a realisation of those external inputs.
Write $`\operatorname{Work}_{g}`$ for the work rule applied to $`g`$. If
this run halts without declaring failure, it returns

|     |                           |     |
|-----|---------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       e=g(r;\langle x,p\rangle)
       ```                        |     |

with assigned work $`\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p\rangle)`$ and
performance $`\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(e)`$. A run that declares
failure also halts. Every halting run has assigned work and a terminal
valid retained state.

The complete description of the run distinguishes a returned response, a
declared failure and non-termination. It includes the emitted response,
assigned work and terminal retained state wherever defined. Here
$`g(r;\langle x,p\rangle)`$ denotes only the returned response, and is
undefined after a declared failure or non-termination.

This mathematical description does not assume an algorithm for deciding
non-termination. When a comparison varies the system or retained state,
the boundary, work rule, task interface, evaluator and any environment
rule are held fixed while the named system or state varies.

The variable string $`p`$ is the task input admitted by the protocol,
and the input bound below applies to its length $`|p|`$. The public
context $`x`$, fixed system description, and retained state are not
counted again as task input. The reference machine is conditioned on
$`x`$. External data are available to it when encoded in that context.

A deterministic run is fixed by the system, its initial retained state,
the public context, the admitted task input and the external inputs
given under the protocol. Data given through the task-input interface
count towards $`|p|`$, while exogenous draws are recorded separately.
The protocol fixes which exogenous inputs are counted. Comparisons of
acquisition histories also count the work and inputs used to obtain the
retained state.

Sampled execution uses the source distribution and delivery protocol of
[definition B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Definition B.5 (Execution under a probability distribution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
For interactive tasks, the protocol also specifies when observations
become available to the evaluated system
([definition B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Definition B.5 (Execution under a probability distribution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

###### Definition A.2 (Accepted level sets and binary tasks).

For a nonempty accepted set $`S\subseteq\Sigma^{*}`$, write
$`\tau=(x,S)`$ for the binary task with performance set $`\{0,1\}`$ in
its usual order and $`\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(e)=1`$ exactly when
$`e\in S`$. More generally, for a graded task and level $`q`$, its
accepted level set is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
S_{q}^{\tau}=\{e\in\Sigma^{*}:q\preceq_{\tau}\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(e)\}.
``` |  |

Thus every performance threshold with nonempty $`S_{q}^{\tau}`$
corresponds to an accepted-set target.

The general theory permits a declared rule assigning finite nonnegative
real work to finite executions. Ordinary work budgets are nonnegative
real numbers. The reliability definitions of
[section B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and the constructions below that count every transition use integer work
and budgets. The general theory also permits work rules that count only
selected operations. A run can then continue indefinitely with finite
counted work.

###### Definition A.3 (Transition-complete systems).

In a *transition-complete* system, output is initially empty, every
executed transition contributes one work unit and at most one response
symbol is emitted per transition. Hence a returned response $`e`$
satisfies $`|e|\leq\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p\rangle)`$.

###### Definition A.4 (Effective exhaustibility).

At a fixed public context, bounded-work runs of a system–state pair are
*effectively exhaustible* when an algorithm, given finite bounds on
task-input length and assigned work in the chosen finite encoding,
terminates with the complete finite list of halting runs within those
bounds and their complete run descriptions. A family is uniformly
effectively exhaustible when one such algorithm also takes the pair’s
index and any varying context as input.

This property lets the evaluator constructions in
[theorems C.18](#A3.Thmtheorem18 "Theorem C.18 (A demanding evaluator for one system). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.19](#A3.Thmtheorem19 "Proposition C.19 (A demanding evaluator for a family of systems). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
list all responses obtainable within the chosen input and work bounds.
For a transition-complete system with a decidable admitted-input set and
fixed external inputs provided by a computable procedure, it suffices to
simulate each bounded-length input for the work bound in transitions. A
work rule that counts only selected operations need not permit this
finite enumeration.

Finally, fix a universal deterministic reference machine $`U_{0}`$.
Reference description lengths below are defined using $`U_{0}`$ with the
public context given as a condition. Changing the fixed universal
reference machine changes these description lengths only by the usual
fixed constants. One $`U_{0}`$ is fixed throughout. These constants
bound length differences, not the work required at the resulting input
bounds. For finite strings $`u,v`$, the plain conditional complexity
$`\mathsf{C}(u\mid v)`$ is the length of a shortest program that halts
on $`U_{0}`$ with output $`u`$, given $`v`$:

|     |                                                |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathsf{C}(u\mid v):=\min\{|a|:U_{0}(a,v)=u\}.
       ```                                             |     |

Multiple conditioning fields are combined through the fixed tuple code.
A reference program is a program for $`U_{0}`$. The evaluated system
$`g`$ can use a different input format and its own work rule.

## Appendix B Definitions and basic results

We first define attainability, depth and grasp, then reliable execution
under a specified probability distribution. Replacing one component
while all other conditions are fixed distinguishes retained and external
contributions. The remaining definitions concern acquiring capability,
organising work, producing further improvements and comparing complete
continuation plans.

### B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp

###### Definition B.1 (Attainable region and least work).

For an evaluated system $`g`$, valid state $`r`$, and task $`\tau`$, let
$`\lambda\in\mathbb{N}`$ bound the length of the task input, let
$`w\geq 0`$ be a real work budget, and let $`q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}`$ be
a required performance level. Define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau)=\left\{(\lambda,w,q):\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\text{ admitted }p\text{ with }|p|\leq\lambda,\\
\operatorname{Work}_{g}(r;\langle x,p\rangle)\leq w,\\
q\preceq_{\tau}\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(g(r;\langle x,p\rangle))\end{array}\right\}.
``` |  |

For a level $`q`$, the least work at input bound $`\lambda`$ is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)=\min\{w:(\lambda,w,q)\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau)\},
``` |  |

with value $`\infty`$ when no qualifying run exists.

For fixed $`\lambda`$, there are finitely many admitted input strings of
length at most $`\lambda`$, each with one deterministic run once the
exogenous inputs are fixed. The set of successful work values is
therefore finite and has a minimum when nonempty. Increasing $`\lambda`$
or $`w`$, or replacing $`q`$ by a level $`q^{\prime}\preceq_{\tau}q`$,
preserves attainability. Least work describes the fixed system and its
work rule, with the minimum taken over admitted task inputs.

###### Definition B.2 (Reference description complexity and depth).

For $`q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}`$ with nonempty accepted level set
$`S_{q}^{\tau}`$, the reference description complexity is the length of
a shortest program that halts on $`U_{0}`$ and returns an output in
$`S_{q}^{\tau}`$, given $`x`$:

|     |                                                                    |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)=\min\{|a|:U_{0}(a,x)\in S_{q}^{\tau}\}.
       ```                                                                 |     |

Let the margin $`s\in\mathbb{N}`$ be the number of extra input symbols
permitted beyond this shortest reference description length. At input
bound $`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$, the depth of $`(g,r)`$ is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
d_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(s)=w_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s).
``` |  |

Thus depth is the least work on the current task when the task input is
at most $`s`$ symbols longer than the shortest reference description of
a successful response, adapting logical and computational depth to the
evaluated system \[[9](#bib.bib3), [5](#bib.bib8)\]. The nonempty
accepted set makes the reference complexity finite, and depth is
$`\infty`$ when the evaluated system has no qualifying run at that input
bound.

To recover the conditional, plain-complexity form of Bennett’s logical
depth, consider a task with one accepted output and let $`g`$ execute
$`p`$ as a program on $`U_{0}`$, with $`x`$ as auxiliary input and no
additional stored information. Count work in steps of $`U_{0}`$. Then
the depth is the least running time of a program for that output whose
length is within $`s`$ of its shortest reference description,
conditional on $`x`$.

To compare two reference machines $`U`$ and $`U^{\prime}`$, keep
$`(g,r)`$, the task and the work rule fixed. Use superscripts
$`U,U^{\prime}`$ on $`\mathsf{C}`$ and $`d`$ to indicate only which
machine defines description length. If
$`|\mathsf{C}^{U}_{\tau}(q\mid x)-\mathsf{C}^{U^{\prime}}_{\tau}(q\mid x)|\leq c`$
for a fixed $`c\in\mathbb{N}`$, then for $`s\geq c`$,

|     |                                                           |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       d^{U^{\prime}}(s+c)\leq d^{U}(s)\leq d^{U^{\prime}}(s-c),
       ```                                                        |     |

with the common task, level and evaluated pair suppressed in this
display. Indeed, the input bound for $`d^{U}(s)`$ lies between those for
$`d^{U^{\prime}}(s-c)`$ and $`d^{U^{\prime}}(s+c)`$, and least work is
nonincreasing in that bound. The universal-machine comparison gives such
a constant uniformly. The inequalities compare shifted margins on the
same work profile.

As in
[section 2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Depth and grasp ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
grasp describes which performance levels a system can reach at each work
budget, with the input bound fixed. The proposition below relates this
view of capability to the work required to reach each level.

###### Definition B.3 (Grasp profile and dominance).

Fix a task $`\tau`$ and input bound $`\lambda`$. Define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{G}_{g,r}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)=\{q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}:(\lambda,w,q)\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau)\}.
``` |  |

The family of these sets over work budgets is the grasp profile at input
bound $`\lambda`$. Conversely, at fixed finite work budget $`w`$ and
target level $`q`$, define the work-bounded complexity

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
K_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(w)=\min\{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}:w_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w\},
``` |  |

with value $`\infty`$ when the set is empty. For two pairs
$`(g_{0},r_{0})`$ and $`(g_{1},r_{1})`$ evaluated on the same task under
a common protocol, write
$`\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)=\mathcal{G}_{g_{j},r_{j}}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$.
Pair $`1`$ weakly grasp-dominates pair $`0`$ over a budget set
$`\mathcal{W}`$ when

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)\quad\text{for every }w\in\mathcal{W}.
``` |  |

For a task family the same inclusion is required taskwise.

If each pair reaches a level that the other misses at some compared
budget, neither pair dominates the other over $`\mathcal{W}`$.

###### Proposition B.4 (Work-bounded complexity and grasp dominance).

Fix a system $`g`$, valid state $`r`$ and task $`\tau`$. For finite
$`w\geq 0`$, input bound $`\lambda\in\mathbb{N}`$ and level
$`q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
K_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(w)\leq\lambda\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad w_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w,\qquad\mathcal{G}_{g,r}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)=\{q:K_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(w)\leq\lambda\}.
``` |  |

For a level with nonempty $`S_{q}^{\tau}`$ and margin
$`s\in\mathbb{N}`$, a finite depth satisfies
$`K_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(d_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(s))\leq\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$.

Fix $`\tau`$, $`\lambda`$ and two pairs $`(g_{0},r_{0})`$ and
$`(g_{1},r_{1})`$ evaluated as in
[definition B.3](#A2.Thmtheorem3 "Definition B.3 (Grasp profile and dominance). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
Write
$`\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)=\mathcal{G}_{g_{j},r_{j}}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$
and $`w_{j}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)=w_{g_{j},r_{j}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$. Let
the compared budget set $`\mathcal{W}`$ contain
$`w_{0}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ for every level $`q`$ reachable by pair
$`0`$ at input bound $`\lambda`$. Then the following are equivalent. (i)
$`\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$
for every $`w\in\mathcal{W}`$, and (ii)
$`w_{1}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w_{0}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ for every level
$`q`$ reachable by pair $`0`$. If the performance order is total and
both grasp sets have greatest elements at every compared budget, (i) is
equivalently the statement that pair $`1`$ attains at least as high a
performance as pair $`0`$ at every compared budget.

###### Proof.

If the set defining $`K`$ is nonempty, its least integer member attains
the bound, and all larger input bounds also suffice. Conversely, any
qualifying $`\lambda`$ bounds that minimum. This proves the first
equivalence. The equality of grasp sets follows from their definition.
At finite depth, substitute $`w=d_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(s)`$ and
$`\lambda=\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$ in that equivalence.

Assume (i) and put $`w=w_{0}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$. Then
$`q\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$, so by inclusion
$`q\in\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$ and hence
$`w_{1}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w`$. Conversely, if
$`q\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$ then
$`w_{0}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w`$. Clause (ii) gives
$`w_{1}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)\leq w`$, so
$`q\in\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\tau}(\lambda,w)`$. Attainable level sets are
downward closed in any performance order. In a total order, inclusion of
two such sets with greatest elements is equivalent to ordering those
elements. ∎

### B.2 Reliable execution

For the language-model processes in
[section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Reliable language-model performance ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
we also ask how likely a run is to return a successful response within a
work budget. We add a probability distribution on a finite source set
(such as the seed set in
[section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Reliable language-model performance ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"))
for the bounded executions below.

###### Definition B.5 (Execution under a probability distribution).

Fix a deterministic finite-description execution procedure $`G`$, a
valid retained state $`r`$, task $`\tau`$ with public context $`x`$,
task-input interface, and nonnegative integer work rule. Fix a finite
set $`\mathcal{U}`$ of external input strings and a probability mass
function $`\nu`$ on it. The distribution and the rule selecting it are
fixed independently of the outcomes being evaluated. The same
distribution is used for all task-input strings and all retained states
being compared. Its specification can depend on the common public
context. The task input $`p`$ is given independently of the realised
source draw.

Write $`G(r;x,p,u)`$ for the run from $`r`$ with context $`x`$, task
input $`p`$ and external input $`u`$. If it returns a response $`e`$
with $`q\preceq_{\tau}\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}(e)`$, let
$`X_{r}^{\tau,q}(p,u)`$ be its work. Set this variable to $`\infty`$ for
every other run. For a finite budget $`b\in\mathbb{N}`$, define the
success probability

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
F_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q)=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\nu(u)\mathbf{1}\{X_{r}^{\tau,q}(p,u)\leq b\}.
``` |  |

The indicator $`\mathbf{1}\{E\}`$ equals $`1`$ if $`E`$ holds and $`0`$
otherwise. The notation suppresses the execution procedure and other
fixed evaluation conditions.

The protocol’s fixed input-delivery and observation procedure delivers
$`u\in\mathcal{U}`$ through the input channels and observes the actual
response, assigned work, status and terminal state of $`G(r;x,p,u)`$
wherever defined. In interactive uses, it reveals observations only at
their authorised stage and computes action-dependent responses under the
fixed environment rule. This procedure implements the protocol rather
than accepting the evaluated system’s report of what occurred. Its
operations are counted when they lie inside the evaluated boundary. A
failing or nonhalting run is not successful.

Finite random seeds, bounded sequences of sampler inputs, and a finite
collection of environmental input sequences fit this definition. These
draws are not counted as deliberate task input, but they remain external
inputs of the run. Searching for a favourable seed is a different
process whose work and selected information must be counted.

Representing a run as deterministic given its external inputs does not
give an outside user access to a hosted model’s hidden state.
Reproducing a run through an application programming interface (API)
still requires the inputs and execution conditions on which that run
depends.

###### Definition B.6 (Reliability-indexed region and reliable work budget).

For each input bound under consideration, let $`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$
be a nonempty class of admitted task-input strings of length at most
$`\lambda`$. The input classes are nested as $`\lambda`$ increases over
the compared input bounds. Set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{G}^{\nu}(r,\tau)=\{(\lambda,b,q,\alpha):\;&0<\alpha\leq 1,\quad\exists p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\\
&F_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q)\geq\alpha\},\end{split}
``` |  |

and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)=\min\{b\in\mathbb{N}:(\lambda,b,q,\alpha)\in\mathcal{A}_{G}^{\nu}(r,\tau)\},
``` |  |

with value $`\infty`$ if no finite budget qualifies.

This is the least budget within which some task input in
$`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ gives a probability of at least $`\alpha`$ of
returning a response that meets target $`q`$.

Nesting alone does not imply that these classes are the length
truncations of one fixed set of admitted strings. A fixed-input
comparison with string $`p_{0}`$ uses
$`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}=\{p_{0}\}`$ only for $`\lambda\geq|p_{0}|`$. The
finite-alphabet length bound makes each class finite. No algorithm for
finding a maximising input is assumed.

For comparison with a deterministic procedure $`g`$, write
$`\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})_{\lambda}`$ and let
$`w_{g,r;\mathcal{H}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ be the minimum in
[definition B.1](#A2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition B.1 (Attainable region and least work). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
restricted to $`p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$, with value $`\infty`$ when
none succeeds. The corresponding attainable region requires the
successful task input $`p`$ to belong to $`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$.

###### Proposition B.7 (Reliable work budgets and deterministic execution).

Keep the execution procedure and probability distribution fixed. Then
$`F_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q)`$ is nondecreasing in $`b`$ and nonincreasing
under increases in the performance threshold. For finite $`b`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)\leq b\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\max_{p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}F_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q)\geq\alpha.
``` |  |

If $`G`$ ignores $`u`$, or $`\nu`$ is concentrated at one fixed
$`u_{0}`$, define $`g`$ by fixing $`u_{0}`$ (any element of
$`\mathcal{U}`$ when $`G`$ ignores $`u`$) in $`G`$. With the same work
rule, task and retained state, for every $`0<\alpha\leq 1`$ and compared
input bound $`\lambda`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)=w_{g,r;\mathcal{H}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda).
``` |  |

At integer work budgets and with inputs restricted to
$`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$, every positive reliability requirement
therefore gives the same attainable region as deterministic execution by
$`g`$. When $`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ contains all admitted task inputs
of length at most $`\lambda`$, the restricted minimum equals
$`w_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ at that input bound.

###### Proof.

For fixed $`p,u`$, the event $`X_{r}^{\tau,q}(p,u)\leq b`$ is nested as
$`b`$ increases. Raising $`q`$ can only remove accepted responses.
Summing the indicators with nonnegative weights proves monotonicity. A
nonempty set of integer qualifying budgets has a least member, and the
maximum over inputs is attained over its finite class. These facts give
the displayed inverse. In the deterministic case every event probability
is zero or one, so a positive reliability threshold holds exactly when
the run on $`p`$ succeeds within $`b`$. Substitution in the reliability
and restricted ordinary region definitions, followed by taking the least
budget, proves recovery, including the value $`\infty`$. ∎

At fixed $`\lambda`$ and $`\alpha`$, the attainable performance set at
budget $`b`$ is $`\{q:b_{r}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)\leq b\}`$. For
two states, the first state’s set is contained in the second’s at every
budget exactly when the second state needs no larger least budget at any
level that the first state reaches, by the argument of
[proposition B.4](#A2.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition B.4 (Work-bounded complexity and grasp dominance). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
With a singleton input class, these sets and least budgets concern that
fixed input. Larger classes permit minimisation over their members.

Comparing success probabilities requires the probability distribution
for each system. Comparing outcomes run by run additionally requires a
joint distribution specifying how the two systems’ source inputs are
paired. In an interactive comparison, a fixed environment response rule
can still return different replies when the systems take different
actions.

### B.3 Persistent information

To measure the work saved by a component,
[section 2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Work-reducing information ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
compares variants that differ only in that component, at the same input
bound. The definition below includes both retained state and persistent
external information, whose treatment depends on the boundary discussed
in
[section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

Fix a later process and write its persistent condition as $`(r,y)`$.
Here $`r\in\mathcal{R}_{g}`$ is valid retained state belonging to the
system, and $`y`$ is a finite persistent external state stored in a
distinguished field of public context and available across the occasions
under comparison. The counted task input is not part of $`y`$.

For a retained-component replacement write $`r[z]=(\rho,z)`$, where
$`\rho`$ is the fixed rest of the state, with $`y`$ fixed. For an
external-state replacement write $`y[z]=(\eta,z)`$ with $`r`$ fixed. In
either case $`z`$ ranges over a valid replacement class $`\mathcal{Z}`$,
and we write $`r^{\pm}=r[z^{\pm}]`$ or $`y^{\pm}=y[z^{\pm}]`$.

The common task specification, with its persistent external-state field
omitted, is
$`\bar{\tau}=(\bar{x},\mathcal{Q},\preceq,\operatorname{perf})`$. To
include a particular external state $`y`$, write
$`\tau[y]=(\langle\bar{x},y\rangle,\mathcal{Q},\preceq,\operatorname{perf})`$.

###### Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement).

A matched comparison requires both variants $`z^{+}`$ and $`z^{-}`$ to
be valid under the same protocol. The later process, task-input
interface, admitted task inputs, task evaluator, any environment rule,
system boundary and work rule are fixed. Every nonvaried state and
public-context field is also fixed, as is the source realisation.

At input bounds where the relevant minima are finite define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{\rm work}((r^{+},y){:}(r^{-},y);\bar{\tau},q,\lambda)=w_{g,r^{-}}^{\tau[y],q}(\lambda)-w_{g,r^{+}}^{\tau[y],q}(\lambda)
``` |  |

for a retained-state replacement, and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{\rm work}((r,y^{+}){:}(r,y^{-});\bar{\tau},q,\lambda)=w_{g,r}^{\tau[y^{-}],q}(\lambda)-w_{g,r}^{\tau[y^{+}],q}(\lambda)
``` |  |

for a persistent external state replacement. A joint replacement of
retained and external fields, with every other condition fixed, uses

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{\rm work}((r^{+},y^{+}){:}(r^{-},y^{-});\bar{\tau},q,\lambda)=w_{g,r^{-}}^{\tau[y^{-}],q}(\lambda)-w_{g,r^{+}}^{\tau[y^{+}],q}(\lambda)
``` |  |

when both values are finite.

With the external state fixed,
[section 2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Work-reducing information ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
uses the shorter notation
$`\Delta_{\rm work}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)`$ with
$`\tau=\tau[y]`$.

When a difference is attributed to the content of a field, the
replacements should keep the rest of the representation and its access
costs fixed, for example by using fields of equal width. Otherwise the
change of layout must be accounted for in the comparison.

###### Definition B.9 (Reuse of earlier work).

Fix an observation rule $`\mathcal{O}`$ on executions before making any
component replacement. The rule, execution representation and selected
events are common to all variants. For a fixed task input and source
realisation, a later run $`\xi_{z}`$ depends on $`z`$ under this
observation rule when some distinct valid matched replacement
$`z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}`$ gives
$`\mathcal{O}(\xi_{z})\neq\mathcal{O}(\xi_{z^{\prime}})`$. For
historical attribution, $`z`$ must have been produced by counted work
before the cut-off between acquisition and continuation, persisted into
the later state and not been given again as later task input or other
counted exogenous input. Dependence on $`z`$ under these conditions is
reuse of earlier work.

For the finite constructions below, $`\mathcal{O}(\xi)`$ comprises the
halting status, emitted response and assigned work, wherever defined,
together with the ordered sequence $`J(\xi)`$ of writes executed to
retained state. Each event gives the written location and value. The
observer does not insert untouched starting memory into $`J`$. A write
of the value already present is still an event, as is a write
subsequently undone.

Work and storage are still counted from the complete run description in
[definition A.1](#A1.Thmtheorem1 "Definition A.1 (Evaluation protocol). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
The dependence test uses only the observations selected by
$`\mathcal{O}`$.

Only writes to retained state enter $`J`$. The dependence test ignores
differences in scratchpad computation when all selected observations
remain the same. Applications using another execution representation
must specify its corresponding update events. For an interactive system,
the selected observations can include the sequence of its external
actions. Applying the observation rule requires access to the selected
events. Its halting-status observation cannot in general be settled by a
finite timeout, since a run still unfinished at that point may halt
later.

We count any work used to keep stored information available. That work
can be the same for different stored values. Dependence on a value is
tested by replacing it and comparing the selected observations. The
contribution of the storage device that keeps it available is tested by
replacing that device under a fixed preservation task and specified
disturbances.

Work done inside the system boundary to monitor execution or send
information to an outside observer counts. The observer’s inspection and
log can be left outside the work and storage totals. If the process
reads that log to construct messages or choose actions, the log’s
updates, reads and storage must be included in the comparison.

###### Remark B.10 (Cut-off within a computation).

The cut-off between acquisition and continuation in
[definition B.9](#A2.Thmtheorem9 "Definition B.9 (Reuse of earlier work). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
can lie inside a larger computation. If a component is produced before
this cut-off and remains in a valid state at that cut-off, the
continuation can be evaluated as an ordinary later run from that state.
Work before the cut-off belongs to the realised acquisition history.
Work after the cut-off is the work of the continuation, so the prefix is
not counted again as later work.

The earlier computation can be treated as an acquisition run when it
returns a qualifying state under the acquisition protocol. Its work
includes any operations needed to extract and emit that state. The
resulting run gives the upper bound on least acquisition work in
[remark B.17](#A2.Thmtheorem17 "Remark B.17 (Upper bound from an acquisition run). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
The acquisition-depth bound also requires the input-length condition
stated there.

###### Definition B.11 (Work-reducing information).

For a matched retained-state replacement with the persistent external
state fixed, the state difference is *work-reducing information* for
$`(\tau,q,\lambda)`$ when
$`\Delta_{\rm work}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)>0`$. Attributing this
WRI to earlier work requires the production, persistence and input
conditions in
[definition B.9](#A2.Thmtheorem9 "Definition B.9 (Reuse of earlier work). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

WRI identifies a beneficial replacement, and the associated numerical
quantity measures the work saved relative to the specified alternative
component. With all other conditions fixed, the comparison attributes
the difference in least work to that replacement.

###### Remark B.12 (External work-reducing resources).

A persistent external resource can lower least work without being WRI
for the system. WRI names the favourable retained-component case. The
same stored resource can therefore be WRI under a boundary that includes
it and an external work-reducing resource under a narrower boundary.

###### Corollary B.13 (Least-work comparisons imply grasp dominance).

Fix a task family $`\mathfrak{T}`$ and valid states $`r^{+},r^{-}`$ of
one system $`g`$, evaluated on each task under a common protocol. For
each $`\tau\in\mathfrak{T}`$, fix an input bound $`\lambda_{\tau}`$.
Suppose that, for every $`\tau\in\mathfrak{T}`$ and every level $`q`$
with $`w_{g,r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{\tau})<\infty`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{g,r^{+}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{\tau})\leq w_{g,r^{-}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda_{\tau}).
``` |  |

Then $`(g,r^{+})`$ weakly grasp-dominates $`(g,r^{-})`$ on each task at
every finite work budget.

###### Proof.

Apply the implication from (ii) to (i) in
[proposition B.4](#A2.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition B.4 (Work-bounded complexity and grasp dominance). ‣ B.1 Attainability, depth and grasp ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
to each task, with $`\mathcal{W}`$ the set of finite work budgets. ∎

The matched comparison of
[definition B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
can also be made at fixed reliability, using the reliable work budgets
of
[definition B.6](#A2.Thmtheorem6 "Definition B.6 (Reliability-indexed region and reliable work budget). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

###### Definition B.14 (Matched work reduction at fixed reliability).

Write $`r^{+}=(\rho,z^{+})`$ and $`r^{-}=(\rho,z^{-})`$ for valid
retained states, where $`\rho`$ is the fixed rest of the state and $`z`$
is the component being compared. Use the same execution procedure, task
and evaluator, input classes, source mechanism and distribution,
persistent external conditions, interfaces, work rule, and other
resource limits. Fix $`q,\lambda,\alpha`$ for which both reliable work
budgets are finite. Their work effect is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{\alpha}^{\nu}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau,q,\lambda)=b_{r^{-}}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)-b_{r^{+}}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha).
``` |  |

A positive value is a retained-state work reduction at fixed
reliability. Its historical attribution requires the same production,
persistence and input conditions
([definition B.9](#A2.Thmtheorem9 "Definition B.9 (Reuse of earlier work). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

In the deterministic case of
[proposition B.7](#A2.Thmtheorem7 "Proposition B.7 (Reliable work budgets and deterministic execution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
this work effect is the difference of the two ordinary least-work values
restricted to the same $`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$. For the full
bounded-length input class it equals $`\Delta_{\rm work}`$ in
[definition B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
so its positive case is ordinary WRI. A restricted-input claim must keep
that input restriction.

### B.4 Acquiring capability

In
[section 2.5](#S2.SS5 "2.5 Acquisition depth ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
the output to be produced is a state with a required capability. We use
the same depth definition for this acquisition task, with accepted
outputs restricted to states that meet the requirement.

###### Definition B.15 (Accepted-set shorthand).

For a nonempty accepted set $`S\subseteq\Sigma^{*}`$ and public context
$`x`$, let $`(x,S)`$ denote the binary task of
[definition A.2](#A1.Thmtheorem2 "Definition A.2 (Accepted level sets and binary tasks). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
Write

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{C}(S\mid x):=\mathsf{C}_{(x,S)}(1\mid x),\qquad w_{g,r}^{(x,S)}(\lambda):=w_{g,r}^{(x,S),1}(\lambda),
``` |  |

and

|     |                                           |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       d_{g,r}^{(x,S)}(s):=d_{g,r}^{(x,S),1}(s).
       ```                                        |     |

For execution under a probability distribution $`\nu`$, write similarly
$`F_{r}^{(x,S),\nu}(p;b):=F_{r}^{(x,S),\nu}(p;b,1)`$ and
$`b_{r}^{(x,S),\nu}(\lambda;\alpha):=b_{r}^{(x,S),\nu}(\lambda;1,\alpha)`$.
With the system and public context fixed, we write
$`w_{r}^{S}(\lambda)`$, $`d_{r}^{S}(s)`$, $`F_{r}^{S,\nu}(p;b)`$ and
$`b_{r}^{S,\nu}(\lambda;\alpha)`$. When the state is also fixed, we omit
its subscript, as in $`w^{S}(\lambda)`$ and $`d^{S}(s)`$. Here
$`\mathsf{C}(S\mid x)`$ is the minimum description length of an accepted
response, not the description length of $`S`$ as a set. For the empty
set, use $`F_{r}^{\emptyset,\nu}(p;b)=0`$ and
$`w_{r}^{\emptyset}(\lambda)=b_{r}^{\emptyset,\nu}(\lambda;\alpha)=\infty`$.

###### Definition B.16 (Acquisition tasks).

An acquisition pair $`(A,a)`$ is a deterministic acquisition system
$`A`$ with valid starting state $`a`$, evaluated under the protocol of
[definition A.1](#A1.Thmtheorem1 "Definition A.1 (Evaluation protocol). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
with its responses interpreted as candidate valid states. Least work and
depth for an acquisition task are therefore the ordinary quantities
$`w_{A,a}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$ and $`d_{A,a}^{\tau,q}(s)`$.

A *checkpoint* is a finite encoding of a valid state emitted or carried
by an acquisition or improvement process. The acquisition system
produces a state to be used by the evaluated system $`g`$ on the later
tasks.

For $`g`$ with a decidable class of valid states, an effective finite
capability requirement is a finite family

|     |                                                       |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Gamma=((\tau_{j},\lambda_{j},w_{j},q_{j}))_{j=1}^{m}
       ```                                                    |     |

for which one algorithm, given a valid candidate state $`r`$ and index
$`j`$, terminates and decides whether
$`(\lambda_{j},w_{j},q_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau_{j})`$. Its
accepted state set is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
S_{\Gamma}^{g}=\{r\in\mathcal{R}_{g}:(\lambda_{j},w_{j},q_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau_{j})\text{ for every }j\}.
``` |  |

When $`S_{\Gamma}^{g}`$ is nonempty, fix a public acquisition context
$`x_{\rm acq}`$ and define the binary acquisition task
$`\tau_{\rm acq}=(x_{\rm acq},S_{\Gamma}^{g})`$. Its acquisition depth
from $`(A,a)`$ at margin $`s`$ is $`d_{A,a}^{\tau_{\rm acq},1}(s)`$.

A sufficient condition for decidability of the bounded-success tests is
uniform effective exhaustibility of these bounded runs as $`r`$ varies
([definition A.4](#A1.Thmtheorem4 "Definition A.4 (Effective exhaustibility). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
together with decidable response-threshold tests. Membership is
decidable by checking state validity and the finitely many
bounded-success tests.

Completing the acquisition task requires emitting a qualifying state,
even when that state is already available as a checkpoint. The
membership test concerns a given state. Determining whether any
qualifying state exists is a separate problem. For a training comparison
with a fixed corpus available before execution, include the corpus and
the capability specification in $`x_{\rm acq}`$, and keep the starting
checkpoint in $`a`$. The length of any reference program that uses this
context to produce a qualifying state is an upper bound on
$`\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\rm acq}}(1\mid x_{\rm acq})`$. The minimum is over
all such programs. Acquisition depth then minimises the counted work of
$`(A,a)`$ over its admitted inputs at the resulting input bound. Reading
the corpus and emitting the state are included in the training work
rule.

###### Remark B.17 (Upper bound from an acquisition run).

Fix an acquisition pair $`(A,a)`$ and a binary acquisition task
$`\tau_{\rm acq}=(x_{\rm acq},S)`$, for example with
$`S=S_{\Gamma}^{g}`$. If a successful run using a task input $`p`$ with
$`|p|\leq\lambda`$ emits a valid state in $`S`$ as its response in total
work $`W`$, including the required output work, then

|     |                                            |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       w_{A,a}^{\tau_{\rm acq},1}(\lambda)\leq W.
       ```                                         |     |

If moreover

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       |p|\leq\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\rm acq}}(1\mid x_{\rm acq})+s,
       ```                                                       |     |

then

|     |                                      |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       d_{A,a}^{\tau_{\rm acq},1}(s)\leq W.
       ```                                   |     |

Thus one realised acquisition history gives an upper bound on the least
work. To prove that acquisition requires at least a given amount of
work, we must show that every successful run within the same input bound
uses at least that much work.

###### Definition B.18 (Reachable targets across tasks).

For a family
$`\mathfrak{F}=((\tau_{i},\lambda_{i},w_{i},q_{i}))_{i\in I}`$ of tasks
$`\tau_{i}`$ with input bounds $`\lambda_{i}`$, work budgets $`w_{i}`$
and target levels $`q_{i}`$, define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{Cap}_{g,r}(\mathfrak{F})=\{i\in I:(\lambda_{i},w_{i},q_{i})\in\mathcal{A}_{g}(r,\tau_{i})\}.
``` |  |

Dominance on $`\mathfrak{F}`$ is inclusion of these sets.

### B.5 Organisational arrangements

The organisational comparison in
[section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
distinguishes work saved relative to unorganised distributed components,
extra work relative to a pooled implementation, and capability made
possible by interaction.

###### Definition B.19 (Matched organisational comparison family).

Fix one task $`\tau`$, performance level $`q`$, outer task interface,
outer exogenous-input and boundary convention, common work-accounting
units with communication counted \[[107](#bib.bib23)\], and a total
resource limit.

A matched organisational family consists of four system–state pairs
$`M=(g_{M},r_{M})`$ under that common comparison.

- •
  $`M_{\rm central}`$ is a specified pooled implementation with the
  observation and action interfaces of the protocol.
- •
  $`M_{\rm org}`$ is the organised distributed implementation.
- •
  $`M_{\rm flat}`$ is a distributed implementation with matched
  component resources and interfaces but without the shared conventions
  being compared.
- •
  $`M_{\rm isolated}`$ is a matched noncoordinating implementation used
  when the question is whether interaction creates capability
  unavailable to separated components.

The same exogenous inputs and permitted actions cross the common outer
boundary in every comparison. The implementations can distribute them
differently among their internal components, and all work traces are
counted in the same units.

Write $`w_{M}^{\tau,q}(\lambda):=w_{g_{M},r_{M}}^{\tau,q}(\lambda)`$.
For the pooled, organised and unorganised arrangements, abbreviate these
quantities as $`w_{\rm pool}`$, $`w_{\rm org}`$ and $`w_{\rm unorg}`$,
respectively. Comparisons are made only at input bounds for which the
relevant least-work quantities are finite. The work difference from
pooling is $`T_{\rm coord}=w_{\rm org}-w_{\rm pool}`$ and the work saved
by organisation is $`G_{\rm org}=w_{\rm unorg}-w_{\rm org}`$ at such
input bounds.

For a finite task family, these comparisons are made separately on each
task. Capability enabled by interaction is compared separately through
the attainable regions.

### B.6 Recursive improvement

Improvement work must be compared relative to the producer from which
each round begins. Reaching a fixed absolute performance level can
become easier without making a comparable further improvement easier.
The protocol below therefore includes the actual producer in the
evaluated output.

###### Definition B.20 (Improvement evaluated against the producer).

Fix an outer improvement system $`g_{\Delta}`$ that wraps a local
improvement process. Producer and successor states are valid starting
states of $`g_{\Delta}`$. When domain capability is compared, they are
also interpreted as states of one fixed domain system under a common
encoding. When a call begins from a producer state $`r`$ and proposes a
successor $`r^{\prime}`$ together with procedure data $`\sigma`$, the
fixed outer wrapper emits

|     |                                                |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       e^{\Delta}=\langle r,r^{\prime},\sigma\rangle.
       ```                                             |     |

The producer field is inserted by the wrapper from the actual pre-call
state. It is not chosen by the local improvement process. An improvement
task

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\tau^{\Delta}=(x^{\Delta},\mathcal{Q}^{\Delta},\preceq^{\Delta},\operatorname{perf}^{\Delta})
``` |  |

evaluates such outputs by one fixed relation between producer and
successor, optionally together with fixed procedural requirements.
Constructing and emitting the wrapper tuple is counted by the work rule.
Comparability of equal performance levels across producers is defined by
this common evaluator, not by a baseline-independent scale.

###### Definition B.21 (Positive recursive gain).

Suppose an improvement round beginning at $`r_{t}`$ causally produces
$`r_{t+1}`$, and a matched component replacement establishes that a
component already present at $`r_{t}`$ contributed to producing that
successor. Positive recursive gain occurs when the successor can make a
comparable further improvement more efficiently on one fixed task that
evaluates improvement over the producer.

The successor can require less work for the same further improvement. In
this case, for some common gain target $`q`$ and input bound
$`\lambda`$, both least-work values are finite and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{g_{\Delta},r_{t+1}}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda)<w_{g_{\Delta},r_{t}}^{\tau^{\Delta},q}(\lambda).
``` |  |

Alternatively, the successor can achieve a greater improvement at the
same resources. At a common input bound $`\lambda`$ and work budget
$`B`$, there are levels $`q\prec^{\Delta}q^{\prime}`$ for which the
producer’s and successor’s grasp sets satisfy

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
q\in\mathcal{G}_{g_{\Delta},r_{t}}^{\tau^{\Delta}}(\lambda,B),\qquad q^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{G}_{g_{\Delta},r_{t}}^{\tau^{\Delta}}(\lambda,B),\qquad q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{g_{\Delta},r_{t+1}}^{\tau^{\Delta}}(\lambda,B).
``` |  |

Here $`q\prec^{\Delta}q^{\prime}`$ means $`q\preceq^{\Delta}q^{\prime}`$
and $`q\neq q^{\prime}`$. Each gain is evaluated relative to its actual
producer.

For the comparison at a common gain target $`q`$, setting the input
bound to $`\mathsf{C}_{\tau^{\Delta}}(q\mid x^{\Delta})+s`$ for a common
margin $`s`$ gives the corresponding depth comparison. Both least-work
values in this comparison are finite. A finite later value against an
infinite baseline instead extends attainability.

The same gain level can require different work from different producers.

The replacement test for self-contribution uses the dependence test in
[definition B.9](#A2.Thmtheorem9 "Definition B.9 (Reuse of earlier work). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
A difference confined to the copied producer field of $`e^{\Delta}`$, or
to a component copied unchanged into the successor, does not count.

Attributing the successor’s advantage on further improvement to an
inherited product requires a separate test that replaces only that
product in $`r_{t+1}`$, with the other state fixed. Attribution also
requires evidence of the product’s production and persistence history.
For retained products this is the comparison of
[definition B.11](#A2.Thmtheorem11 "Definition B.11 (Work-reducing information). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
for WRI attributed to earlier work. Persistent external products use the
external-state comparison in
[definition B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

###### Definition B.22 (Human and machine work).

Fix a task $`\tau`$ with context $`x`$, target $`q`$, system $`g`$,
state $`r`$ and input bound $`\lambda\in\mathbb{N}`$. Assign each finite
run two finite nonnegative costs, $`\operatorname{Work}_{g}^{\rm hum}`$
for human work and $`\operatorname{Work}_{g}^{\rm mach}`$ for machine
work, in fixed units. At nonnegative finite caps $`\bar{h},\bar{m}`$,
among successful runs with admitted input $`p`$ of length at most
$`\lambda`$, define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{hum},r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda;\bar{m})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\min\{\operatorname{Work}_{g}^{\rm hum}(r;\langle x,p\rangle):\operatorname{Work}_{g}^{\rm mach}(r;\langle x,p\rangle)\leq\bar{m}\},`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{mach},r}^{\tau,q}(\lambda;\bar{h})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\min\{\operatorname{Work}_{g}^{\rm mach}(r;\langle x,p\rangle):\operatorname{Work}_{g}^{\rm hum}(r;\langle x,p\rangle)\leq\bar{h}\}.`$ |  |

An empty qualifying set gives value $`\infty`$. For sampled execution
$`(G,\nu)`$ as in
[definition B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Definition B.5 (Execution under a probability distribution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
use the same two cost rules and fix reliability $`0<\alpha\leq 1`$. The
reliable human-work budget at machine cap $`\bar{m}`$ is the infimum of
$`h\geq 0`$ for which some $`p\in\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ gives
probability at least $`\alpha`$ of success at level $`q`$ with human
work at most $`h`$ and machine work at most $`\bar{m}`$, all on the same
run. If no such $`h`$ exists, the budget is $`\infty`$. Interchanging
the two costs and caps defines the machine-work budget.

### B.7 Costs over future tasks

A work saving can be outweighed by the cost of acquiring and maintaining
the useful structure. The complete-plan comparison in
[section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Costs over future tasks ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
therefore includes acquisition and execution work, updates, storage use
and the lengths of descriptions and task inputs.

Fix a current decision state, finite horizon $`H`$, system boundary and
work rules, retained-state capacity $`S_{\max}`$, required performance
levels, a protocol for exogenous inputs and an overall goal expressed as
a condition on the completed history. Future tasks are specified in
advance or determined by a fixed computable rule from completed history
and realised exogenous inputs. The protocol also fixes the rule for
identifying the start of each stage and the source realisations on which
success is required. A probabilistic requirement instead fixes a source
distribution and a minimum success probability.

###### Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans).

A *feasible continuation plan* $`P`$ is a controller program, executed
inside the boundary under the fixed work rules, that selects runs and
updates over these $`H`$ stages and meets the following conditions.

1.  (i)
    Each choice is computed using only the controller’s current
    configuration and the information delivered to it at that decision.
    Everything kept from one stage to the next belongs to the retained
    state.
2.  (ii)
    On every execution, the retained state stays within capacity
    $`S_{\max}`$.
3.  (iii)
    On the source realisations fixed by the protocol, or with at least
    the minimum success probability, the required performance is
    achieved and the completed history meets the fixed overall goal,
    including when plans lead to different local tasks.

For such a plan, define its cost vector by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{C}_{H}(P)=\bigl(\Lambda_{H}(P),W_{{\rm acq},H}(P),W_{H}(P),S_{H}(P)\bigr),
``` |  |

where $`r_{t}^{P}`$ is the retained state at the start of stage $`t`$
and $`\overline{\cdot}`$ is the self-delimiting code of
[appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"). At each
stage, $`p_{t}^{P}`$ encodes all counted task inputs and
$`\zeta_{t}^{P}`$ all other counted exogenous inputs, using fixed tuple
encodings if there are several. Set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Lambda_{H}(P)=|\overline{r_{1}^{P}}|+\sum_{t=1}^{H}|\overline{p_{t}^{P}}|+\sum_{t=1}^{H}|\overline{\zeta_{t}^{P}}|.
``` |  |

$`W_{{\rm acq},H}(P)`$ counts acquisition, construction and migration
work, while $`W_{H}(P)`$ counts other execution work, including ordinary
state updates and maintenance. A common allocation rule fixed before
comparing plans assigns each counted operation to exactly one of these
coordinates. Also,

|     |                                    |     |
|-----|------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       S_{H}(P)=\sum_{t=1}^{H}|r_{t}^{P}|
       ```                                 |     |

sums the stored lengths over the stages. The initial state enters both
$`\Lambda_{H}`$ as description length and $`S_{H}`$ as storage use.
Producing the final state $`r_{H+1}^{P}`$ is counted in work, but its
length enters this sum only if a further stage is included. Plans
compared from the same decision state run on the same system from the
same initial state. Their programs, or instructions selecting them,
enter the counted input. Plan $`P`$ costs no more than $`Q`$ in any
category when every coordinate of $`\mathbf{C}_{H}(P)`$ is no larger
than the corresponding coordinate of $`\mathbf{C}_{H}(Q)`$.

For given nonnegative coefficients
$`\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{\Lambda},\mu_{\rm acq},\mu_{W},\mu_{S})`$
define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{C}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P)=\boldsymbol{\mu}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{C}_{H}(P),\qquad\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P{:}Q)=\mathcal{C}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(Q)-\mathcal{C}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P).
``` |  |

Report the four coordinates separately. A scalar comparison uses the
stated coefficients. A positive
$`\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P{:}Q)`$ means that $`P`$
has lower weighted total cost than $`Q`$.

For a stochastic procedure, these four quantities give costs for each
realised history. When its finite-horizon execution is represented by
one fixed $`G`$ and a finite source set,
[definition B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Definition B.5 (Execution under a probability distribution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
applies to the whole procedure. Its input-delivery mechanism reveals
observations only after the actions that obtain them. The execution
counts all included work and returns the completed history. The
evaluator accepts histories meeting the fixed overall goal and required
performance levels. Failed or nonhalting runs are unsuccessful.
Averaging coordinate costs requires a specified distribution and cost
convention for all histories, with finite means where used.

Adding separate stage budgets can underestimate the budget needed for
the same reliability over the whole procedure. Suppose two independent
stages always meet their targets, each using one work unit with
probability $`0.9`$ and eleven with probability $`0.1`$. Each has a
$`90\%`$ work budget of one, so their separate budgets sum to two. Total
work is two, twelve or twenty-two with probabilities $`0.81`$, $`0.18`$
and $`0.01`$, respectively. The whole-procedure $`90\%`$ budget is
twelve, whereas expected total work is four. Neither the sum of stage
budgets nor the expected total gives the reliable budget for this
procedure. That budget must be computed from the total-work
distribution.

###### Remark B.24 (Costs of retaining a component).

Let $`P`$ and $`Q`$ be feasible plans from the same decision state. Plan
$`P`$ retains a component $`z`$, acquiring or constructing it during the
continuation if needed. Plan $`Q`$ omits $`z`$ or constructs a
substitute, and it can still learn and keep its own results. Within
$`W_{H}`$, fix a common convention that assigns each counted execution
or update operation exactly once, either to a stage contribution
$`U_{t}`$ or to the remaining work $`W_{H,\rm other}`$. Thus

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
W_{H}(P)=\sum_{t=1}^{H}U_{t}(P)+W_{H,\rm other}(P),\qquad W_{H}(Q)=\sum_{t=1}^{H}U_{t}(Q)+W_{H,\rm other}(Q).
``` |  |

Put $`\Delta_{t}(z)=U_{t}(Q)-U_{t}(P)`$. These are differences for the
given plans, not differences of independently minimised local task
costs. Define the remaining cost difference

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z)={}`$ | $`\displaystyle\mu_{\Lambda}(\Lambda_{H}(P)-\Lambda_{H}(Q))+\mu_{\rm acq}(W_{{\rm acq},H}(P)-W_{{\rm acq},H}(Q))`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\mu_{W}(W_{H,\rm other}(P)-W_{H,\rm other}(Q))+\mu_{S}(S_{H}(P)-S_{H}(Q)).`$ |  |

Subtracting the complete costs of the two plans gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P{:}Q)=\mu_{W}\sum_{t=1}^{H}\Delta_{t}(z)-C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z).
``` |  |

This convention determines how the total divides between the two terms.
When all coordinate differences in
$`C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z)`$ are nonnegative,
$`C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z)`$ is the weighted extra cost
that $`P`$ incurs outside stage task work. Otherwise it is a signed net
difference between the two plans, with some cost categories favouring
$`P`$.

For each horizon $`H`$, compare feasible plans $`P_{H}`$ and $`Q_{H}`$
under fixed conventions and finite nonnegative weights. Let
$`\overline{\Delta}_{H}=\frac{1}{H}\sum_{t=1}^{H}\Delta_{t}(z)`$ be
their average stage task-work difference. The identity gives
$`\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P_{H}{:}Q_{H})=H\mu_{W}\overline{\Delta}_{H}-C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z)`$.
For constants $`C_{\rm init}\geq 0`$, $`\kappa\geq 0`$ and
$`\epsilon>0`$ independent of $`H`$, the bounds
$`C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z)\leq C_{\rm init}+H\kappa`$ and
$`\mu_{W}\overline{\Delta}_{H}\geq\kappa+\epsilon`$ give
$`\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P_{H}{:}Q_{H})\geq H\epsilon-C_{\rm init}`$.
Reversing the bounds to
$`C_{H,\rm other}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(z)\geq C_{\rm init}+H\kappa`$ and
$`\mu_{W}\overline{\Delta}_{H}\leq\kappa-\epsilon`$ gives
$`\operatorname{Adv}_{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(P_{H}{:}Q_{H})\leq-C_{\rm init}-H\epsilon`$.

## Appendix C Statements and proofs

The results below use the definitions of
[appendix B](#A2 "Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
They cover controlled and sampled execution
([section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Reliable language-model performance ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
cache savings
([section 2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Work-reducing information ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
retained shortcuts
([section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Reducing work with a short program ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
cumulative deepening
([section 5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Cumulative deepening ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
finite search
([section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
persistence
([section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
switching
([section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Replacing components ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
organisation
([section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
evaluation
([section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Evaluating success ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp"))
and recursive improvement
([section 5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Recursive improvement ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

### C.1 Controlled and sampled execution

These constructions support
[section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Reliable language-model performance ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
the cache comparison in
[section 2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Work-reducing information ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
the runtime search in
[section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and the evaluator comparison in
[section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Evaluating success ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
In controlled generation, the task input specifies token choices,
whereas in sampled generation, success depends on the distribution of
choices and on the controller. The examples distinguish generating an
acceptable candidate from returning one.

Fix the public context, the retained state and a deterministic
finite-precision next-token implementation with its vocabulary and
finite stopping rule. At each step, list candidate tokens in decreasing
score order, breaking ties by a fixed rule. Fix $`k`$ between one and
the vocabulary size and allow only the first $`k`$ tokens at each step.
The generation procedure accepts a length-delimited sequence of
positions in $`\{1,\ldots,k\}`$, ignores entries after stopping and
declares failure if it needs an entry that is missing or outside this
set. All other execution choices are fixed by the implementation or by
one fixed realisation of the other input channels.

The fixed context and state determine the first candidate list, and the
first position determines the first token and the next state. Repeating
this argument shows that every sequence that causes no failure
determines a unique execution. Conversely, every token trace that uses
the first $`k`$ candidates through the stopping point is reproduced by
the sequence of positions chosen along it. Fixed-width binary coding of
$`L`$ positions has payload length $`L\lceil\log_{2}k\rceil`$, and a
standard length header adds $`O(\log(L+2))`$ bits. The sequence and any
accompanying instructions form part of the task input. Computing the
candidate lists, decoding the sequence and updating the state are
counted as work of the implementation.

An interface that also accepts programs can express regular sequences
more briefly, for example by a program that chooses the first candidate
at every step. The program then counts as task input and its execution
counts as work.

###### Example C.1 (Equal controlled work with different sampling reliability).

Consider a finite generation procedure with two possible one-symbol
responses, $`0`$ and $`1`$, where only $`1`$ succeeds. Fix two retained
states. In both, the ordered menu is $`(0,1)`$. This one-step interface
takes a single control bit, without a length header, to select either
token. Those controlled runs have the same fixed work $`c`$ in both
states. They therefore have the same least successful controlled work at
any input bound that admits the one-bit control. In the default-sampling
mode, with no token choice given, draw $`u`$ uniformly from
$`\{0,\ldots,9\}`$. One state returns $`1`$ exactly when $`u<1`$. The
other does so exactly when $`u<4`$. Pad every controlled or sampled
generation run to the same integer work $`c`$. Their empty-input success
probabilities at $`c`$ are $`1/10`$ and $`2/5`$, although the controlled
executions coincide.

For the reliable-work comparison, admit only the empty task input, so
$`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}=\{\varepsilon\}`$. Let a common controller make
at most $`m`$ independent attempts, using a uniform draw from
$`\{0,\ldots,9\}^{m}`$. Each complete attempt, including an exact check
and the controller’s operations, is padded to a common integer work
cost, again denoted $`c`$. The controller returns on success and
declares failure after $`m`$ unsuccessful attempts. At budget $`kc`$ for
$`1\leq k\leq m`$, a state whose per-attempt success probability is
$`\theta\in(0,1)`$ succeeds with probability $`1-(1-\theta)^{k}`$. Its
reliable work budget is therefore

|     |                                                               |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       c\left\lceil\frac{\log(1-\alpha)}{\log(1-\theta)}\right\rceil
       ```                                                            |     |

for $`0<\alpha<1`$ when the required number of attempts is at most
$`m`$, and is infinite otherwise. With $`m\geq 44`$ and required success
probability $`\alpha=0.99`$, the first state needs $`44c`$ and the
second $`10c`$. At $`\alpha=0.9`$, they need $`22c`$ and $`5c`$. Thus
equal controlled work can coexist with more than a fourfold difference
in the work needed to return a correct response at the required
reliability. The positive saving at fixed reliability concerns the
restricted input class. Attributing that saving to earlier work would
also require earlier production and persistence of the changed state
component.

###### Example C.2 (Valid code repairs).

Consider repairing a Boolean expression that returns a where
$`\max(a,b)`$ is required. Candidate repairs are always-halting
expressions in $`a,b`$. A repair is valid when it returns $`\max(a,b)`$
on all four input pairs. The expressions a or b, not (not a and not b),
a if a \>= b else b, and b if b \>= a else a all compute $`\max(a,b)`$.
The single-variable expressions a and b each pass the tests with
$`(a,b)=(0,0)`$ and $`(1,1)`$ but fail one test with unequal inputs.
Testing all four inputs is an exact verifier. The equal-input tests
alone are not.

At a common padded generation cost, let generator A assign probability
$`1/4`$ to one valid repair and the rest to an invalid repair. Let
generator B assign probability $`1/8`$ to each of the four valid repairs
and the rest to an invalid repair. B succeeds with probability $`1/2`$,
versus A’s $`1/4`$, even though each of its valid repairs is less likely
than A’s.

Suppose B’s invalid repair is a. A controller draws two independent
candidates from B and returns the first that passes its tests, or
reports failure if neither passes. A valid candidate is present with
probability $`3/4`$. Testing all four inputs returns a valid repair with
this probability. The equal-input tests accept every candidate, so the
controller returns the first, which is valid with probability $`1/2`$.

We compare the proxy and intended evaluators of
[section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Evaluating success ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
on the same returned responses, keeping any internal checking and
selection fixed.

###### Proposition C.3 (Proxy and goal success probabilities).

Fix the execution procedure $`G`$, retained state $`r`$, task input
$`p`$, source distribution $`\nu`$ and work budget $`b`$. Let
$`\widehat{S}`$ be the responses that pass the proxy test and $`S^{*}`$
those that meet the intended goal. Write $`\widehat{F}`$ and $`F^{*}`$
for their success probabilities within budget $`b`$.

Let $`e`$ be the returned response and $`W`$ the work of its execution.
The difference between the success probabilities is the probability of a
false pass minus that of a false fail within budget $`b`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{F}-F^{*}={}`$ | $`\displaystyle\Pr[W\leq b,\ e\in\widehat{S}\setminus S^{*}]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle-\Pr[W\leq b,\ e\in S^{*}\setminus\widehat{S}],`$ |  |

where both events require the run to halt and return a response. If the
proxy test is sound, so that $`\widehat{S}\subseteq S^{*}`$, there are
no false passes and $`\widehat{F}\leq F^{*}`$. Since this holds for
every admitted $`p`$ and budget $`b`$, the same task input classes
$`\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}`$ and reliability requirement $`0<\alpha\leq 1`$
give

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b_{r}^{S^{*},\nu}(\lambda;\alpha)\leq b_{r}^{\widehat{S},\nu}(\lambda;\alpha).
``` |  |

If the proxy test is complete, so that $`S^{*}\subseteq\widehat{S}`$,
there are no false fails and both inequalities are reversed.

###### Proof.

Partition the two success events into their common accepted part and
their two disjoint differences. The common probabilities cancel.
Soundness removes the first difference event and completeness the
second. The resulting probability inequality holds for every admitted
$`p`$ and budget $`b`$, so any budget qualifying for the target with the
smaller accepted set also qualifies for the other target. ∎

The key-value cache in
[section 2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Work-reducing information ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
stores intermediate results computed from a shared prompt prefix.

###### Proposition C.4 (Exact work savings from a stored cache).

Fix two matched states of one execution procedure, $`r^{+}`$ storing a
valid cache and $`r^{-}`$ recomputing it from the same stored prefix.
The subscripts $`+`$ and $`-`$ below refer to these two states. Suppose
that for every admitted $`p,u`$ they have the same response and status,
and each finite completed run satisfies $`W_{-}(p,u)=W_{+}(p,u)+C`$ for
one constant integer $`C>0`$. Cache reading and reconstruction are
included in these counts. Then, for every finite budget $`b`$, the
success probabilities satisfy

|     |                                                    |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       F_{+}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b,q)=F_{-}^{\tau,\nu}(p;b+C,q).
       ```                                                 |     |

Whenever the corresponding reliable work budgets are finite,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b_{-}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)=b_{+}^{\tau,\nu}(\lambda;q,\alpha)+C.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

For each fixed task input $`p`$ and source input $`u`$, the two runs
have the same response and status, and every completed run from
$`r^{-}`$ costs $`C`$ more than its counterpart from $`r^{+}`$.
Consequently, the run from $`r^{+}`$ returns an accepted response within
budget $`b`$ exactly when the run from $`r^{-}`$ does so within budget
$`b+C`$. Averaging their equal success indicators over the common source
distribution proves the first identity. Every successful run from
$`r^{-}`$ costs at least $`C`$. The least integer budget attaining any
fixed positive success probability therefore increases by $`C`$ for each
task input. Minimising over the same finite input class proves the
second identity. ∎

Fix one controller, an integer $`m\geq 1`$, and one admitted task input.
The controller makes up to $`m`$ attempts and returns the first that
passes its check, which accepts exactly the correct answers. Under the
````
