````math
source distribution the attempts succeed independently, each with
probability $`\theta\in(0,1)`$. From the state with valid precomputed
results stored in the cache, each complete attempt costs an integer
$`c>0`$, including generation, checking, cache access and control. The
matched state has the same underlying prefix, responses and probability
distribution, and reconstructs the identical cache at integer cost
$`C>0`$.

###### Example C.5 (Repeated attempts with a stored cache).

For a required success probability $`0<\alpha\leq 1-(1-\theta)^{m}`$,
the minimum number of attempts the budget must allow is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
k_{\alpha}=\left\lceil\frac{\log(1-\alpha)}{\log(1-\theta)}\right\rceil.
``` |  |

If the cache is reconstructed once before the first attempt, the
reliable work budgets are $`ck_{\alpha}`$ and $`ck_{\alpha}+C`$. If it
is reconstructed before every attempt, they are $`ck_{\alpha}`$ and
$`(c+C)k_{\alpha}`$, so the saving $`Ck_{\alpha}`$ is nondecreasing in
$`\alpha`$ and changes in steps. For $`\alpha>1-(1-\theta)^{m}`$ neither
budget is finite.

###### Proof.

Within budget $`b`$, up to $`\min\{m,\lfloor b/c\rfloor\}`$ attempts fit
for the state that retains the cache, so it succeeds with probability
$`1-(1-\theta)^{\min\{m,\lfloor b/c\rfloor\}}`$. This is at least
$`\alpha`$ exactly when at least $`k_{\alpha}`$ attempts fit within the
budget, so its least qualifying budget is $`ck_{\alpha}`$. One
reconstruction before the first attempt adds $`C`$ to every path without
changing responses or status, which gives $`ck_{\alpha}+C`$, as in
[proposition C.4](#A3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition C.4 (Exact work savings from a stored cache). ‣ C.1 Controlled and sampled execution ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
Reconstruction before every attempt makes each attempt cost $`c+C`$, and
the same argument gives $`(c+C)k_{\alpha}`$. At most $`m`$ attempts are
made, so no budget reaches a success probability above
$`1-(1-\theta)^{m}`$. The singleton input class leaves no further
minimisation over inputs. ∎

The runtime search discussed in
[section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
enumerates candidate inputs for one task. The controller performs the
search and checks the candidates, and both kinds of work are counted.

###### Proposition C.6 (Searching for a task input).

Fix a deterministic system in a given starting state and an available
computable acceptance test whose accepted responses meet the intended
target. There are $`N_{\lambda}=2^{\lambda+1}-1`$ binary strings of
length at most $`\lambda`$ to consider as task inputs to this system.

Fix a controller that holds the system’s description and starting state,
the test and the bounds, and that can decide which task inputs the
system admits. Suppose one complete bounded attempt, including candidate
enumeration, the admission check, reset, decoding, simulation to a fixed
primitive-step limit, verification of any returned response and output
of an accepted one, terminates and costs at most $`T`$ work. If some
admitted task input of length at most $`\lambda`$ produces an answer
that passes the test within that limit, this controller, with an empty
task input of its own, returns an accepted response within

|     |                            |     |
|-----|----------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       W_{\rm setup}+N_{\lambda}T
       ```                         |     |

work, where $`W_{\rm setup}`$ covers reading this information and
initialisation.

###### Proof.

Enumerate the finite strings and perform the bounded attempt for each,
rejecting inputs not admitted by the system. No attempt blocks the
enumeration. The successful one occurs in the list, so summing the
per-attempt bound gives the result. ∎

Each additional symbol allowed in the searched inputs roughly doubles
the number $`N_{\lambda}`$ of candidates. Levin’s universal search
\[[57](#bib.bib6)\] refines such enumeration by dividing running time
among candidates according to their length.

###### Proposition C.7 (Expected work of independent attempts).

Suppose complete attempts terminate, are independent with the same
distribution, reset to the same condition and succeed with probability
$`\theta>0`$. The work of each attempt, successful or not, includes
reset, generation, checking and control. For a successful attempt it
also includes returning the accepted response. The mean work $`\mu`$ of
an attempt is finite. An available exact checker stops the process at
its first success or after $`m`$ attempts. The success probability is
$`1-(1-\theta)^{m}`$, and the mean work of the attempts is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mu\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(1-\theta)^{j}=\mu\,\frac{1-(1-\theta)^{m}}{\theta}.
``` |  |

Any work before the first attempt is added separately. With an unlimited
independent source sequence and the same assumptions, the mean work of
the attempts to first success is $`\mu/\theta`$.

###### Proof.

The process reaches attempt $`j+1`$ exactly when the first $`j`$
attempts failed, an event of probability $`(1-\theta)^{j}`$. The cost of
attempt $`j+1`$ is independent of those preceding outcomes and has mean
$`\mu`$, even if its cost is correlated with its own success. Summing
expected costs over the attempted stages gives $`\mu`$ times the
expected number of attempts, as in Wald’s identity. The all-fail event
has probability $`(1-\theta)^{m}`$. For the unlimited process, monotone
convergence gives the limit of the nonnegative cost sum, and the
geometric series has sum $`1/\theta`$. The unlimited case goes beyond
the finite source sets of
[definition B.5](#A2.Thmtheorem5 "Definition B.5 (Execution under a probability distribution). ‣ B.2 Reliable execution ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and uses the infinite product of independent draws. ∎

Here total work counts the actual work of unsuccessful runs, whereas
$`X_{r}^{\tau,q}`$ assigns them infinity. With unlimited attempts and
$`\theta=1/4`$, successful attempts costing four units and unsuccessful
ones costing ten give mean work per attempt $`\mu=17/2`$ and mean total
work $`34`$. Assigning every attempt the work of a successful one would
give the incorrect value $`16`$. Expected work is finite although no
finite work bound covers all runs.

### C.2 Reducing work through retained information

The first construction gives the work saving from a given retained
routine
([section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Reducing work with a short program ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
The next constructs a cumulative history in which intermediate results
are kept
([section 5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Cumulative deepening ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
Finite search then selects a routine by the work it requires on a set of
training tasks
([section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

#### C.2.1 Reducing work with a short program

We use the cells $`a_{i}(t)`$ and the additive rule
$`a_{i}(t+1)=a_{i}(t)\oplus a_{i+1}(t)`$ of
[section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Reducing work with a short program ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
with its standard power-of-two identity \[[67](#bib.bib76)\]. The two
retained states, $`r^{-}`$ and $`r^{+}`$ below ($`r_{\mathrm{sim}}`$ and
$`r_{\mathrm{short}}`$ in
[section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Reducing work with a short program ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp")),
store the direct simulator and the shortcut.

###### Proposition C.8 (A shortcut for an additive cellular automaton).

Let $`T=2^{n}`$. For every binary window $`x=a_{0}(0)\cdots a_{T}(0)`$
given as public context, let $`\tau_{n,x}`$ require response
$`a_{0}(T)`$ from a serial tape initially positioned at $`a_{0}(0)`$ and
terminated after $`a_{T}(0)`$ by a fixed end marker. There is a
transition-complete system $`g_{\rm CA}`$
([definition A.3](#A1.Thmtheorem3 "Definition A.3 (Transition-complete systems). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"))
with matched states $`r^{-},r^{+}`$ differing only in a fixed-width
method field that encodes one of two fixed finite routines, interpreted
by the common finite control. State $`r^{-}`$ encodes the direct
one-step simulation routine and $`r^{+}`$ encodes one fixed power-of-two
shortcut routine. Only the empty task input succeeds, and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{g_{\rm CA},r^{-}}^{\tau_{n,x},1}(0)=\Theta(T^{2}),\qquad w_{g_{\rm CA},r^{+}}^{\tau_{n,x},1}(0)=\Theta(T),
``` |  |

while the shortcut description is bounded independently of $`n`$. Hence

|     |                                                                |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Delta_{\rm work}(r^{+}{:}r^{-};\tau_{n,x},1,0)=\Theta(T^{2}).
       ```                                                             |     |

###### Proof.

The rule gives $`a_{i}(1)=a_{i}(0)\oplus a_{i+1}(0)`$ for every initial
row and cell $`i`$. If $`a_{i}(t)=a_{i}(0)\oplus a_{i+t}(0)`$ for every
row and cell at $`t=2^{k}`$, applying the same identity to the row at
time $`t`$ gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle a_{i}(2t)`$ | $`\displaystyle=a_{i}(t)\oplus a_{i+t}(t)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=a_{i}(0)\oplus a_{i+t}(0)\oplus a_{i+t}(0)\oplus a_{i+2t}(0)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=a_{i}(0)\oplus a_{i+2t}(0),`$ |  |

since the two copies of $`a_{i+t}(0)`$ cancel. By induction,
$`a_{0}(T)=a_{0}(0)\oplus a_{T}(0)`$.

The direct implementation updates the triangular dependency cone,
executing a constant-work update at each site of the cone. Its work is
bounded above and below by constant multiples of
$`1+\cdots+T=\Theta(T^{2})`$. The shortcut stores $`a_{0}(0)`$, scans
the serial window to its fixed end marker while retaining the last bit
$`a_{T}(0)`$, adds the two endpoint bits modulo two and emits the
result. Its finite control is independent of $`n`$, and the serial scan
is both $`O(T)`$ and $`\Omega(T)`$. Because only the empty task input
can succeed and each state selects one deterministic path, these path
counts are the least-work values. ∎

Within this system, a fixed-size routine saves unbounded work as the
horizon grows, without a growing literal answer table. This is a
comparison of two specified methods. Discovering the routine remains a
separate acquisition task. In the following example, the stored
component is instead produced by the earlier tasks of the history.

#### C.2.2 Cumulative deepening

A history shows cumulative deepening when, along the compared stages,
the target depths from the original persistent condition are finite and
strictly increasing at a fixed margin, and inherited products of earlier
work give a positive matched work saving at each stage
([section 5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Cumulative deepening ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
Each saving compares finite least-work values at the same input bound,
with the inherited products kept available across stages instead of
being given again as task input.

Fix $`N\geq 2`$, a binary word $`x_{1}\cdots x_{N}`$, and a serial
streaming interface. Task $`\tau_{n}`$, for $`1\leq n\leq N`$, has
public context $`x^{(n)}=x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\#`$ and accepts the one-bit
parity of that prefix. The growing prefix is public task context, so the
baseline from $`r_{0}`$ sees the same current prefix as the inherited
state. Treating it as persistent external state would instead compare
against its original value under
[section 5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Cumulative deepening ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
The same $`x^{(n)}`$ is used in both variants of every comparison for
task $`n`$.

One fixed deterministic process $`g`$ has a one-bit accumulator and an
actual input-head position in its retained configuration. A bit
transition xors the scanned bit into the accumulator and advances the
head one cell. At $`\#`$, one transition emits the accumulator and
halts, leaving the head at the marker. These are the counted primitive
transitions. The only admitted task input is $`\varepsilon`$. Fix a tape
capacity of at least $`N+1`$ before the history. Valid configurations
include every head position in that capacity and either accumulator
value. The original state $`r_{0}`$ has accumulator zero and head at the
first cell.

Between successive tasks, a common external rule replaces the old marker
by the next bit and places a new marker one cell later, without moving
the retained head. The public tape is physically continuous across the
history, but its current contents specify $`x^{(n)}`$ in the task
protocol. A serial-tape system retaining only a numerical cursor must
count the work of moving its head to that position. A different input
interface must account for any loading or copying it requires. Here the
external extension is outside the counted process and is common to both
variants.

###### Example C.9 (Computing successive prefix parities).

Let $`r_{n-1}`$ be the state left by successful task $`n-1`$. For
$`n\geq 2`$, replace its head position and accumulator by their original
values, keeping $`x^{(n)}`$ and all other conditions fixed, which gives
$`r_{0}`$. For every $`\lambda\geq 0`$ and every margin $`s\geq 0`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle D_{n}=d_{g,r_{0}}^{\tau_{n},1}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle=n+1,`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle w_{g,r_{n-1}}^{\tau_{n},1}(\lambda)`$ | $`\displaystyle=2,`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle w_{g,r_{0}}^{\tau_{n},1}(\lambda)-w_{g,r_{n-1}}^{\tau_{n},1}(\lambda)`$ | $`\displaystyle=n-1>0.`$ |  |

Thus the actual history satisfies the cumulative-deepening criterion on
$`2,\ldots,N`$.

###### Proof.

After task $`n-1`$, the accumulator is the parity of
$`x_{1}\cdots x_{n-1}`$ and the head is at position $`n`$. This holds
after the first task and is preserved by one bit transition and the
end-marker transition on every extension. Hence each inherited-state
execution takes two transitions and returns the correct next parity.
From $`r_{0}`$ the same task instead executes $`n`$ bit transitions and
one end-marker transition, giving $`n+1`$. Replacing them restores this
path.

The only admitted task input is empty, so each successful path gives the
exact least work for every input bound. The empty input also lies within
every input bound used for depth, giving the stated finite depth at
every margin. Each earlier successful task produces and retains the head
position and accumulator used next. It is not given again as counted
input. The positive matched savings concern the same targets whose
depths from the original starting condition strictly increase. These are
the two conditions for cumulative deepening in
[section 5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Cumulative deepening ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
The construction works for every chosen finite $`N`$ with that capacity.
∎

#### C.2.3 Finite search for work-reducing routines

The cellular-automaton example in
[proposition C.8](#A3.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition C.8 (A shortcut for an additive cellular automaton). ‣ C.2.1 Reducing work with a short program ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
starts with the useful routine already retained. For the search
described in
[section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
an acquisition process instead enumerates a finite class of candidate
strings, executes them and counts their later task work under one fixed
protocol, then emits a state containing a minimiser.

Fix a finite family of binary tasks $`(\tau_{i})_{i=1}^{m}`$, with
public contexts tagged by the task index, nonempty decidable accepted
sets and target level one, and fix $`L\in\mathbb{N}`$.

###### Proposition C.10 (Finite search for work-reducing routines).

There exist a fixed transition-complete deterministic system
$`g_{\rm s}`$ for performing these tasks and a fixed deterministic
acquisition system $`A_{\rm s}`$ for producing its states, with the
following properties. The valid states of $`g_{\rm s}`$ include

|     |                                 |     |
|-----|---------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       r_{z}=(\rho,z),\qquad|z|\leq L,
       ```                              |     |

for one fixed nonvaried field $`\rho`$, with $`r_{\varepsilon}`$ the
baseline state. The comparison includes the complete encoding of the
candidate field, and reading and decoding it count in the work of the
run. On each task, $`r_{\varepsilon}`$ runs a fixed exhaustive baseline
directly and halts with an accepted response. For $`z\neq\varepsilon`$,
$`g_{\rm s}`$ runs the procedure encoded by $`z`$, alternating its steps
with those of the same exhaustive baseline. If the candidate halts, the
fixed acceptance test runs to completion. If accepted, that response is
returned. Otherwise the baseline resumes. The baseline therefore
guarantees termination for every candidate. Executing both procedures,
switching between them and checking the response are counted by the same
work rule.

Evaluate every state at input bound zero and write its total work across
the tasks as

|     |                                                         |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       W(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{g_{\rm s},r_{z}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0).
       ```                                                      |     |

The acquisition system exhaustively evaluates the finite set
$`\{z:|z|\leq L\}`$, uses a fixed tie-breaking rule, and emits a state
$`r_{z^{*}}`$ with

|     |                                   |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       z^{*}\in\arg\min_{|z|\leq L}W(z).
       ```                                |     |

This acquisition halts after finite work, and

|     |                              |     |
|-----|------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       W(z^{*})\leq W(\varepsilon).
       ```                           |     |

If the inequality is strict, then for at least one $`i`$,

|     |                                                                |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Delta_{\rm work}(r_{z^{*}}{:}r_{\varepsilon};\tau_{i},1,0)>0,
       ```                                                             |     |

so the selected state difference is work-reducing information for at
least one task in the evaluated family.

###### Proof.

Because the family is finite and each accepted set is decidable and
nonempty, a fixed baseline for the family can enumerate finite response
strings in a computable order and stop at the first accepted response.
Hard-code the finite task tags and their deciders into one fixed
baseline branch of $`g_{\rm s}`$. For a nonempty candidate field $`z`$,
a fixed program reads and executes the procedure encoded by $`z`$ on the
tagged public context. A syntactically invalid code disables that
candidate branch. The system alternates candidate and baseline
transitions. If the candidate halts, its terminating acceptance test is
completed and the response is returned only if accepted. Otherwise the
baseline resumes. The decider is total, so this check cannot prevent
eventual baseline progress. Hence every state–task run terminates, and
all transitions used to run the candidate, run the baseline, switch
between them and check responses are included in the transition-complete
work count.

At input bound zero the empty string is the only admitted task input.
Thus, for every $`z`$ and $`i`$, the work of this unique deterministic
successful run is exactly $`w_{g_{\rm s},r_{z}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)`$. There
are finitely many strings with $`|z|\leq L`$ and finitely many tasks, so
$`A_{\rm s}`$ can simulate every such run to completion, count its work,
compute each finite sum $`W(z)`$, and emit a minimiser under the fixed
tie-breaking rule. This uses finite acquisition work.

Since $`\varepsilon`$ is among the candidate fields, minimality gives
$`W(z^{*})\leq W(\varepsilon)`$. If the inequality is strict, not every
summand can be at least its baseline counterpart. Hence for some $`i`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{g_{\rm s},r_{z^{*}}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)<w_{g_{\rm s},r_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0),
``` |  |

which is exactly the stated positive work difference between matched
retained states under
[definitions B.8](#A2.Thmtheorem8 "Definition B.8 (Matched component replacement). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.11](#A2.Thmtheorem11 "Definition B.11 (Work-reducing information). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
∎

Minimising total work across the training tasks can increase work on
individual tasks. To prevent this, restrict the same finite enumeration
to candidates satisfying
$`w_{g_{\rm s},r_{z}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)\leq w_{g_{\rm s},r_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{i},1}(0)`$
for every $`i`$. The feasible set contains the baseline
$`z=\varepsilon`$. At input bound zero, each state has one run on each
task, and that run returns an accepted response. Its work is therefore
the least work for both binary levels, so
[corollary B.13](#A2.Thmtheorem13 "Corollary B.13 (Least-work comparisons imply grasp dominance). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
gives weak grasp dominance at this input bound on every tested task.
Neither version establishes optimality outside the bounded
retained-state class or the evaluated task family.

For the length-penalised search in
[section 2.6](#S2.SS6 "2.6 Finite search for work-reducing routines ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
extend the same construction to all finite candidate fields and fix a
rational $`\gamma>0`$. Every candidate still terminates through the
baseline. Since work is nonnegative, any $`z`$ with
$`|z|>W(\varepsilon)/\gamma`$ has $`W(z)+\gamma|z|>W(\varepsilon)`$. The
acquisition procedure first computes $`W(\varepsilon)`$, then evaluates
all codes of length at most $`\lfloor W(\varepsilon)/\gamma\rfloor`$. A
minimiser $`z^{*}`$ over this finite set is a global minimiser of the
penalised objective, and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
W(z^{*})+\gamma|z^{*}|\leq W(\varepsilon),\qquad W(\varepsilon)-W(z^{*})\geq\gamma|z^{*}|.
``` |  |

The conclusion concerns this fixed execution procedure and the training
tasks.

### C.3 Preserving capability

The example with shared storage and participant memory shows how a
system can retain a capability while its participants are replaced
([examples C.11](#A3.Thmtheorem11 "Example C.11 (Maintaining capability through turnover). ‣ C.3.1 Maintaining capability ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
The switching results then bound the length of a replacement checkpoint,
and the work of producing it, when the checkpoint must preserve
functions that later work depends on, as discussed in
[section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Replacing components ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

#### C.3.1 Maintaining capability

Fix $`k\geq 1`$ and $`z\in\{0,1\}^{k}`$. There is a fixed
transition-complete system $`g_{k}`$ whose valid states are pairs
$`r=(R,I)`$, where each slot is either blank ($`\bot`$) or a $`k`$-bit
word and, when both are nonblank, they agree. The same system stores and
emits whichever $`k`$-bit word its state contains, so $`z`$ is not
hard-coded in $`g_{k}`$. The persistent slot $`R`$ represents shared
storage, and the slot $`I`$ represents the current participant’s memory.
Only the empty task input can succeed.

If both slots are nonblank, $`g_{k}`$ emits $`I`$. If exactly one slot
is nonblank, it copies that word into the missing slot and emits it. If
both are blank it fails. For a fixed constant $`c`$, choose the
implementation so serial emission costs $`k+c`$ work and a copy of one
missing slot contributes exactly $`k`$ additional work. For
$`\tau_{z}=(\varepsilon,\{z\})`$, define the states with both copies,
only the shared copy, only the participant’s copy and no copy,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
r_{11}=(z,z),\quad r_{10}=(z,\bot),\quad r_{01}=(\bot,z),\quad r_{00}=(\bot,\bot).
``` |  |

Define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{Turn}(R,I)=(R,\bot),\qquad\operatorname{Del}(R,I)=(\bot,I).
``` |  |

Thus turnover of the participant gives
$`\operatorname{Turn}(r_{11})=r_{10}`$, deletion of the shared copy
gives $`\operatorname{Del}(r_{11})=r_{01}`$, and loss of both copies
gives $`r_{00}`$.

###### Example C.11 (Maintaining capability through turnover).

For the system $`g_{k}`$ and task $`\tau_{z}`$ above,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{g_{k},r_{11}}^{\tau_{z},1}(0)=k+c,\qquad w_{g_{k},r_{10}}^{\tau_{z},1}(0)=w_{g_{k},r_{01}}^{\tau_{z},1}(0)=2k+c,
``` |  |

while level one is unattainable from $`r_{00}`$ at input bound zero. A
successful run from either one-copy state reconstructs $`r_{11}`$.
Repeated run–turnover cycles preserve the target capability across
arbitrarily many participant replacements, provided each post-turnover
run is allowed at least $`2k+c`$ work. The $`k`$-work gap is
implementation-relative.

###### Proof.

From $`r_{11}`$ the deterministic successful path only emits the $`k`$
stored bits, so its work is $`k+c`$. From either one-copy state it first
performs exactly $`k`$ copy transitions and then the same emission,
giving $`2k+c`$. From $`r_{00}`$ there is no word to emit and the run
fails. Only the empty string is admitted at input bound zero, so these
path counts are the least-work values. A run from either one-copy state
fills the blank slot and therefore leaves the terminal state $`r_{11}`$.
The turnover and deletion identities are immediate. Induction over
run–turnover cycles gives persistence through arbitrarily many
participant replacements. ∎

The example makes explicit the point in
[section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
that a budget must also cover the work of maintenance. For this process,
a budget $`k+c\leq w<2k+c`$ permits a response from $`r_{11}`$ but not
after either copy is lost. In the restoring procedure, with at least
$`2k+c`$ work per run, one remaining copy allows the system to answer
and restore the missing copy, so the capability persists under
$`\{\operatorname{Turn},\operatorname{Del}\}`$ in the sense of
[section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Maintaining capability ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
with $`\mathcal{S}=\{r_{11}\}`$.

Under turnover alone restoration is not needed. A variant that emits the
shared copy without restoring the participant’s copy uses $`k+c`$ work
after turnover and persists under $`\{\operatorname{Turn}\}`$ with
$`\mathcal{S}=\{r_{11},r_{10}\}`$, but a later deletion of the shared
copy then leaves $`r_{00}`$.

With one disturbance between completed runs, the original procedure uses
an extra $`k`$ work to restore the lost copy after either turnover or
deletion. Both copies are then available before the next disturbance.
Each copy saves $`k`$ work given the other,
$`\Delta_{\rm work}(r_{11}{:}r_{10};\tau_{z},1,0)=\Delta_{\rm work}(r_{11}{:}r_{01};\tau_{z},1,0)=k`$,
while losing both copies removes attainability.

More generally, removing a component without changing performance does
not by itself locate the capability. Another stored copy or a different
procedure may preserve performance.

#### C.3.2 Replacing components

In the preceding example, shared storage and participant memory preserve
capability within one system. Here useful structure can remain
externally accessible, and switching changes what is still available to
the replacement. The replacement may use another implementation or an
adapter. The bounds below concern the required responses it must give,
whatever method produces them.

The service below returns one bit in response to each required query.
Its replacement checkpoint encodes the whole retained state of the
replacement, including any program and stored data. The checkpoint must
give those bits using only the resources that remain available after
switching. We first bound the checkpoint’s encoded length, then use the
work required to emit it to bound the migration work.

Fix one transition-complete finite-description system $`g_{\rm port}`$
whose valid checkpoints have the form $`r=\langle f,\sigma\rangle`$,
where $`f\in\{A,B\}`$ names the architecture and $`\sigma`$ is the rest
of the checkpoint. For $`T\geq 1`$, let
$`R=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{m}\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,T\}`$ be the indices of
later queries that are actually required, and let
$`z_{R}=z_{1}\cdots z_{m}\in\{0,1\}^{m}`$ be their required response
table, where $`z_{j}`$ is the bit required by query $`i_{j}`$. For each
finite persistent external state $`y`$, assume the query contexts
$`x_{i}(T,R,y)`$ are uniformly computable from $`(T,R,y)`$, and let the
work bound $`W_{T}`$ for each query be computable from $`T`$. Only the
empty task input is used in these service tests. In the notation of
[section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Replacing components ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
the required responses are
$`\Phi=\{(x_{i_{j}}(T,R,y),z_{j}):j\leq m\}`$, with input bounds zero
and work bounds $`W_{T}`$. Here $`T`$ counts possible queries and
$`m=|R|`$ counts required queries. The main-text case requiring every
response has $`m=T`$.

For $`f\in\{A,B\}`$ define the function-preserving checkpoint set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
S_{f}(T,R,z_{R};y)=\Bigl\{\langle f,\sigma\rangle\in\mathcal{R}_{g_{\rm port}}:\begin{array}[]{l}g_{\rm port}(\langle f,\sigma\rangle;\langle x_{i_{j}}(T,R,y),\varepsilon\rangle)=z_{j},\\
\operatorname{Work}_{g_{\rm port}}(\langle f,\sigma\rangle;\langle x_{i_{j}}(T,R,y),\varepsilon\rangle)\leq W_{T}\quad\text{for every }j\leq m\end{array}\Bigr\}.
``` |  |

Membership in $`S_{f}`$ is defined using $`z_{R}`$. The system receives
no separate query-input field containing $`z_{R}`$. It can obtain the
table through the checkpoint or persistent external state. The queries
and their work bound are reconstructible from $`(T,R,y)`$ without an
additional $`z_{R}`$ input.

###### Lemma C.12 (Checkpoint length needed to preserve the required responses).

There is a fixed constant $`c`$ such that every
$`r_{B}\in S_{B}(T,R,z_{R};y_{B})`$ satisfies

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       |r_{B}|\geq\mathsf{C}(z_{R}\mid T,R,y_{B})-c\log(T+2)-c.
       ```                                                       |     |

###### Proof.

Given $`(T,R,y_{B})`$ and a literal $`r_{B}`$, one fixed program on
$`U_{0}`$ reconstructs $`W_{T}`$ and the contexts
$`x_{i_{j}}(T,R,y_{B})`$, simulates $`g_{\rm port}`$ for the bounded
runs, and collects the emitted bits in increasing index order. If
$`r_{B}\in S_{B}(T,R,z_{R};y_{B})`$, the recovered string is exactly
$`z_{R}`$. Hence

|     |                                                                |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathsf{C}(z_{R}\mid T,R,y_{B})\leq|r_{B}|+O(\log(|r_{B}|+2)).
       ```                                                             |     |

Since $`\mathsf{C}(z_{R}\mid T,R,y_{B})\leq m+O(1)\leq T+O(1)`$, either
$`|r_{B}|\geq T`$, in which case the claimed inequality is immediate
after adjusting the fixed constant, or $`\log(|r_{B}|+2)=O(\log(T+2))`$.
Rearranging gives the result. ∎

The bound therefore also covers any decoder, emulator or adapter in the
checkpoint. A small checkpoint is possible only if the required response
table has a sufficiently short description given $`T`$, $`R`$ and the
post-switch external state. In the construction of
[theorem C.13](#A3.Thmtheorem13 "Theorem C.13 (Switching cost when required responses must be preserved). ‣ C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
keeping the table externally available permits a constant-size
checkpoint that routes queries to it.

The next theorem compares continuing with external access, making a
direct switch, and switching while preserving the required service. The
representation bound of
[lemma C.12](#A3.Thmtheorem12 "Lemma C.12 (Checkpoint length needed to preserve the required responses). ‣ C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
gives a lower bound on the work required for a switch that preserves the
service. A copying procedure shows that such a switch is feasible and
gives an upper bound. Constant-work continuation also requires an input
format that allows the selected mode to be read without scanning
irrelevant external data. The proof specifies that format.

###### Theorem C.13 (Switching cost when required responses must be preserved).

There are a fixed system $`g_{\rm port}`$ of the above form, a fixed
transition-complete acquisition system $`M`$, uniformly computable query
contexts, and a computable service-work bound $`W_{T}`$ with the
following property. Consider any instance $`(T,R,z_{R},y_{A},y_{B})`$ of
the construction in which $`y_{A}`$ begins with the $`T`$-bit indicator
string of $`R`$, followed by the bits of $`z_{R}`$ in increasing index
order. The construction uses one fixed constant-size A-routing
checkpoint that reads this format, answers each required query by lookup
and belongs to $`S_{A}(T,R,z_{R};y_{A})`$. Suppose the developed
acquisition state $`a_{A}`$ contains the realised $`T`$-bit table of
responses under architecture A in the serial representation of
[appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"), with the
entries at the indices in $`R`$ forming $`z_{R}`$. Then the three
ordinary binary acquisition tasks from $`a_{A}`$, namely
function-preserving A continuation $`\tau_{A}^{\rm cont}`$, a direct B
switch $`\tau_{B}^{\rm bare}`$, and a function-preserving B switch
$`\tau_{B}^{\rm pres}`$, satisfy

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{M,a_{A}}^{\tau_{A}^{\rm cont},1}(0)=O(1),\qquad w_{M,a_{A}}^{\tau_{B}^{\rm bare},1}(0)=O(1),
``` |  |

and, for every finite input bound $`\lambda`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{M,a_{A}}^{\tau_{B}^{\rm pres},1}(\lambda)\geq\mathsf{C}(z_{R}\mid T,R,y_{B})-O(\log(T+2)).
``` |  |

A fixed migration routine also achieves $`\tau_{B}^{\rm pres}`$ in
$`O(T)`$ work at input bound zero. Consequently, when
$`R=\{1,\ldots,T\}`$ and

|     |                                             |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathsf{C}(z_{R}\mid T,R,y_{B})\geq T-O(1),
       ```                                          |     |

function-preserving continuation under A has $`O(1)`$ acquisition work
whereas function-preserving switching to B has $`\Theta(T)`$ acquisition
work. The direct B switch takes $`O(1)`$ work. The preserving-switch
lower bound and copying upper bound do not require the assumption about
$`y_{A}`$. From the decision state before development, adopting A or B
takes the same $`O(1)`$ work. If $`y_{B}`$ contains the same required
indicator string and response bits in the external-routing format, a
fixed constant-size B-routing checkpoint belongs to
$`S_{B}(T,R,z_{R};y_{B})`$.

###### Proof.

Implement $`g_{\rm port}`$ with a fixed interpreter, a program that
reads the checkpoint and executes its instructions. Give checkpoints
decidable syntax and external-routing, literal-table and general-program
modes in both architectures $`f=A`$ and $`f=B`$. A literal checkpoint
stores $`T`$, a string of $`T`$ bits whose $`i`$th bit is $`1`$ exactly
when $`i\in R`$, and the $`m`$ required response bits in increasing
order of their indices. Its query routine reads the requested index and
scans the string identifying $`R`$, advancing through the stored
responses whenever it encounters a $`1`$, until it reaches the requested
response. Use input encodings in which the service input starts with
$`(T,i)`$, followed by the $`(R,y)`$ fields, with length metadata for
unused suffix fields placed after them. The literal lookup reads the
stored table without inspecting $`y`$. For the external-routing
instance, $`y_{A}`$ begins with the same string identifying $`R`$,
followed by the required response bits, so that routine performs the
corresponding lookup there. The routine leaves other trailing external
data unread. A fixed multitape implementation can parse these $`O(T+1)`$
relevant symbols and perform the scans with binary counters within
$`W_{T}=K(T+1)^{3}`$ transitions for a sufficiently large fixed
computable integer $`K`$. Choose this bound once for both routines. No
such guarantee is required for arbitrary program-mode checkpoints.

Begin each acquisition task’s public context with a fixed-length tag
selecting continuation, direct switching or preserving switching,
followed by the relevant $`(T,R,y_{A},y_{B})`$ fields. Their accepted
sets are respectively $`S_{A}(T,R,z_{R};y_{A})`$, all valid B
checkpoints, and $`S_{B}(T,R,z_{R};y_{B})`$. On the continuation tag,
$`M`$ emits the fixed A-routing checkpoint, which succeeds with
$`y_{A}`$ by hypothesis. On the direct-switch tag it emits a fixed
constant-size empty B checkpoint. Neither branch needs to scan the
unused context suffix, so both use bounded work and an empty task input.

For preserving switching, every successful run emits some
$`e\in S_{B}(T,R,z_{R};y_{B})`$. By
[lemma C.12](#A3.Thmtheorem12 "Lemma C.12 (Checkpoint length needed to preserve the required responses). ‣ C.3.2 Replacing components ‣ C.3 Preserving capability ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),

|     |                                                      |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       |e|\geq\mathsf{C}(z_{R}\mid T,R,y_{B})-O(\log(T+2)).
       ```                                                   |     |

Transition-completeness gives $`|e|\leq\operatorname{Work}_{M}`$ for
every successful run, so the same lower bound holds for least work at
every finite input bound.

For the upper bound, the developed state $`a_{A}`$ stores the realised
table under architecture A in serial layout. A fixed migration branch
scans that table and the $`T`$-bit string identifying $`R`$, emits that
string with the B literal-mode header, and copies the selected response
bits in order. These scans and output take $`O(T)`$ work at input bound
zero. The lookup of the resulting checkpoint returns every $`z_{j}`$ at
$`i_{j}`$ within the chosen $`W_{T}`$, independently of $`y_{B}`$. The
checkpoint therefore belongs to $`S_{B}(T,R,z_{R};y_{B})`$, so the
migration succeeds. The incompressible case where every response is
required combines this upper bound with the representation lower bound.

For the initial-choice comparison, add two symmetric initial public tags
at the undeveloped decision state. They emit constant-size A- and
B-routing checkpoints with their paths padded to the same fixed cost.
The subsequent construction is unchanged by exchanging the branch names.
These initial choices precede the development history, whose work
belongs to acquisition. ∎

The least-work values of $`\tau_{B}^{\rm bare}`$ and
$`\tau_{B}^{\rm pres}`$ are $`w_{\rm direct}`$ and $`w_{\rm preserve}`$
of
[section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Replacing components ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
When continuation is the chosen baseline, $`w_{\rm comparator}`$ is the
least work for $`\tau_{A}^{\rm cont}`$. The theorem compares
alternatives after the history under architecture A. Continuing under A
and switching to B are different acquisition tasks, not two states in a
matched WRI comparison. The initial choice can have equal cost on both
sides. Under the same later inputs, either choice can lead to analogous
functions that later work depends on. After development under A,
preservation is costly when less required information remains accessible
under B. An adapter that preserves external access can make the
representation bound small. Keeping that resource available still incurs
horizon costs
\[[definition B.23](#A2.Thmtheorem23 "Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

#### C.3.3 Short descriptions under query work limits

The preceding bound uses description complexity without limiting the
work needed to reconstruct the output. As discussed in
[section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Replacing components ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
reconstructing the responses from a short description can take too much
work to meet a query limit. The construction below uses the same
distinction as classical time-bounded incompressibility results
\[[21](#bib.bib51), [60](#bib.bib2)\] and imposes the limit on each
required response. With $`T`$ one-bit queries and empty external state,
B checkpoints shorter than $`\lfloor T/2\rfloor`$ bits are fewer than
the $`2^{T}`$ possible response tables, so some table is not returned by
any of them within the per-query work limit. A fixed program finds the
first such table from $`T`$ by finite search, so the table has a
description of constant length given $`T`$.

###### Theorem C.14 (Short descriptions with costly function-preserving replacement).

Under the efficient binary encodings of
[appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"), there
exist a fixed transition-complete interpreter with A and B checkpoint
modes, a fixed transition-complete migration system $`M`$, a computable
polynomial service-work bound $`B_{T}`$, and a uniformly computable
family $`z_{T}\in\{0,1\}^{T}`$ such that

|     |                               |     |
|-----|-------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathsf{C}(z_{T}\mid T)=O(1).
       ```                            |     |

The service contexts give only $`T`$, the query index and the external
state. Each service query admits only the empty task input and starts
from the same checkpoint, so updates made during one query are not
available to later queries. With external state $`y_{A}=z_{T}`$, one
constant-size A-routing checkpoint answers all $`T`$ service queries
within $`B_{T}`$. With $`y_{B}=\varepsilon`$, every valid B checkpoint
that answers those same queries within $`B_{T}`$ has length at least
$`\lfloor T/2\rfloor`$. The B interpreter admits literal tables and
arbitrary programs.

From a developed migration state $`a_{T}`$ containing $`z_{T}`$, there
are ordinary binary acquisition tasks $`\tau_{A}^{\rm cont}`$,
$`\tau_{B}^{\rm bare}`$ and $`\tau_{B}^{\rm pres}`$ for A continuation,
direct B switching, and function-preserving B switching such that

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{M,a_{T}}^{\tau_{A}^{\rm cont},1}(0)=O(1),\qquad w_{M,a_{T}}^{\tau_{B}^{\rm bare},1}(0)=O(1),
``` |  |

whereas, for every finite migration input bound $`\lambda`$,

|     |                                                         |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       w_{M,a_{T}}^{\tau_{B}^{\rm pres},1}(\lambda)=\Theta(T).
       ```                                                      |     |

###### Proof.

Fix a serial interpreter with decidable checkpoint syntax whose valid B
checkpoints include a literal-table mode and a general program mode. On
query $`i`$ with size $`T`$, the literal mode reads its stored table and
returns its $`i`$th bit. Program mode executes its stored program on
$`(T,i)`$. An A-routing mode reads the corresponding bit of external
state. All transitions are counted. Choose a computable polynomial
$`B_{T}`$ bounding the work of every literal-table and A-routing query
under these fixed encodings. This choice is made before selecting
$`z_{T}`$.

Put $`k_{T}=\lfloor T/2\rfloor`$. Enumerate every valid B checkpoint
whose *complete encoded length* is less than $`k_{T}`$. There are at
most $`2^{k_{T}}-1`$ such binary strings. For each, simulate all $`T`$
queries for at most $`B_{T}`$ transitions per query. Keep the resulting
$`T`$-bit response table only when every query halts within the limit
with a single-bit response. Each deterministic checkpoint contributes at
most one table. Since $`2^{k_{T}}-1<2^{T}`$, at least one $`T`$-bit
table is absent. Define $`z_{T}`$ as the lexicographically first absent
table.

The finite checkpoint enumeration, bounded simulations, and
missing-table selection are uniformly computable from $`T`$. One fixed
program on $`U_{0}`$ therefore produces $`z_{T}`$, proving
$`\mathsf{C}(z_{T}\mid T)=O(1)`$. By construction, no B checkpoint
shorter than $`k_{T}`$ gives all its responses within $`B_{T}`$. The
bound covers short generators and compatibility programs because program
mode includes them.

Let $`M`$ receive a public tag selecting the acquisition task. From
$`a_{T}`$, its continuation branch emits the fixed A-routing checkpoint,
and its direct-switch branch emits a fixed empty B checkpoint. Both use
constant work and an empty task input. Its preserving branch can scan
the table in $`a_{T}`$ and emit a literal B checkpoint in $`O(T)`$ work
under the serial encoding convention. That checkpoint meets every
service bound by the choice of $`B_{T}`$. Any successful migration that
preserves the required service, irrespective of the task input, must
emit a B checkpoint of length at least $`k_{T}`$. Transition
completeness makes its work at least its output length. This gives the
uniform $`\Omega(T)`$ lower bound. The empty-input copy branch gives the
upper bound for every $`\lambda`$. ∎

The B checkpoint that runs the short generator of $`z_{T}`$ has constant
length, so once $`T`$ is large it needs more than $`B_{T}`$ work on some
query. The work of initially computing $`z_{T}`$ belongs to acquisition.
Migration begins with that table already stored. Continued external
access and a sufficiently large replacement checkpoint meet the original
service limit. With enough additional work per query, the generator
becomes usable too. The work limits and the resources that survive the
switch determine which alternatives are feasible, while
[definition B.23](#A2.Thmtheorem23 "Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
compares their complete costs over a finite horizon.

### C.4 Comparing organisational arrangements

The reporting example works through the organisational comparisons of
[section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Comparing organisational arrangements ‣ 4 Persistence and organisation ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
We then bound the trials needed to assign workers with different
capabilities to suitable roles.

###### Example C.15 (Establishing reporting roles).

Two components run the same controller. On each task one receives a bit
$`u`$ and the other a bit $`v`$ across the common outer boundary, and
only the empty task input is admitted. Either component may report, and
a response is accepted when it contains at least one report and every
report equals $`u\oplus v`$. The controller cannot use component names
or identifiers. Each component has a persistent role field, initially
unset, that can hold sender or reporter. In each communication round
both components emit their messages before either receives them.
Delivery is reliable and a component waiting for a message performs no
operations. The next task begins after both have finished. Since the
schedule treats the components alike, deterministic steps on equal task
bits keep identical states identical, so complementary roles can be
chosen only from different task bits or from random choices
\[[2](#bib.bib104), [47](#bib.bib105)\].

Each executed instruction counts as one unit of work, and sending,
delivering and receiving a bit are separate operations. On every task
each component reads its bit, tests its role field, dispatches and
finally finishes, which gives eight common operations.

- •
  *Immediate exchange.* Without roles, both components send their bits,
  compute the parity and report, in $`18`$ operations.
- •
  *Retained roles.* The sender transmits its bit, and the reporter
  computes and reports the parity, in $`13`$ operations. The saving of
  five consists of one send, delivery and receipt, one parity
  computation and one report.
- •
  *Roles from different bits.* Both components send their bits and
  compare them. Equal bits leave the roles unset, and both report $`0`$,
  in $`18`$ operations. When the bits differ, the component holding
  $`0`$ writes sender and the component holding $`1`$ writes reporter.
  Both dispatch on the new role and only the reporter reports $`1`$, in
  $`21`$ operations.
- •
  *Random proposals.* Each component draws a fair private bit, proposes
  sender or reporter accordingly and sends the proposal with its task
  bit. Equal proposals leave the roles unset, and both report the
  parity. Different proposals are stored, and only the proposed reporter
  reports. Compared with immediate exchange, an attempt adds two draws,
  the send, delivery and receipt of two proposal bits and two
  comparisons. A successful attempt also writes the two roles, and
  dispatching on them costs as much as the second parity computation and
  report that it avoids. An attempt therefore takes $`28`$ operations
  when the proposals are equal and $`30`$ when they differ.

At the start of the horizon each component reads a common two-bit
instruction that selects the plan. It is an external input other than
the task input and adds two operations. All plans start from unset roles
with the same storage, and their external inputs have the same length
apart from the random bits drawn under random proposals. Over $`H`$
tasks, immediate exchange uses $`2+18H`$ operations. If the bits first
differ on task $`k`$, establishing roles from them uses $`13H+5k+5`$, a
saving of $`5(H-k)-3`$, and if they never differ it uses $`2+18H`$.
Under random proposals, task $`t`$ begins without roles with probability
$`2^{-(t-1)}`$, so the expected total work is

|     |                                                                |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       2+13H+16\sum_{t=1}^{H}2^{-(t-1)}=2+13H+32\bigl(1-2^{-H}\bigr).
       ```                                                             |     |

The expected saving $`5H-32(1-2^{-H})`$ is negative for $`H\leq 6`$ and
positive for $`H\geq 7`$. If the proposal operations and role writes are
counted as acquisition work, each task that begins with roles has stage
saving $`5`$ in the notation of
[section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Costs over future tasks ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
and each attempt adds ten operations of acquisition work, with two more
when it succeeds. The total varies between histories. It is
$`13H+15j+4`$ when roles are first established on task $`j`$ and
$`2+28H`$ when no attempt succeeds, so a budget met on every history
must be at least $`28H+4`$
([definition B.23](#A2.Thmtheorem23 "Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

For the organisational family of
[definition B.19](#A2.Thmtheorem19 "Definition B.19 (Matched organisational comparison family). ‣ B.5 Organisational arrangements ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
fix immediate exchange as the procedure used without roles. The states
with complementary and with unset roles are then matched states
differing only in their role fields. Their least work at input bound
zero is $`13`$ and $`18`$ on every task, so $`G_{\rm org}=5`$ and the
retained roles are work-reducing information
([definition B.11](#A2.Thmtheorem11 "Definition B.11 (Work-reducing information). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
Budgets from $`13`$ to $`17`$ admit the organised state but not the
unorganised one. A pooled routine that reads both bits, computes and
reports the parity and finishes uses $`5`$ operations, so
$`T_{\rm coord}=8`$. These counts belong to this instruction table.

Without communication, including shared-state, timing and termination
signals, each component’s behaviour depends only on its own bit and its
own random draws. A report by one component then stays the same when
only the other bit changes, although the parity changes. No fixed
isolated implementation therefore succeeds on all four input pairs.

A hidden bijection $`\psi`$ assigns one of $`n\geq 1`$ roles to each of
$`n`$ workers. A trial asks whether $`\psi(i)=j`$ for a chosen worker
$`i`$ and role $`j`$ and returns a reliable yes or no. A separate worker
action performs a requested role. The public context contains $`n`$ and
a finite description of the function that answers the trials. This
description allows the reference machine to compute the assignment. An
acquisition procedure uses it only through trials, starts without
assignment-specific information and admits only the empty task input. It
may keep every answer and use any deduction from them. For a set $`R`$
of $`m`$ required roles, where $`0\leq m\leq n`$, consider deterministic
procedures that return the correct worker for every role in $`R`$ for
every $`\psi`$. The work rule counts one unit for each trial and each
worker action. Control, inference, encoding and output have zero cost
under this rule.

Once these workers have been obtained, the evaluated system reads its
stored routing table and sends a request to the worker assigned to the
requested role. It admits only the empty task input. Let $`\Gamma_{R}`$
require a correct action in each role in $`R`$ within one unit of work.
A routing table meets $`\Gamma_{R}`$ exactly when it gives the correct
worker for every role in $`R`$. Let $`\tau_{\rm acq}`$ accept those
tables, with the trial description and $`R`$ as its public context. When
$`R`$ contains every role, a qualifying table gives the whole
assignment.

###### Proposition C.16 (Discovering which worker can perform each role).

1.  (i)
    A state retaining a table that is correct on $`R`$ meets every
    requirement in $`\Gamma_{R}`$ in one worker action. Performing one
    action in every role of $`R`$ takes $`m`$ units of work.
2.  (ii)
    Every acquisition procedure in this class uses at least
    $`m(2n-m-1)/2`$ trials for some assignment, which is $`n(n-1)/2`$
    when $`m=n`$. For any fixed such procedure $`(A,a)`$, at that
    assignment its acquisition depth is at least this number at every
    margin. Testing the possible workers for each role of $`R`$ in turn
    attains this worst-case trial count. The reference complexity of a
    qualifying table is bounded by a constant independent of $`n`$,
    $`R`$ and $`\psi`$.
3.  (iii)
    If the assignments of the other workers are retained and $`k`$
    workers are replaced by workers assigned to the $`k`$ freed roles in
    an unknown order, identifying the new assignment has worst-case
    trial count $`k(k-1)/2`$.

###### Proof.

For (i), the table selects the correct worker for any requested role in
$`R`$.

For the lower bound in (ii), consider the bipartite graph of worker–role
pairs not yet excluded, which initially contains all $`n^{2}`$ pairs.
Answer no whenever deleting the queried pair leaves a perfect matching,
and yes otherwise. A yes answer concerns a pair in every remaining
perfect matching, so every perfect matching of the current graph is
consistent with all answers given. The deterministic procedure returns
the same table for all of them. When a correct procedure finishes, they
therefore all give each role in $`R`$ the same worker. Fix one of them,
$`M`$, and two workers $`i`$ and $`i^{\prime}`$ at least one of whose
roles under $`M`$ lies in $`R`$. If both pairs $`(i,M(i^{\prime}))`$ and
$`(i^{\prime},M(i))`$ remained, exchanging the roles of $`i`$ and
$`i^{\prime}`$ in $`M`$ would give another remaining perfect matching
with a different worker for a role in $`R`$. One of the two pairs was
therefore deleted after a trial. A pair $`(i,M(i^{\prime}))`$ determines
$`i`$ and $`i^{\prime}`$, so different choices of the two workers
require different deleted pairs. Their number is

|     |                                                  |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \binom{n}{2}-\binom{n-m}{2}=\frac{m(2n-m-1)}{2},
       ```                                               |     |

and $`M`$ is an assignment consistent with all the answers on which this
many trials were made. For $`m=n`$ the remaining graph has a unique
perfect matching, and the count agrees with the classical bound that
such a bipartite graph has at most $`n(n+1)/2`$ edges
\[[64](#bib.bib106)\]. Each acquisition procedure admits only the empty
input, so its run at $`M`$ gives its least work at every input bound and
hence its depth at every margin.

For the upper bound, take the roles of $`R`$ in turn. When the workers
for $`\ell-1`$ of them are known, at most $`n-\ell+1`$ workers remain
possible for the $`\ell`$th role, and at most $`n-\ell`$ trials identify
its worker, since the last possibility can be inferred. Summing over
$`\ell\leq m`$ gives $`m(2n-m-1)/2`$. A fixed reference program performs
this search using the trial description and $`R`$ in the context and
emits the routing table. This proves the constant description bound.

For (iii), the retained assignments fix $`n-k`$ pairs. The new workers
and freed roles form the same identification problem of size $`k`$ with
all $`k`$ roles required. ∎

### C.5 Evaluating success

These results distinguish the work required to reach a score from
whether success under that score meets the intended target
([section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Evaluating success ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

For the proxy evaluator $`\widehat{\operatorname{perf}}`$ and intended
evaluator $`\operatorname{perf}_{\tau}`$ of
[section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Evaluating success ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
write $`\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}`$ and $`S_{q}^{*}`$ for their accepted
level sets
([definition A.2](#A1.Thmtheorem2 "Definition A.2 (Accepted level sets and binary tasks). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"))
at a proxy level $`\hat{q}`$ and a goal level $`q`$, which need not
agree. Each comparison fixes the system, state and public context unless
stated otherwise.

The inclusion $`\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}\subseteq S_{q}^{*}`$ discussed in
[section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Evaluating success ‣ 3 Evaluating capability ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
has a direct work consequence. At a fixed input bound, restricting the
accepted responses cannot reduce the required work. Depth sets that
input bound from the shortest reference description of an accepted
response, so changing the accepted set can also change the input bound.

###### Proposition C.17 (Bounds for nested accepted sets).

Fix a system and state, a common public context $`x`$, a proxy level
$`\hat{q}`$, a goal level $`q`$ and $`\kappa\in\mathbb{N}`$, and suppose
$`\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}`$ and $`S_{q}^{*}`$ are nonempty. As in
[definition B.15](#A2.Thmtheorem15 "Definition B.15 (Accepted-set shorthand). ‣ B.4 Acquiring capability ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
$`\mathsf{C}(S\mid x)`$ is the length of a shortest reference
description of a response in $`S`$, and $`d^{S}`$ is the depth of the
binary task $`(x,S)`$. If $`\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}\subseteq S_{q}^{*}`$
and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{C}(\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}\mid x)-\mathsf{C}(S_{q}^{*}\mid x)\leq\kappa,
``` |  |

then, for every $`s\in\mathbb{N}`$,

|     |                                                           |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       d^{S_{q}^{*}}(s+\kappa)\leq d^{\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}}(s),
       ```                                                        |     |

so a depth lower bound for $`S_{q}^{*}`$ at margin $`s+\kappa`$
transfers to $`\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}`$ at margin $`s`$. For every input
bound $`\lambda`$, inclusion alone gives
$`w^{\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}}(\lambda)\geq w^{S_{q}^{*}}(\lambda)`$.

###### Proof.

Target inclusion gives
$`w^{\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}}(\lambda)\geq w^{S_{q}^{*}}(\lambda)`$ for
every input bound. Write
$`\widehat{C}=\mathsf{C}(\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}\mid x)`$ and
$`C_{*}=\mathsf{C}(S_{q}^{*}\mid x)`$. Since
$`\widehat{C}+s\leq C_{*}+s+\kappa`$ and least work is nonincreasing in
input bound,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle d^{\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle=w^{\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}}(\widehat{C}+s)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq w^{S_{q}^{*}}(\widehat{C}+s)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq w^{S_{q}^{*}}(C_{*}+s+\kappa)=d^{S_{q}^{*}}(s+\kappa).`$ |  |

∎

Since $`\mathsf{C}(S_{q}^{*}\mid x)\geq 0`$, the length of any known
reference description of a member of $`\widehat{S}_{\hat{q}}`$ is an
admissible $`\kappa`$.

[Proposition C.17](#A3.Thmtheorem17 "Proposition C.17 (Bounds for nested accepted sets). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
relates success under two evaluators. We now ask whether successive
levels of one evaluator can require increasing work. For a fixed system,
starting state and margin, the next construction accepts at each level
only responses that the system cannot return within that level’s input
and work bounds. The accepted responses nevertheless have short
reference descriptions, as in the classical existence of deep strings
\[[9](#bib.bib3)\]. When the system can execute reference programs with
a fixed input overhead and the margin covers that overhead, the
constructed levels have finite depth, as shown after
[proposition C.19](#A3.Thmtheorem19 "Proposition C.19 (A demanding evaluator for a family of systems). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp").

###### Theorem C.18 (A demanding evaluator for one system).

Fix a system–state pair $`(g,r)`$ and public context $`x`$, and suppose
bounded-work runs of $`g`$ from $`r`$ are effectively exhaustible,
meaning that an algorithm terminates with a complete list of halting
runs within given bounds on input length and work
([definition A.4](#A1.Thmtheorem4 "Definition A.4 (Effective exhaustibility). ‣ Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
Fix $`s\in\mathbb{N}`$ and a computable strictly increasing sequence
$`T_{1}<T_{2}<\cdots`$. There is a finite-description graded task
$`\tau_{\mathrm{dem}}`$ with public context $`x`$, performance set
$`\mathbb{N}`$ in its usual order, and a constant $`K`$ such that, for
every $`n\geq 1`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
d_{g,r}^{\tau_{\mathrm{dem}},n}(s)>T_{n},\qquad\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\mathrm{dem}}}(n\mid x)\leq K+\ell(n).
``` |  |

Here $`\ell(n)=O(\log(n+2))`$ is the length of the self-delimiting code
of $`n`$.

###### Proof.

The input bound used for depth depends on the length of a shortest
reference description of an accepted response, so we first bound that
length by the length of the construction’s own description. Fix a finite
construction algorithm with an integer parameter $`D`$. If its fixed
part, which includes the listing algorithm, the sequence $`(T_{n})`$ and
$`s`$, has description length $`c`$, choose $`D`$ so large that
$`c+\ell(D)\leq D`$, freeze this value and set $`K=D`$. Such a choice
exists by the logarithmic integer-code convention in
[appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"). Put

|     |                    |     |
|-----|--------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       L_{n}=K+\ell(n)+s.
       ```                 |     |

Effective bounded-work exhaustibility makes the finite set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
B_{n}=\{g(r;\langle x,p\rangle):|p|\leq L_{n},\text{ the run halts within work }T_{n}\}
``` |  |

computable.

A response of level $`n`$ is also accepted at every lower level, so
choose $`z_{n}`$ as the first string in length-lexicographic order for
which $`y_{n}=\langle n,z_{n}\rangle`$ is outside
$`B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{n}`$. The excluded set is finite, so this
effective search terminates. The evaluator assigns level $`m`$ exactly
to $`y_{m}`$, and zero to every other response. It first checks for a
canonical tuple encoding with integer leading field $`m\geq 1`$,
returning zero otherwise. On a valid encoding it computes $`y_{m}`$ for
the leading field $`m`$ and checks equality. Thus it is total,
computable and finitely described. The same fixed construction, with
frozen parameter $`K`$ and the self-delimiting code of $`n`$, outputs
$`y_{n}`$, proving

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\mathrm{dem}}}(n\mid x)\leq K+\ell(n).
       ```                                                       |     |

Fix $`n`$. Every response of performance at least $`n`$ is some
$`y_{m}`$ with $`m\geq n`$, and each such response was chosen outside
$`B_{n}`$. Thus no task input of length at most $`L_{n}`$ reaches level
$`n`$ within work $`T_{n}`$. The input bound
$`\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\mathrm{dem}}}(n\mid x)+s`$ in the definition of
depth is at most $`K+\ell(n)+s=L_{n}`$, hence
$`d_{g,r}^{\tau_{\mathrm{dem}},n}(s)>T_{n}`$. ∎

The preceding construction fixes one system–state pair and one margin.
We can extend the construction to an effectively enumerated family of
pairs and every margin, using a single evaluator. For each fixed pair
and margin, its growing work lower bounds hold from a starting level
that depends on that pair and margin.

###### Proposition C.19 (A demanding evaluator for a family of systems).

Fix an effective enumeration $`(g_{1},r_{1}),(g_{2},r_{2}),\ldots`$ of
finite-description system–state pairs with uniformly effectively
exhaustible bounded-work runs, a public context $`x`$, and a computable
strictly increasing unbounded schedule $`(T_{n})`$. There is one
finite-description graded task $`\tau_{\rm pub}`$ with public context
$`x`$ and performance set $`\mathbb{N}`$ in its usual order, and a
constant $`K`$ such that for every pair $`(g_{i},r_{i})`$, every margin
$`s`$, and every $`n\geq\max\{i,s,1\}`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
d_{g_{i},r_{i}}^{\tau_{\rm pub},n}(s)>T_{n},\qquad\mathsf{C}_{\tau_{\rm pub}}(n\mid x)\leq K+\ell(n).
``` |  |

Hence, for every fixed pair, margin and work threshold, every
sufficiently high level has depth above that threshold.

###### Proof.

The construction follows the proof of
[theorem C.18](#A3.Thmtheorem18 "Theorem C.18 (A demanding evaluator for one system). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
with two changes. At stage $`n`$ it considers the first $`n`$ pairs, and
its input bound covers the one used for depth at every margin up to
$`n`$, so each fixed pair and margin is covered from some stage onwards.
Choose $`K`$ as in that proof, for a construction whose fixed part
includes the uniform listing algorithm and $`(T_{n})`$ but no margin.
Put $`L_{n}=K+\ell(n)+n`$. Uniform exhaustibility gives a finite
computable set $`B_{n}`$ containing every response obtainable by any of
the first $`n`$ pairs using task inputs of length at most $`L_{n}`$ and
work at most $`T_{n}`$. Choose $`z_{n}`$ as the first string in
length-lexicographic order for which $`y_{n}=\langle n,z_{n}\rangle`$ is
outside $`B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{n}`$. The finite exclusion makes this
search terminate.

Define the evaluator from these responses as in the proof of
[theorem C.18](#A3.Thmtheorem18 "Theorem C.18 (A demanding evaluator for one system). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
assigning level $`m`$ exactly to $`y_{m}`$ and zero otherwise. It is
total and finitely described. The same fixed construction outputs
$`y_{n}`$ from the self-delimiting code of $`n`$, giving the stated
complexity bound.

Now fix $`i,s`$ and $`n\geq\max\{i,s,1\}`$. As in the previous proof,
the input bound in the definition of depth is at most
$`K+\ell(n)+s\leq L_{n}`$. Every response of level at least $`n`$ is
some $`y_{m}`$ with $`m\geq n`$, and every such response was chosen
outside $`B_{n}`$. Since $`i\leq n`$, the outputs of pair $`i`$ within
input length $`L_{n}`$ and work $`T_{n}`$ are contained in $`B_{n}`$.
Hence level $`n`$ cannot be attained within work $`T_{n}`$ at that input
bound, proving the depth inequality. The unboundedness of $`(T_{n})`$
gives the final claim about sufficiently high levels. ∎

Under the following interface condition, the depths bounded in
[theorems C.18](#A3.Thmtheorem18 "Theorem C.18 (A demanding evaluator for one system). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [C.19](#A3.Thmtheorem19 "Proposition C.19 (A demanding evaluator for a family of systems). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
are finite whenever the margin covers a fixed input overhead. Suppose a
pair $`(g,r)`$ admits, for every reference program $`a`$, an input
$`p_{a}`$ of length at most $`|a|+c`$, for a fixed $`c\in\mathbb{N}`$,
and returns $`U_{0}(a,x)`$ in finite counted work whenever that
reference computation halts. At any level $`q`$ with nonempty accepted
set $`S_{q}^{\tau}`$, choose a shortest successful reference program. If
$`s\geq c`$, its translated input fits the bound
$`\mathsf{C}_{\tau}(q\mid x)+s`$, so $`d_{g,r}^{\tau,q}(s)<\infty`$. The
condition holds with $`c=0`$ when $`g`$ runs its task input as a program
on $`U_{0}`$ and work counts the steps, as in the recovery of logical
depth in
[section 2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Depth and grasp ‣ 2 The formal framework ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
Bounded-work runs are then also effectively exhaustible. This applies to
the pair in
[theorem C.18](#A3.Thmtheorem18 "Theorem C.18 (A demanding evaluator for one system). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
when it has this property, and to any such pair in
[proposition C.19](#A3.Thmtheorem19 "Proposition C.19 (A demanding evaluator for a family of systems). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
For those pairs and margins, the displayed lower bounds concern finite
depths. When $`(T_{n})`$ is unbounded, as in
[proposition C.19](#A3.Thmtheorem19 "Proposition C.19 (A demanding evaluator for a family of systems). ‣ C.5 Evaluating success ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
these finite depths tend to infinity with the required level.

These constructions give work lower bounds that grow with the required
score. Cumulative deepening, as defined in
[section 5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Cumulative deepening ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
additionally requires a history in which earlier achievements reduce the
work of later ones and the later targets have finite, increasing depths
from the original starting condition.
[Example C.9](#A3.Thmtheorem9 "Example C.9 (Computing successive prefix parities). ‣ C.2.2 Cumulative deepening ‣ C.2 Reducing work through retained information ‣ Appendix C Statements and proofs ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
gives such a history for every chosen finite horizon.

### C.6 Recursive improvement

We ask whether a state with greater capability also reduces the work
needed for further improvement
([section 5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Recursive improvement ‣ 5 Improvement over time ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).
In the example below, each round before the last builds part of a search
index that reduces the work of the next round. The proposition that
follows bounds the work needed for one fixed program to execute changing
improvement procedures stored as code.

###### Example C.20 (Constructing a search index).

A search can organise data in a way that helps later searches. Suppose a
catalogue lists every four-bit word once in an arbitrary order and a
procedure must find the position of $`0000`$. It divides the entries by
their first bit, divides the group beginning with $`0`$ by the second
bit and continues in this way until $`0000`$ is isolated, keeping both
groups at every division. A later search for $`1000`$ can start from the
group beginning with $`1`$, which the first search set aside. Building a
search structure only as far as queries require is known as deferred
data structuring \[[49](#bib.bib107)\]. Database cracking similarly
reorganises stored data as a byproduct of query processing
\[[46](#bib.bib108)\].

Fix $`d\geq 3`$ and $`N=2^{d}`$. The public context holds a catalogue
listing every $`d`$-bit word once in an arbitrary order, with each bit
stored separately. An entry’s identifier is its position. A valid
retained state consists of a fixed-width binary prefix tree, a request
counter $`k\in\{0,\ldots,d\}`$ and $`d`$ answer slots, of which the
first $`k`$ hold the identifiers found for the first $`k`$ requests. The
active leaves of the tree hold linked lists of identifiers, and each
lists exactly the identifiers whose words begin with its prefix. An
internal node records a division already made, and nodes below a leaf
are inactive. The search follows the bits of the requested word through
internal nodes. At a leaf it reads the next bit of every listed entry,
divides the list into two, keeps both and continues until the word is
isolated. Work counts the executed instructions of a direct
random-access-machine implementation, one for each read, write,
arithmetic operation, register assignment or branch. Dividing a group of
$`m`$ entries takes eight instructions for each entry and nine to
prepare and mark the division, $`8m+9`$ in all.

For a domain task, the same search is run from a retained state $`r`$
with only the empty task input and returns the identifier of one given
word. When every group on the word’s path is already divided it takes
$`8d+5`$ instructions, and each missing division adds its own $`8m+9`$.
Let $`\mathcal{L}(r)`$ be the set of words that the search locates from
$`r`$ within work $`8d+5`$, and $`Q(r)=|\mathcal{L}(r)|`$. Dividing an
active group of two entries isolates two words and raises $`Q`$ by two,
while dividing a larger group isolates none.

The improvement process reads its requests from the public context. From
a state with counter $`t-1<d`$ it searches for the word
$`1^{t-1}0^{d-t+1}`$, writes its identifier in answer slot $`t`$ and
increments the counter, and from counter $`d`$ it fails. The requests
are chosen so that each search after the first begins its divisions in
the group that the previous search set aside. Let $`r_{0}`$ hold one
undivided group and counter $`0`$, and let round $`t`$ begin at
$`r_{t-1}`$ and produce $`r_{t}`$. The wrapper $`g_{\Delta}`$ of
[definition B.20](#A2.Thmtheorem20 "Definition B.20 (Improvement evaluated against the producer). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
emits $`\langle r,r^{\prime},\varepsilon\rangle`$ with empty procedure
data. Let $`\tau^{\Delta}`$ be the binary task that accepts exactly when
$`r`$ and $`r^{\prime}`$ are valid, the counter of $`r^{\prime}`$
exceeds that of $`r`$ by one, $`r^{\prime}`$ keeps the answers of $`r`$,
$`\mathcal{L}(r)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(r^{\prime})`$ and
$`Q(r^{\prime})=Q(r)+2`$. Only the empty task input is admitted. Round
$`t`$ divides the groups with prefixes
$`1^{t-1},1^{t-1}0,\ldots,1^{t-1}0^{d-t}`$, of sizes
$`N/2^{t-1},\ldots,4,2`$. Each round is accepted and gives
$`Q(r_{t})=2t`$. Each division reduces the work of the domain tasks
whose words lie in the divided group and leaves the others unchanged.
Each $`r_{t}`$ therefore weakly grasp-dominates $`r_{t-1}`$ on these
tasks at every finite work budget
\[[corollary B.13](#A2.Thmtheorem13 "Corollary B.13 (Least-work comparisons imply grasp dominance). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")\].

Besides its divisions, a round takes $`8d+11`$ instructions for
following the requested word through the $`d`$ levels and for
bookkeeping. Emitting the producer and the successor as two fixed-width
arrays takes $`8R`$ more, where the $`R=3N+d`$ words of a state hold its
$`2N-1`$ tree nodes, $`N`$ list links, $`d`$ answer slots and counter.
Building the undivided list of $`r_{0}`$ takes $`10N+1`$ instructions
before the first round. Since the process is deterministic, its least
work for the improvement from $`r_{t-1}`$ is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
W_{t}=w_{g_{\Delta},r_{t-1}}^{\tau^{\Delta},1}(0)=8R+8d+11+\sum_{i=1}^{d-t+1}\bigl(8\cdot 2^{i}+9\bigr),\qquad W_{t}-W_{t+1}=\frac{8N}{2^{t-1}}+9>0\quad\text{for }1\leq t<d.
``` |  |

In the four-bit case the rounds read $`30`$, $`14`$, $`6`$ and $`2`$
catalogue bits, raise $`Q`$ to $`2`$, $`4`$, $`6`$ and $`8`$ and take
$`735`$, $`598`$, $`525`$ and $`484`$ instructions, so the reductions
are $`137`$, $`73`$ and $`41`$.

For $`1\leq t<d`$, replace the components that round $`t`$ changes in
$`r_{t-1}`$, namely its new divisions, the counter and answer slot
$`t`$, by their values in $`r_{t}`$. This gives the valid state
$`r_{t}`$, from which the same process with only the empty task input
runs round $`t+1`$ with work $`W_{t+1}\neq W_{t}`$. The observation rule
of
[definition B.9](#A2.Thmtheorem9 "Definition B.9 (Reuse of earlier work). ‣ B.3 Persistent information ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
for finite constructions records work, so the producing run depends on
these components, which is the self-contribution condition of
[definition B.21](#A2.Thmtheorem21 "Definition B.21 (Positive recursive gain). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp").
Rounds $`1`$ to $`d-1`$ therefore show positive recursive gain in the
sense of
[definition B.21](#A2.Thmtheorem21 "Definition B.21 (Positive recursive gain). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
and $`r_{0}\mapsto r_{1}\mapsto\cdots\mapsto r_{d-1}`$ is a finite
instance of recursive improvement.

To find the source of each reduction, take $`1\leq t<d`$ and replace the
division of the group $`1^{t-1}`$ in $`r_{t}`$, the first made in round
$`t`$, by an undivided list of that group. The counter, the answers and
the other fields are unchanged and the nodes below become inactive. The
next round must make this division again, so its work rises from
$`W_{t+1}`$ to $`W_{t}`$ while it still meets the target relative to the
altered producer. The whole reduction thus comes from the index built in
round $`t`$. A comparison of building the index during searches with
building it in advance must count the work of both complete plans
````
