````math
([definitions B.23](#A2.Thmtheorem23 "Definition B.23 (Feasible continuation plans). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")
and [B.24](#A2.Thmtheorem24 "Remark B.24 (Costs of retaining a component). ‣ B.7 Costs over future tasks ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp")).

Recursive improvement is assessed under one fixed outer system. An
improvement procedure that changes from one stage to the next can be
represented in such a system by storing it as code and executing it with
a fixed interpreter. The proposition below adapts the standard
universal-machine simulation \[[7](#bib.bib109)\] to bound the
interpreter’s work when it reproduces a finite improvement history. The
interpreter’s own work can change which procedure uses less work. Any
claimed saving must therefore be checked with both procedures executed
by the same interpreter.

Fix in advance a system boundary, staged-input and exogenous-input
interfaces and finite checkpoint and successor encodings. Also fix a
deterministic small-step representation in which each admissible local
process is a finite transition table. Here staged input is the task
input delivered to the procedure at each stage, while other external
inputs use the exogenous-input interface. Transition tables and state
encodings have syntax that can be checked in polynomial time.
Admissibility requires a finite represented history and well-formed
successor outputs. At stage $`t`$, the successor output encodes the next
transition table $`F_{t+1}`$ and state $`r_{t+1}`$, so a procedure can
replace its own code. Each small step changes only a bounded number of
symbols, apart from literal strings explicitly contained in its
transition table. Count every outer transition as one work unit.

###### Proposition C.21 (A fixed interpreter for changing improvement procedures).

For checkpoints $`c_{t}=\langle F_{t},r_{t}\rangle`$, pairing the
transition table $`F_{t}`$ of the stage-$`t`$ procedure with its state
$`r_{t}`$, there is one finite-description deterministic
transition-complete interpreter $`g_{\rm int}`$ which reproduces every
finite admissible history

|     |                                                   |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       (F_{0},r_{0})\leadsto\cdots\leadsto(F_{T},r_{T}).
       ```                                                |     |

At each stage it simulates $`F_{t}`$, emits the same well-formed
successor output as $`F_{t}`$ and retains
$`c_{t+1}=\langle F_{t+1},r_{t+1}\rangle`$. Stage work is bounded by one
fixed polynomial in the lengths of the transition table, current state,
staged input, exogenous input and emitted output, and in the number of
local transitions.

###### Proof.

Construct $`g_{\rm int}`$ as the universal interpreter for the fixed
small-step representation, using the efficient transition-table and
configuration encodings of
[appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A The model ‣ From Grind to Grasp"). At each
stage it decodes the checkpoint and checks the transition table and
state syntactically. It initialises the simulated configuration and
delivers staged and exogenous inputs only through the original interface
at their authorised steps. Describing a realised input sequence for the
comparison does not make its future values available early to the local
process. For each local transition it scans the finite table, updates
the encoded configuration and copies any emitted symbol. A well-formed
local halt emits the same successor description and retains the decoded
next checkpoint. Malformed successor syntax produces a declared failure.

If the local computation uses $`m`$ transitions and its transition table
has length $`L_{F}`$, configuration length is at most a constant
multiple of the sum of $`1+m(L_{F}+1)`$ and the initial-state,
staged-input and exogenous-input lengths. The factor $`L_{F}+1`$ covers
any explicitly stored table literal written by one local step. A table
scan and encoded update cost at most a fixed polynomial in these lengths
and $`m`$ per step. Summing over the $`m`$ steps and adding decoding,
syntactic checks, interface handling and output copying gives one
polynomial bound in the stated quantities. The interpreter itself is
fixed. Induction over stages then gives equality of the emitted
successor and retained checkpoint with the represented admissible
history. ∎

For the producer-relative evaluation of
[definition B.20](#A2.Thmtheorem20 "Definition B.20 (Improvement evaluated against the producer). ‣ B.6 Recursive improvement ‣ Appendix B Definitions and basic results ‣ From Grind to Grasp"),
let $`g_{\Delta}`$ consist of $`g_{\rm int}`$ and a fixed wrapper. The
producer state of a stage is then the checkpoint $`c_{t}`$, including
the current code $`F_{t}`$. The wrapper copies $`c_{t}`$ from the actual
starting state and emits $`\langle c_{t},c_{t+1},\sigma_{t}\rangle`$,
where $`\sigma_{t}`$ is any procedure data in the local output. This
copying takes work polynomial in the stated lengths, so the stage work
of $`g_{\Delta}`$ is bounded by one fixed polynomial in the same
quantities.
````
